The Vietnam Archive 
Oral History Project 
Interview with John McRainey 
Conducted by Steve Maxner 
June 15, 2001 
Transcribed by Christina Witt 
 
NOTE: Text included in brackets [ ] is information that was added by the narrator after reviewing 
the original transcript.  Therefore, this information is not included in the audio version of the 
interview.

SM:  This is Steve Maxner conducting an interview with Mr. John McRainey on 
the fifteenth of June 2001 at approximately ten thirty.  I am in Lubbock Texas and Mr. 
McRainey is in McAllen Texas.  Sir I’d like to begin our interview with a brief 
discussion of your early life and if you would go ahead and tell us where you were born 
and when you were born. 
JM:  Alright.  I was born in Fayetteville, North Carolina May 20, 1935.   
SM:  Now were you living there in a civilian capacity, was your family attached 
to the military? 
JM:  No.  My father at that time was working for my grandfather.  My grandfather 
had a machine shop called the McRainey Iron Works in Fayetteville North Carolina. 
SM:  Okay.  Is that still in existence? 
JM:  Oh no.  No my grandfather passed away many years ago and he sold the 
business probably fifteen years before he died. 
SM:  Okay.  How long did you live in that part of the country? 
JM:  My father got a civil service job with the department of the Navy in 1940 
and we moved to Washington D.C. and my dad was working in the Naval yard, 
Washington Naval yard.  He started out as a welder, but he was pretty knowledgeable 
you now street smart young guy at the time I guess.  They had him doing a lot of special 
work on that first bathysphere you know that dining bell.  They sent him to a school on 
  1

31 
metallurgy and he eventually wound up as inspector of ordinance and transferred out of 
Washington.   
SM:  How much do you remember about living in Washington during the forties? 
JM:  Well you know I was very young, I remember a little about it.  It was an 
apartment building, we were there before the war and I think yeah we were still there 
when the war started.  So I can remember all of the hubbub of the Japanese attacking 
Pearl Harbor I was in Washington when that happened.   
SM:  Do you remember at all listening to radio programs? 
JM: I remember the family listening to radio programs yeah.  Everybody was 
quite intense about it in those days.  My dad I guess had mixed emotions.  He was then at 
that time even his job was priority where they wanted him…he was in a job that they felt 
that he was more important there then he was serving in the military and I think dad 
would have preferred to have gone in the military at that time, but he was frozen for 
defense reasons in the job that he was in.   
SM:  In an essential position. 
JM:  Yes. 
SM:  How much do you remember about the Second World War and especially I 
guess as you got older and the war ended I would imagine you probably remember VE-
Day, VJ-Day. 
JM:  Oh yes.  I can’t tell you where I was or what I was doing in those days, but I 
can remember.  We were in Minneapolis while most of the war was going on.  My father 
was transferred to Northern Ordnance was the name of it and they manufactured big 
naval guns and my father was sent up there as an ordinance inspector, a naval inspector.  
He worked there throughout the war in fact he worked there until he retired.  He stayed in 
Minneapolis almost his entire life.   
SM:  Do you remember when you met your wife, the first time you met her? 
JM:  Oh yes! 
SM:  You met her in Minneapolis… 
JM:  I met her in Minneapolis in high school and we started dating.  I guess we 
dated for two or three years through high school and right at the end I had made up my 
mind I was going to go into the service.  I was really caught up in the conflict that the

French were having in Dien Bien Phu and of course we were involved in the Korean 
War.  I just felt that I was missing something by missing the Korean War.  I enlisted as 
soon as I was out of school, but I missed any action in the Korean War I wound up going 
to Germany. 
SM:  When did you graduate, 1952? 
JM:  1954. 
SM:  ’54.  As you were in high school and of course there’s quite a bit going on 
around the world with regard for the Cold War.  Was this a topic of conversation very 
much? 
JM:  Well, yes it was.  My father of course you know working for the government 
was keenly interested in it and by that time you know we were talking…of course 
everyone I think was concerned with the possibility of nuclear war.  The Naval ordinance 
that changed dad I think at that time they were working on missile launching tubes for 
trident submarines, which was the big thing.  The Cold War was on everybody’s mind I 
believe at that time. 
SM:  Oh yeah.  Do you recall any of the domestic turmoil that was occurring at 
the time?  The McCarthy hearing, Atoms Spies. 
JM:  Oh yeah.  Yeah and I think even at that tender age I wasn’t caught up in the 
McCarthy hearings I thought he was a turkey from the very beginning. (laughing) 
SM:  (laughing)  Okay.  Well I’m curious also in terms of just your family 
interaction, did you have much discussion about the great depression?  What things were 
like before the war and how did that affect you? 
JM:  Well it…it didn ’t affect my immediate family too much because my 
grandfather was a real entrepreneur he always worked for himself, I don’t think my 
grandfather ever worked for anyone else and he could find an opportunity in almost every 
disaster.  He managed to come through the depression in fact I think he became very 
wealthy during the depression. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  My grandfather specialized during the depression in building water towers 
for communities that needed water.  He would weld through his machine shop and his 
  3

portable welding machine and everything he’d go out on the sites and literally build them 
a water tower in the middle of nowhere. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  So he stayed busy and he prospered and of course my dad worked for him so 
my dad came through it pretty good too. 
SM:  As you were nearing graduation and I guess even in the couple of years 
before you graduated from high school, how much concern was there about the draft?  
How much concern was there about going to Korea? 
JM:  Well you know as I recall it all the people that I lived with, my friends were 
anxious to go.  There was nobody there that was looking for deferments or trying to get 
away from it.  We were blue collar worker types that…we felt it was duty and I think 
almost all of my friends felt that there was no question about the fact that they were going 
to serve in the military and that they all wanted to get it out of the way sooner rather then 
later.  Many of my friends and myself went in about the same time, we all went in 
different branches of the service, we all had our ideas of what we wanted to do.  Even the 
ones that intended to go on to college wanted to get the military service out of the way 
first. 
SM:  Now did you know anyone say in your neighborhood for instance who 
served in Korea and came back? 
JM:  Well yes my girlfriend at the time.  Sandy’s sister dated a young man that 
well she married when he finally came back, but he was in the Korean War.  His name 
was Robert Myers, Bob Myers.   
SM:  Did you ever have opportunity to talk with him about it? 
JM:  Oh yes, yes many, many times. 
SM:  Now was that before you graduated from high school or after that you got to 
talking? 
JM:  No actually it was after. 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  It was after. 
  4

SM:  Okay.  How about relatives of yours who served either in World War II or in 
Korea and maybe that you were able to talk to before graduating from high school to 
learn about what it was like to be in a war? 
JM:  Well my uncle, my dad’s brother served in the Air Force and he…let m e 
think what outfit he was in, I can’t remember exactly.  I know that he was in a B-17 
group and they flew out of England during World War II.  His name was Brown 
McRainey.  First Brown was the type that you know it was all an adventure to him, he 
never, he wasn’t very emotional about the broader aspects of the war to him it was just an 
adventure. 
SM:  So he did talk to you some about it as you were in high school? 
JM:  Oh sure, sure.  Yeah I was very young at the time that I’d talked to Brown 
about the war, but he…like I say he thought it wa s a big adventure.  A hell of a thing to 
say anybody would enjoy a war, but I think Brown actually enjoyed going to war. 
SM:  (laughing) Okay.  Did he fly? 
JM:  No, no.  He was a crewmember.   
SM:  Okay.  When did you become interested in flying? 
JM:  I became interested in flying in the service when… I’d m ade up my mind, 
I’m trying to think of the actor’s name now he was a short guy they always put stilted 
shows on him, but he was quite an actor.  Well he played the Great Gatsby, do you know 
how that was? 
SM:  Oh, um… 
JM:  Alan Ladd. 
SM:  Yeah, yeah Alan Ladd. 
JM:  Alan Ladd had made a movie while I was in high school called Paratrooper 
and geEASY that made me decide I had to be in the airborne.  I wanted to be a 
paratrooper.  So that’s what I enlisted in and while I was in it of course after having made 
about fifty jumps over a period of three years I decided it’d be nice to land in an airplane.  
(laughing) 
SM:  (laughing)  So that was part of the motivation for you going into the Army 
versus the other services? 
  5

JM:  Well yes, oh yes absolutely.  The airborne thing…that adventure is what I 
wanted more then anything in the world at that time.   
SM:  Okay.  Well how did your father and mother respond to you going straight 
into the military? 
JM:  Oh they thought I was crazy, both of them.   
SM:  (laughing) Okay.  And how about Sandy? 
JM:  No I don’t think… Sandy thought it would be nice to get your military 
obligation behind  you and then settle down and then do whatever you’re going to do.   
SM:  So when you graduated from high school you went straight to the recruiter 
and signed up? 
JM:  Yes and went over almost immediately.  I went through the basic infantry 
training first of course and that was at Fort Leonard Wood Missouri and then I went to 
advanced infantry basic in Fort Dix New Jersey.  Then after that I went to the airborne 
school at Fort Campbell, Kentucky.   
SM:  Oh Campbell, okay.  And where were you assigned after that? 
JM:  After that I was assigned to the 511th airborne infantry regiment of the 11th 
airborne division.  It was the regular old grunts you know, the light weapons infantry.   
SM:  Yes.  What was basic training like for you? 
JM:  Well the infantry training was standard Army training.  I think back in those 
days they trained pretty hard.  I think I enjoyed it; as I recall I did!   
SM:  Was…  
JM:  The airborne training was very challenging and again I enjoyed that.  I 
wanted to be there and I knew more or less what to expect from talking to other people 
and I enjoyed it. 
SM:  When you went through basic training was…there had been quite a few 
changes over the last fifty years. 
JM:  Oh yeah. 
SM:  In terms of the training regimen and the way that instructors can interact 
with trainees. 
JM:  That’s right. 
  6

SM:  And of course it’s pretty common knowledge that there used to be a little bit 
more physical contact between… 
JM:  Yes there was.  A sergeant never gave you an order he couldn’t physically 
back up! (laughing) 
SM:  Okay, okay.  Were there incidents like that in your basic training platoon? 
JM:  No there were some people that rebelled and of course the cadre, the NCOs 
that were leading the training responded. 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  It was a no nonsense operation. 
SM:  How about blanket parties, G.I. showers, that kind of stuff? 
JM:  Well, no I don’t think we had much of that.  We were…our discipline was 
such that we trained hard from very early in the morning until very late in the evening 
and then we were given things like you’d been out trenching through the mud all day and 
your boots had to be spit-shined and ready to go for inspection first thing in the morning.  
So there wasn’t much time for nonsense during, particularly the airborne phase of the 
training.  It was 100% focused to the task at hand and there wasn’t any extracurricular 
activities going on. 
SM:  How about your advanced infantry training? 
JM:  Oh yeah there were some games played in that, but nothing really 
significant, nothing more then boys will be boys type stuff. 
SM:  Well in terms of the training, what was the focus in advanced infantry 
training versus your basic infantry training? 
JM:  Well it was actually just refining more of the same and bringing the 
tolerances up a little higher.  Of course they tried to give you a cross training in all the 
weapons you could possibly come in contact with so if you were a light weapons 
infantrymen you got some training on every single weapon they had in the arsenal as well 
you actually went out in the field and fired every single weapon in the arsenal.  So you 
fired everything from a M-1 rifle up, you know, the BAR, the 30 caliber machine gun 
both the light weight and the heavy, water cooled, and the mortars which would be the 60 
millimeter and the 81 millimeter mortar, and the 3.5 rocket launcher, and the 57 
millimeter rocket launcher, and the 45 caliber handgun, the M-2 carbine.  Everything that 
  7

you might come across you were supposed to be experienced with.  And when I say come 
across it might not be the weapon that was assigned to you, but in a combat situation you 
might come across one and that’s what you had to use.   
SM:  In terms of the training in addition to weapons training, how much emphasis 
was placed on patrolling?  Patrolling techniques, setting up ambushes, responding to 
ambush? 
JM:  Oh a lot, quite a bit.  We actually had actual field trials then.  Of course 
when… after we got… you know the line com panies train constantly.  As an airborne 
company when I was assigned to the 511th we spent I’d say fifty percent of our time in 
the field and even when we were in garrison we were training everyday, you weren’t just 
sitting around in the barracks passing the time of day.  We really got refined in ambushes 
and patrolling and tactics once we were assigned to an infantry company and that 
company participated as a whole.  They trained everyday and then they trained at 
battalion level and they trained at regiment level and they trained at division level.  We 
would have an operation on everyone at least once every few months.  I mean you know 
we’d have a division level exercise at least once a year. 
SM:  In the training aspect of basic training and advanced infantry training, did 
you ever conduct live fire exercise?  That’s not necessarily range time you know learning 
to fire the weapons, but actually going out and conducting a patrol where the end result is 
you end up on a live fire range. 
JM:  No I don’t believe we did. 
SM:  Okay.  How about night patrol, things like that? 
JM:  Oh yes we did that, particularly on maneuvers of various types.  I remember 
we went to Camp Stewart Georgia during an exercise and we were taken to I believe it 
was the Okeefenokee Swamps and we did some night patrols and forest night marches 
through the swamps at night.  I’ll never forget those days.  In fact I don’t know if you’re 
interested in humorous incidents and those things, but… 
SM:  Oh yeah, yeah, absolutely! 
JM:  We were on a night forest march with you know full equipment, our packs, 
and everything and we were slogging in water a lot of times almost up to your waist most 
of the time knee deep.  There was a fellow behind me and… I rem ember I could never

understand how he got in the airborne because he was overweight and they were pretty 
hard on people to stay in shape.  I remember he was from New York and he was scared to 
death of snakes and earlier in the evening before it got dark we saw a couple of snakes, 
water moccassins and oh god he was panicked to be stomping through the swamp all 
night with all those snakes around.  We were going through some pretty heavy brush and 
we were single file and branches would hit and flap against you and what have you and 
we were about knee deep in water.  I being a country boy from North Carolina I thought 
it was appropriate to have a plug of tobacco in my mouth and I had a plug of tobacco in 
my jaw.  I step into a hole and it’s like stepping off of a curb stiff legged when you don’t 
know it’s there, it was quite a jolt and I swallowed my plug!  And oh my god it was… I 
thought I was dying.  It was a terrible experience and I’m gasping for breath.  Well the 
same time I stepped in that whole a big branch flapped back against my leg from the man 
in front of me and this fellow behind us saw it and thought it was a snake and at that time 
I went down to my knees and into the water.  I went completely into the water.  This kid 
from New York started screaming at the top the lungs, “Snake bite!  Snake bite!  Medic! 
Medic!”  (laughing)  And I couldn’t talk and of course the whole company stopped and 
the company commander came running back and he was a big black gentleman named 
Ernest V. Martin, really one of the I think the first commanders of a white unit probably 
in the Army, real early anyway in it to be a full captain.  He came back and I can’t talk 
I’m gasping, but I started throwing up at that time.  So now they were convinced that I 
had a bad problem. (laughing) 
SM:  (laughing)  Yeah. 
JM:  So they got the medic back and they cut my pants leg open and I was afraid 
they’re going to start cutting me with that knife and I’m trying to stop them.  Finally I 
guess that figured out that had swallowed my tobacco. (laughing) You could hear 
expletives deleted going up the ranks. (laughing) 
SM:  I would imagine. 
JM:  Thank god the captain was madder at the fellow from New York that… 
Aggot was telling how big the snake was and what it was. (laughing)  
SM:  (laughing)  Oh no.  So after that were there rules against having plugs on 
patrol? (laughing) 
  9

JM:  (laughing) No he never said anything about it, but I never put another plug of 
tobacco in my life that was the end of my tobacco chewing. 
SM:  Really! 
JM:  Oh absolutely the end! 
SM:  Okay, wow!  How about other humorous incidences at basic training or at 
any of your advance course? 
JM:  Well I…we…I cant rem ember any in basic training or the advanced course, 
once we got into a line company of course there was always something going on like that. 
SM:  How about airborne training, anything funny happen there? 
JM:  Oh gosh.  Airborne training was pretty intense in those days.  We had a 
number of people, actually they always take more in, they want people to quit that’s the 
idea.  So they’ll put well say, I don’t remember how big the classes were they were pretty 
large, but the idea was a significant percentage were going to drop out and they kept 
asking… And you could quit that was the thing about he airborne in those days.  You 
could quit anytime you wanted, but then you just went into a regular infantry company.  
So if you didn’t have the desire to be there and do the work you were out.  And of course 
if you didn’t measure up, if you couldn’t meet the standards they’d wash you out.  But 
there were a lot of people that quit when the training got rough, they just you now throw 
up they’re hands and say, “I’ve had it!”  Fine they’re gone.   
SM:  Now in airborne that was… What tim e of year was that?  Was that still ’54 
or was that ’55? 
JM:  Pardon. 
SM:  What year was that? 
JM:  That would have been 1954 that I went through all the training. 
SM:  Okay.  Do you remember what time of year you went to jump school? 
JM:  Well let’s see.  The jump school was in the fall of the year I think because I 
went through the summer months… let’s see, tw o months of basic and went in in June, so 
say June and July basic; August and September advanced basic; and probably October 
that I was in jump school in Fort Campbell.  Nice time of the year to be doing that kind of 
stuff. 
SM:  Yeah it sure is.  In jump school was it a three-week program at that time? 
  10

JM:  Four weeks. 
SM:  Four weeks?  So you had zero week, ground week, tower week, jump week? 
JM:  Yeah.  There was… I think the firs t week was more physical training type 
and then… of course we started learning basi c parachute, PLF parachute landing falls and 
we had the wind speed slide trainer.  While we were there they shut that trainer down, 
they got some many people with broke arms and legs and what have you on it they 
decided it was a bit much. 
SM:  Wow. 
JM:  But we had the thirty-four foot tower and then we had oh gosh… any 
platforms they could jump of for PLF training and what have you.  The wings smith slide 
trainer was a cable that went from a platform about twenty or thirty feet in the air, about 
twenty feet I’d guess.  It would go down to the ground maybe fifty or a hundred yards 
down.  The idea was they put you on a regular parachute harness and hooked you on this 
thing with a quick release and a rope on the quick release.  You never knew when you 
were going to be released you just start sliding down it, which was like you were landing 
with the wind.  You know as the wind was blowing ten miles an hour you were going ten 
miles an hour when you hit the ground on a parachute.  The idea was always to try to land 
on your side you know, you’d hit with your legs and then roll on your back and then back 
on your feet.  Of course you couldn’t always do that unless you position yourself properly 
by adjusting yourself in the harness, grabbing the harnesses and twisting yourself.  You 
didn’t want to land going backwards that was the feet, bottom and head.  (laughing)  It 
really didn’t like straight ahead either that wasn’t much good either.   You wanted it on 
your side; I always preferred it on my right side.   
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  They would position you.  On this trainer they could twist the harness and 
get you anyway they wanted.  They could get you going backwards or forward and turn 
you lose.  Boy you’d get some pretty rough bumps on that one!   
SM:  How about the 250-foot tower, did they have that operating there at Fort 
Campbell? 
  11

JM:  No our big tower was the thirty four foot tower, but when you were in the 
door of the thirty four foot tower you thought you were on the top of the Empire State 
building.  It looked a long ways down!   
SM:  What do you remember most about your first jump? 
JM:  Well it was pretty dramatic as I recall!  You know they tapped you out 
individually.  You’d stand in the door and then they’d tap you out.  It was a long ways 
down!  I remember the most significant thing about it was you know that we were using 
119s in those days and very, very noisy.  They had the doors open and it was a deafening 
noise.  You stepped out and the thing that hit you more then anything else was the 
silence, the gentle swishing of the air!  (laughing) It was quite dramatic.  That’s what got 
me the most is how quite it got all of a sudden.   
SM:  At what altitude did you jump? 
JM:  Most training jumps are maybe between a thousand- twelve hundred feet.   
SM:  These were all static line of course? 
JM:  These were all static line, yeah.  While we were in they were just changing 
over from T-7s to the T-30… what the heck  was it?  T-7 and T-12 I guess or T-10. 
SM:  You’re talking about the parachute? 
JM:  Yeah.  I can’t remember what the heck the other parachute was that we 
stayed with, but the early one was the 7 and it had a real fast opening.  The later 
parachute that became the standard for many, many years was a parabolic chute that the 
canopy kind of curved in and all of the suspensions lines came out first and the canopy 
came out last.  In fact the canopy was tied to the deployment bag. 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  It was a much slower, smoother opening.  The T-7 the first thing they jerked 
out of the bag was the canopy and then all the lines came out and it was a WHAM!  Boy 
it opened with a jolt!  The thing about it was [there was] no question the parachute was 
open.  (laughing)  The darn later parachute opened so much slower it scared the devil out 
of you and if you’d made a couple of jumps with the T-7 you were prone to pull the 
reserve a little early. (laughing) 
SM:  Oh!  Well what was your count at that…?  
JM:  Four thousand was what they were trying to get out.

SM:  It was four thousand, okay.  The latter parachute… 
JM:  Oh that’s four, you know one thousand, two thousand… 
SM:  Right.  The latter parachute that you used, did it have an opening in the back 
of the parachute so you’d have to look at a forward movement in this chute or was it a 
completely closed canopy? 
JM:  No, no, no.  The only movement you could get… You could get m ovement 
in any direction by slipping the parachute, by pulling down on the suspension lines until 
you actually spilled air out of it.  You could actually steer it fairly well, but it wasn’t like 
the later parachutes with slits in them that you could really steer around.  Over the years 
I’ve managed to always land where I wanted to or close to where I wanted to depending 
upon how strong the wind is.  You know when the wind starts getting up I think ten knots 
I believe was the maximum they’d jump us in during training.  When you get up close to 
ten knots an hour you’re pretty much going where that dad gum wind is taking you. 
SM:  During your jump school jumps where there any may west, cigar rolls, or 
anything like that? 
JM:  During the training no, but on one of my very first jumps in an easy 
company of the 511th there was a total malfunction. 
SM:  Oh no! 
JM:  and it brought home that ‘it’ could happen. 
SM:  Yeah.  I take it it was someone you knew? 
JM:  Well I didn’t know him well he was in a different platoon from me, but he 
was in the same company.  You’re only talking about 250 men I think in a full strength 
company.  So I had seen him.  I’d only been in it a couple of weeks when that happened.   
SM:  You mentioned earlier that some guys would deploy their reserves I take it 
that he…? 
JM:  Well when they were using the… I cannot believe that I can’t remember the 
name of that darn parachute, but I guess it was the T-10, it was a T-7 and T-10 I believe.  
But anyway…it opened so m uch slower and you know they didn’t feel the jolt and by the 
time they got to about three they’re “Holy mackerel!”  and they’d just pull the reserves 
thinking they had a streamer on and of course then they had two canopies out, which is 
not good.  (laughing)

JM:  They had to pull the one back in before it deployed.  
SM:  Yeah, okay.  The incident in the 11th Airborne Division, I guess he didn’t get 
his reserve deployed in time? 
JM:  What actually… he wound up, I don’t know how he managed to do it, maybe 
a horrible body position or horrible exit from the airplane, but he got tangled up in the 
suspension lines and it was just a… the chute ne ver deployed.  They just went all the way 
in, he never pulled the reserve.   
SM:  Whoa. 
JM:  We had about the first division jumps that we went on, the company was 
split in three regiments.  One went to Alaska, one went to I believe Puerto Rico and one 
went to Fort Bragg for this major operation.  They found that the nylon webbing that they 
were using in the T-10 was defective because in cold weather, you know they’d bend 
them back and forth and back and forth and tie them with a rubber band.  They were 
taking a set up there in the unheated hangers where they were storing them and they had 
several that when they jumped and the static line was deployed it would slap against the 
side of the airplane and break.   
SM:  Oh boy. 
JM:  Just broke.  So they had numerous malfunctions where they had to pull 
reserves and a few of them went all the way in because they just didn’t realize that they 
didn’t have anything above them.  Then they modified the nylon.  I don’t know whether 
they put cotton with it or what they did, but they solved that problem. 
SM:  Let’s go ahead and talk about your time at Fort Campbell. 
JM:  Okay.   
SM:  Unless…is there anything else that you want to talk about with regards to 
training? 
JM:  No, no. 
SM:  Okay.  At what point did you get married?  
JM:  I got married on leave, on my first leave between my first eight weeks basic 
and my second eight weeks basic they gave me two weeks.  We corresponded back and

SM:  Okay.  I guess it was probably just after you’d finished airborne training that 
you and Sandy moved to Clarksville together? 
JM:  Yes, yes.  As soon as I had finished the training and been assigned to EASY 
Company the 511th then I could live off post and Sandy came down and we lived in 
Clarksville Tennessee. 
SM:  Okay.  What was the morale of your company? 
JM:  Excellent!  Absolutely excellent!   
SM:  Okay.  
JM:  We’re all gung ho young guys, we wanted to be there, we were fortunate that 
we had some excellent officers.  This Ernest V. Martin was a highly decorated Korean 
War veteran.  He had never missed a single rank from private E-1 up through Captain.  
He’d gone all the way through the enlisted ranks, he got a battlefield commission as a 
second lieutenant then after the Korean War he came back to the states, went through the 
OCS and go his regular commission and was first lieutenant and captain.  So at the time 
that he was our commanding officer he had done through every single one of the ranks 
and he was an excellent officer, he was a good soldier.  Years, years later, I can tell you 
when we were with Air America I ran into two people from that company that I was in 
Vietnam.   
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  The executive officer was a fellow named William Carlson and Carlson was 
a West Point grad and when he was a second lieutenant his first assignment was the 
executive officer of easy of the 511th working under Captain Ernest V. Martin. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  I ran into him oh about 1968.  I was flying a VIP air-conditioned DC-3 in 
Bangkok and as we were taxi out across the airport the cockpit door opened and this guy 
stuck his head in and said, “Do you mind if I sit in the jump seat?”  and I turned around 
and I could see…the first thing I saw  was the nametag ‘Carlson’ then I looked at his face 
and sure enough.  I said, “Well certainly Colonel Carlson.  Do you remember me?”

SM:  Wow!  Who else did you run into? 
JM:  I ran into one of the NCOs and I can’t remember his name.  He was a 
sergeant first class, he was a master sergeant when I ran into him.  But I ran into him 
downtown in a restaurant in Saigon.  I recognized him right off and he recognized me and 
we had dinner together, but that was the end of it.  He was back…he told m e though that 
Ernest V. Martin that time was the full colonel and he was special forces and he was over 
in Okinawa and they went back and forth, in-country and back to Okinawa.   
SM:  Wow!  Do you know about how old he was when he was your CO, Captain 
Martin? 
JM:  Oh he was young, I’m gonna say… let’s see he’d gone in just before the 
Korean War so he’d probably in the service about what 1949 and at that time…’54… I’m  
gonna say he was probably 27, 28 years old.   
SM:  Okay.  Young man.  You mentioned that you jumped out of 119s in jump 
school, did you also use fly out of those in the easy 511th? 
JM:  Yes, yes.  That was the mainstay for the airborne in those days.  They 
grounded all the 119s.  Remember during operation Flash Burn in Fort Bragg, North 
Carolina a 119 lost an engine and went down through a number of paratroops.  Killed 
them and then crashed in a mess hall and killed a bunch of people.  And they ground all 
the 119s.  I don’t remember what the problem was, but it was it was some airworthiness 
in its directive.  It took several months before they put them back in service.  During that 
period of time we jumped anything we could get and I did make one drop in a C-46 and 
one or two in 124.   
SM:  Your airborne status that lasted through your time at Fort Campbell.  How 
about when you went to Europe? 
JM:  Yeah I was still airborne in Europe, we made jumps in Europe. 
SM:  Okay.  A couple of questions about life there in Clarksville and service in 
Fort Campbell, of course at the time you’re in the military it’s become a completely 
integrated institution. 
JM:  Yes.

SM:  It is all… 
JM:  Yeah, the military was yeah. 
SM:  But of course in the United States and especially areas of the south… 
JM:  Ah yeah it was very bad. 
SM:  significant amount of segregation still. 
JM:  Yes. 
SM:  So I was what life was like there in Clarksville. 
JM:  Well it a… I felt sorry for the black  soldiers.  They were second class 
citizens unfortunately and it was you know, they went to the balcony at movie theaters 
and they had their own waiting rooms and bus tunnels and they just… They were 
segregated.  You know having been raised in the north I found it very offensive.  I’d 
made some very good friends who were black in the Army.  Actually within our unit I 
never saw a hint of racial discrimination, either in the states or when we went to Europe. 
SM:  And of course your company commander was black. 
JM:  Yes, yes.  That was the thing that black soldiers thought that it’d be great to 
be in a company with a black commanding officer, but he seemed to be a lot harder on 
the blacks then he was the whites.  (laughing)   
SM:  Really? 
JM:  In that he insisted you know ‘you aren’t in here for fun and games, you’re 
going to measure up or else,’ I think.  He was a soldier first and foremost.  He had his job 
to do and he did it.  He was a good man.   
SM:  How did your NCOs enforce discipline in this team/squad/platoon level? 
JM:  Discipline was not a problem because everybody wanted to be there.  That’s 
one of the things about an elite unit I think where all of the people want to be there.  You 
don’t have the problems you do, I don’t think you have the malcontent that you do in a 
regular Army unit or a regular Marine unit or a regular Air Force unit.  Like I talk about 
the esprit de corps in the Marines, but you can’t quite as a Marine you know.  (laughing)  
If you’re a malcontent you’re still there where in the airborne if you’re a malcontent they 
throw you out and you’re in the Army! 
SM:  They throw you to a leg unit. 
JM:  Yep, that’s exactly right.  Back in those days they sure did.

SM:  Yeah.  What was it like for you to bring your wife there to Fort Campbell, to 
Clarksville and what was the transition like for you as a couple? 
JM:  Oh it was tough because of money quite frankly.  We were very poorly paid 
and the cheapest place you could get was expensive in those days.  We lived in a, the first 
place was a converted garage apartment and it was pretty grim.  Another member of my 
company who lived in a basement apartment that had an outside entrance to a private 
residence, they just rented out this little one room and a bathroom type deal and it was in 
their basement.  He was transferred to another unit and that unit came open, he told me 
about it, and I managed to get in there and life got a whole lot better when we got in 
there.  It was small, but it was in a fairly decent neighborhood and we could come and go 
as we wanted we had our own private entrance.  So it wasn’t bad, but money was always 
a problem.  I had a car, but we couldn’t afford to drive it or anything so my buddies 
would borrow my car and always bring it back full of gas that’s how we managed to 
drive the car.  (laughing)   
SM:  (laughing)  Okay.  How long did you stay at Fort Campbell? 
JM:  Well we were there about, I’m going to say about six months probably 
before we transferred over.  They transferred the entire division to Germany.   
SM:  Oh okay. 
JM:  That was back when the Cold War was really fierce and the U.S. had five 
divisions in Germany.  I think it was kind of a show to the Soviet Union that they were 
really committed because they sent you know, one of their very best units.  Back in those 
days the 11th airborne division was one of the top units in the military so to send an 
airborne over there was making a statement.   
SM:  Yes sir.  What do you think was the primary motivator for most of the men 
who served with you in the company?  Why did they decide to join the Army? 
JM:  Well back in those days you know everybody was going to go in the Army 
sooner or later I mean you were eligible for the draft and they got just about everybody at 
one time or another.  Most of the people thought that ‘well I’ve got three years of military 
service to do and I’m going to do it and put it behind me.’  The people that were in our 
unit of course that was what they really wanted to be in so I think that to get a feel of 
what the regular Army was like would be considerably different then what the 11th

SM:  In terms of the focus of training, what were you preparing for? 
JM:  Well in Europe we were preparing for a major land battle you know.  We 
were deploying divisions against what we thought would be superior divisions on the 
other side as far as being out numbered and out tactics were more or less conventional, in 
the early days they were anyway.  Towards the end they got into the concept of 
dispersing the troops you know, they didn’t want any large units in a relatively small area 
because they were afraid of atomic weapon.  But the first year I spent in Europe it was 
conventional, conventional warfare training.  We were real concerned with tanks, we 
knew the Soviet Union had lots of tanks so there was lots of anti-tank training you know 
and how to use that 57 recoilless rifle and the 3.5 rocket launchers and defending yourself 
against tanks.  Like if you see a tank stand up you know.  In our training missions they’d 
always say that when we would train with tanks, “If you see it stand up!  It’s a lot easier 
to run that way!”  (laughing)  First they can see you and second it’s easier to run that 
way.   
SM:  (laughing) So that was the ground infantrymen’s defense against a tank? 
JM:  Yeah well in training anyway.  When they were friendly tanks you wanted 
them to see you.  (laughing) 
SM:  Right.  (laughing)  Okay.  The relationship between the military and the 
civilian communities at both Fort Campbell and in Europe, what was it like? 
JM:  Well we were accepted I thought very well in Europe.  Fort Campbell I think 
you know there was an awful a lot of money [that] came from that post so I think they 
appreciated the fact that they had that captive audience.  I didn’t notice any real 
animosity, but I didn’t notice any real rigorous friendship either.  In Europe on the other 
hand we were welcomed with open arms into all these restaurants and when we did get 
passes, which was infrequent to get out on the town, when we’d go out on the local 
economy we were treated very well.   
SM:  What was your position in EASY?

JM:  Well I worked my way through the ranks I for the bulk of my time I 
managed to go up through the ranks fairly fast.  Thanks goodness Ernest V. Martin liked 
me!  (laughing) 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  I made staff sergeant and we were a little light on rank in the company so as 
the staff sergeant I was the platoon sergeant and I was that for most of my, almost all of 
my time in Europe.  I was… the way I thi nk I got up through the ranks they… W e were 
normally the first in the division in all of our training and inspection and our arms room 
failed an inspection and Ernest V. Martin went ballistic and he just grabbed me out of the 
ranks one day.  At that time I was a corporal and he said, “McRainey you’re the company 
armor.”  And is started to protest and I could see by the look in his face that wasn’t the 
right way to go!  (laughing)  So I became the company armory and I was very unhappy 
with it because I liked the fact of training with the troops and the armor was down there 
everyday all day in that god darn arms room.  But we got it shaped up and the next 
inspection we did very well and then he put somebody else in there and made me a 
platoon sergeant.  (laughing) So it was worthwhile for my few months of service as the 
company armorer.  
SM:  You were rewarded.  While you were in Europe you said you were basically 
platoon sergeant for pretty much your whole time. 
JM:  Oh almost my whole time, yeah. 
SM:  How many jumps did you make in Europe? 
JM:  Oh we jumped about once a month the whole time we were there.  There was 
a part of our company, a part of our division had a function in training German 
paratroopers.  They were starting to train the Germans and they call themselves the 
Fallschirmjaggers I believe, parachutist.  So we had jumps where we would take a 
platoon of 11th Airborne and jump with a platoon or company of Germans on training 
exercises.  
SM:  Was there any kind of residual well I guess animosity or concern… In the 
American ranks in the units that were serving over there there had to have been young 
men who’s fathers were perhaps killed in World War II…

JM:  Very strange…we actually had Germ an citizens who joined the U.S. Army

you became eligible to be a citizen and we had several Germans in our company.  No,

in fact we all like them, we got along fine with them.   
SM:  How about Americans who didn’t like being in Germany because maybe 
their parents, their father, their uncles were killed? 
JM:  You know I didn’t see that either in our unit anyway.  Most of our people in 
our company anyway were Korean War and they had just been through hell.  We had 
several highly decorated, in fact almost all of our senior NCOs and officers with the 
exception of Carlson were Korean War veterans who saw a lot of combat in the 187th 
Airborne Infantry regiment in Korea.  So I think Korea was the big focus with us rather 
then World War II. 
SM:  Okay.  Well is there anything else that you wanted to discus regarding your 
time with either the EASY company 5-11 at Campbell or in Europe? 
JM:  No not particularly. 
SM:  Well let’s go ahead and look at some of the larger political events that were 
going on and how maybe they impacted you as [not] just an American citizen, but also an 
American soldier.   
JM:  Okay. 
SM:  Or course an awful lot of Cold War activity occurring. 
JM:  Oh yeah. 
SM:  and let’s see when you were in Europe had the Hungarian Uprising 
occurred? 
JM:  Oh yeah, in fact they put us on full alert and they were…all of our senior 
officers were planning who was going to do what to whom.  We really thought we were 
going to be air dropped in and we’d led them on you know.  We were listening to radio 
where the freedom fighters were begging for the Americans to come in because the 
Americans more or less led them to believe that if they rebelled they would support them.  
And Old Eisenhower never did so they just went down the tubes, but that would have 
probably World War III right there if he had.  I don’t know whether he made the right

decision or the wrong decision, but never the less we were ready to go to war.  When we 
had exercises they would…and we went on th em frequently at least once a month, but 
they were as close to the actual deployment as they could get.  What they would do is we 
would empty out…every com pany and station in these various Kasernes, which were 
little military establishments nestled into villages and cities that we were in…had all the 
supplies they needed to go to war.  They had C-rations; they had ammunition; and they 
had them you know stored, they had ordinance lockers and they had food lockers and 
what have you.  When we would deploy our company…the trucks that were assigned to 
us soldiers would load all the ammunition into the trucks you know and the Krait, they’d 
load all the C-rations in and then we would take off out into the boon docks and deploy 
where we supposed to deploy.  But it was never break open the boxes of ammunition and 
break open the C-rations.  And we were never issued parachutes on any of those that I 
recall.  But on the temporary deployment or the exercise that we went through for the 
Hungarian Uprising they broke open the boxes and issued the standard bandoliers of 
ammunition and they broke open the C-rations and issued the basic supply of C-rations.  
Took us out to the airport and we went through the parachute loft and everybody was 
issued their parachute and hey, we’d been through it at that time a lot of maneuvers and 
we knew this was no ordinary maneuver.  So we were ready to go, I think they were very 
serious about deployment at that time. 
SM:  But ultimately you never did [?]? 
JM:  I never did.  No. 
SM:  Did the [?] become a topic of conversation?  Why we would support them? 
JM:  Oh yeah.  A lot animosity about that, our people from our division 
commander on down were really hacked that we didn’t.  They thought that we’d let those 
people down, that we’d let them believe that we would support them and then turn our 
backs on them.  Heck I think the troops were ready to go to war! 
SM:  Yeah.  Did that ever happen again? 
JM:  Well when they had the problem in Lebanon, I had just left, but they sent the 
part of our outfit down there.  In fact my whole company went down there. 
SM:  Whoa!

JM:  So that was way back you know, that would have been in the late fifties.  But

SM:  Of course in terms of the Soviet developments…and this would have been a 
little bit before, well yeah, this was before your military experience, but I’m curious what 
you heard about, what you thought and perhaps what was discussed amongst you and 
your family members and friends when it became news that the Soviets had the Atom 
bomb.   
JM:  Well of course, hell there was concern that the, you know in those days the 
deterrent was the big thing if you destroy us, we’ll destroy you and it’s who can destroy 
the most and when it got to the point to where it was totally unacceptable to either side 
then we had that stand off that lasted so long.  But no, the threat was there, the threat was 
real and I think everybody felt that threat; I know I certainly did.  When we started 
training for atomic warfare what we were expecting atomic artillery to be used against the 
troops on the field that’s when they changed their tactics and went from the division-
battalion company to what they call regimental combat teams.  The idea was smaller self-
contained units that you know they could move fast and disperse rather then have a big 
cumbersome unit in one location.   
SM:  And that would… I assum e that that’s also an emphasis of training that you 
had in Europe? 
JM:  Yes, yes it was. 
SM:  How affective did you think that would be? 
JM:  Well in all the scenarios that we played we thought we were highly effective, 
but whether it really came down to shooting you know it’s anybody’s guess, I guess.  I’m 
not sure you know, we don’t know what type of nuclear, we don’t know if all of their 
nuclear weapons were the intercontinental ballistic missile types or whether they really 
had concentrated on the smaller units to be used in the field.  I think that we felt that they 
had them, but I’m not sure they really did even to tell you the truth. 
SM:  Was it ever talked about that the use of atomic or nuclear weapons by the 
Russians especially in Germany if they were to come through the Fulda Gap and all that, 
tried to take over the rest of Europe that they…

JM:  Well we… the m ilitary thinking at that time that they wouldn’t, it would be a

were you know, felt so smug that they were vastly superior in ground forces that they

five divisions there to try to stop them.  So we were really expecting a conventional war. 
SM:  How about when China fell in 1949.  Was that much of a topic of 
conversation for you and your family? 
JM:  Well no not for me I was pretty young then, but I… later on by the tim e I 
was military age it had seen very important to me.  I had read a lot about Chiang Kai 
Shek and Chennault and it fascinated me, I’ll put it that way.   
SM:  Well what made you decide not to reenlist?  
JM:  Well you know I came real close to reenlisting and I thought long and hard 
about it, but I thought, ‘well you know, I really should get home and start a family!’ 
(laughing)  But I liked the military and it was on my mind.  I felt very seriously about 
reenlisting.  Martin wanted me to go OCS and he was going to see that I got in and it was 
real tempting, it really was.   
SM:  Of course we should clarify; you were alone in Europe, Sandy… 
JM:  That’s right that was an unaccompanied tour, that’s right.  She was back in 
the states.  I was anxious to get home, I’d been married two weeks, but that’s all!  
(laughing)   
SM:  (laughing)  Okay.  Well so what were your plans when you got out? 
JM:  Well I kind of thought like my grandfather I wanted to be in business for 
myself and we’d saved the money.  Sandy had worked and lived on my folks while I was 
overseas.  I leased a shell service station and we setup general maintenance as well as 
gas.  I friend of mine from the service, Ray White, came back with me and he work with 
me in my filling station and we ran a successful station.  After I had been at it a year one 
of my better customers, by better customer a fellow that stopped in all the time, he was a 
young fellow that just got out of the Army about the same time we did.  He was learning 
to fly and he told us about the G.I. Bill and boy Ray and I both said, “Wow!  That sounds 
good!”  And he said, “Yeah, if you want to be a commercial pilot then…” you know.  So 
we went out to the airport with him and of course he got himself an hour free instruction

by signing us up.  We had to apply through the VA to get the benefits, but within a very 
short period of time we were enrolled in a VA flight school, Nelson Ryan out in 
Minneapolis and started learning to fly.  Once, ah heck I think the very first time I went 
up I realize that’s what I wanted to do!  At the station of course we worked long, hard 
hours there out of the garage and I could see that it would take me many years to learn to 
fly if I kept doing it at the rate I was starting at, I was lucky to get two or three hours a 
week.  So we sold the business and I took a job as a security guard at night so that I could 
go out to the airport everyday all day.  I got my ratings very fast.  I got my commercial 
and my instrument and my flight instructor’s all in about a year. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  I got hired there as a flight instructor right off too, so my flying career started 
off with a boom!  I found myself just living at the airport working seven days a week, 
teaching ground school in the evenings, and flying charter, and flying flight instructing.  
The company was a large outfit that had a lot of specialized Contracts so we got to do 
some interesting things.  We did power line patrol for the REA and we sprayed for the 
Twin City mosquito control.  So I got to do a little spraying and I got to fly the power line 
and I got to fly charters.  We did a lot of fishing trips and fish camp supplying up in 
Northern Saskatchewan and the Hudson Bay area.  Within three or four years I was a 
fairly experience aviator, I was flying over a thousand hours a year. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  So by ’61 or ’62 I was very, very experienced.  The airlines weren’t hiring 
much back in those days.  Northwest, one of my good friends got hired by Northwest and 
he lived with us in a trailer park.  They had a…when they got into th e jet, I’m trying to 
think of what year that was it probably around about ’52 or 3… They had a big strike 
over whether the third person in the airplane and back in the DC-6 and 7 days the 
engineers were mechanic engineers and they were aircraft mechanics that had flight 
engineer certificates.  Now the pilot’s union wanted the pilots to be flight engineers and 
they wanted to train another pilot to be on there.  They’re argument was you need a third 
set of eyes.  Well a mechanic engineer can see as well as a pilot engineer, but you know 
how unions are.  So Northwest had a long, lengthy strike about seventeen months.  My 
friend Rex was laid off and he found a blind ad in a Miami paper looking for flight

and sending in my resumes and ah heck I updated my resume every thirty days for about 
two years before I finally got… In fact I re member the night well.  I was flying for the 
Creamets Company, the Creamets Macaroni Company, I started their flight department.  
We had landed in Minneapolis, the weather was really lousy, we couldn’t get in the 
Flying Cloud it was below minimum so we landed over at the Minneapolis International, 
in those days it was called Old Chamberlain.  It was below zero and it’s snowing, it was 
really nasty.  I had called Sandy… we left fr om Detroit Lakefront Airport… and I called 
her and told her what time we were coming in and asked her if she’d pick me up at the 
airport because I wouldn’t be able to get my car out at Flying Cloud.  So she came to 
Wool to pick us up and the fellow that ran the flight department, a fellow named John 
Westerburg, he was the senior vice-president of the company in charge of marketing and 
the flight department fell under his baliwick.  He was with me and we were going to drive 
him over to flying cloud airport and Sandy had the kids bundled up in the car, she didn’t 
want to leave them in the trailer alone, naturally.  So she said, “John, these people from 
Washington called and said you were hired and for you to send your passport in 
immediately to get visas!”  And I just turned and John was in the backseat and I said, 
“John you’ve got my resignation right now!” (laughing)  “you’ve got two weeks.”  And 
that’s how I wound up going with… back in those days CAT hired you it wasn’t Air 
America, it was still Air Transport.   
SM:  Did you know who was actually owning and running that airline? 
JM:  Oh yes… well no, you know, it was hinted  at, but we knew that CAT was an 
airline it was a flag carrier of Nationalist to China and we knew that they did some Terry 
and the Pirates stuff.  I’d never heard the word ‘Air America’ up until that time though.  
SM:  Okay.  Now when did you get out of the Army?  That was 1956? 
JM:  1957. 
SM:  Oh ’57.

would have less accidents with it and heck we had one of those little turkeys upside down 
in every airport in Minnesota.  It was like balancing on a three-legged milk stool.

JM:  But then… this company was a Cessu a dealer and we had 1-72s and 1-50s 
and we had 3-10s from multi-engine training for charter.  I of course flew all of them 
once I was an instructor.

JM:  Oh yeah I got my multi-engine rating with 3-10 and one of the old sky king 
you know straight tail and vertical tip tanks and augmenter tubes over the top of the 
wing…The early, early 310.

successful program.  We did it the whole time I was there and I think they continued it

great salaries and I had just taken over the Creamet Company flight department, I was 
making pretty good money and I had a good future with Creamet.  She thought I was 
absolutely lost my mind because the base pay looked a lot lower for civil air transport 
then what I was getting, but I knew that there was a lot more to be made then what they… 
but the Contract price for, you were hired as a first officer naturally, I can’t remember 
what the base salary for a first officer I think it was 1200 dollars a month and that was a 
lot less then I was making then.

JM:  Well yes I did.  Old Rick Nelick had told me about that and it just…I was 
drooling that’s what I wanted to do! 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  But I also knew that there was a big company and you could be based in any 
number of different operations.  I could have wound up from Japan, Taipei, or Southeast 
Asia, I was hoping for Southeast Asia of course.  I lucked out when I got to Taipei they 
had had a dry spell of about two years that they hadn’t been able to get anybody through 
the Chinese pilot’s license program.  Most of our C-46s and C-47s and even a lot of our 
lighter airplanes were Chinese registered and the Chinese are real bureaucrats and they 
had tightened up on the testing where nobody could pass the written test.  The company 
had hired one of the senior Chinese aviation officials when he retired from their 
equivalent of the FAA, they hired him to be a ground instructor for CAP and the first 
thing he did was setup a ground school to teach the new Chinese senior commercial 
pilot’s license program.  So we had a two-week ground school, very extensive ground 
school. 
SM:  Whoa! 
JM:  and it wasn’t easy.  They had used, oh gosh, they used several different 
textbooks like the Navy all-weather flight manual, British… ah I can’t remember what the 
heck that was.  Like flight engineer program and the FAA, the ATP and they’d taken 
questions and problems out of all these different manuals and they’d made up a two-day 
written examination.  It was a nightmare! 
SM:  Whoa! 
JM:  And…well to give you an idea of how ridiculous they were they had radius 
of action to a moving base ten question.  Yeah, you work that out on an DC 6-B you 
know and they wouldn’t allow you to just write down the answer you had to do it in 
longhand and turn in the paper so they saw you really knew how to do it.  That was one 
of the jokes on the ground school.  One of the wags that thought he was going to be in the 
Beech problem said, “If I get to Saigon on those Beech crafts that have got tail hooks I’m 
quitting right now!”  (laughing)   
SM:  Well what was the…was there a ra tional behind them changing the testing 
scheme, making it [?]?

JM:  It was just to stop the gringos from getting pilot’s licenses, they were made 
at CAT for one reason or another.  There was always a little animosity there.  There was a 
faction that supported CAT of course or they wouldn’t have been in the country, but then 
there was a faction that supported China Airlines that was trying to get started and 
wanted the flag.  So these gringos were seen as the threat to them.  This Chinese 
instructor that they hired, this senior official turned out to be one heck of a guy.  He was 
an excellent instructor, he was really a man of substance.  He taught the test.  They’d 
brought a lot of people from Southeast Asia up there and all the new hires that came in 
they kept there so that they could get as many as possible through this class.  I believe 
about twenty of us went to the class and I think about fourteen of us passed the darn thing 
right off, which was tremendous success for them because they hadn’t gotten anybody 
through in almost two years.  Now I didn’t realize how much that changed my future.  I 
was supposed to be assigned to a C-46, C-46 first officer is what I was told and when I 
got to…we did the ground school in Taipei and we did some link training in Taipei and 
then they assigned me to Saigon as a base, which surprised me, but then they had…our 
ground school was in Bangkok for the airplane, you know aircraft specific training.  So 
we went up to Bangkok and I had this week of C-46 program and I thought ‘well, you 
know, I’ll be flying a C-46,’ but then somebody told me they didn’t have any C-46s.  
(laughing)  I thought ‘what the heck is this?’  They didn’t at that time although they knew 
that there was a bunch of them coming in and right after I got there nine came in all B-
registered.  I was just about ready to go to Saigon when they stopped me and said, “No.  
Now you’ve got to go through the 10-2 program.”  The 10-2 is a Beech 18 that they had a 
lot of MODs on that grows 10,200 pounds.  When you went through that program they 
also put you through ground school on the dornier and the Apache because they call it the 
Lake Twin Program in Saigon.   
SM:  Okay.  So you were actually qualified for three aircrafts? 
JM:  I was qualified…w ell actually at that time I was qualified for four as far as 
ground school’s concerned, the C-46, the 10-2, the dornier, and the Apache.   
SM:  Okay.  You mentioned in the interview with the, I guess it’s the History 
Channel that Monty Markum asks you what you thought of the dornier. 
JM:  Yeah.

SM:  (laughing) Well I was surprised he didn’t ask you why…why didn’t you?  
JM:  Well it wasn’t…it was supposed to be a STOL airplane and it wasn’t all that 
great of performer.  It wasn’t all that nice to fly an airplane either.  You know I mean it 
wasn’t a death trap or anything like that, but it was in my opinion it was a poor excuse for 
an airplane.   
SM:  Okay.  So it just didn’t fly well? 
JM:  Well I flew it.  I checked out in it and I flew it a little operationally, but I 
never really cared much for it.  I’d checked out in the Apache and I flew it and of course 
that’s a nice flying little airplane.  My primary airplane…w hen I first got there they were 
desperately short of Beech pilots, 10-2 pilots.  The chief pilot was a fellow named Jim 
Russell, which if you’d wanted a poster for a mercenary character, I guess Jim Russell 
would have fit it perfectly.  He was kind of a handsome dude, a big guy with broad 
shoulders and barrel chested and little thin mustache and talked out of the side of his 
mouth.  He was quite a character.  Jim was the light airplane pilot chief pilot.  At that 
time he was having a heck of a time training pilots in it.  He’d got guys that had spent 
twenty years in the military, but unfortunately the last fifteen had been in center line jets 
you know and they forgot to use their feet, they forgot how to use their feet on the 
rudders.  So he was washing people out right and left and he was flying the line and 
trying to train people and he said, “You know.”  He doesn’t have time to go back to 
basics and put people through an extensive course in the airplane, he wanted somebody 
that knew how to fly it.  I had Beech 18 time on my resume and when they saw that they 
diverted me to go back into the Beech program, come over there and try to check out as a 
Beech pilot.  I was disappointed at first, but when I got to Saigon and old Russell grabbed 
me right off the bat and said, “Let’s go fly the Beech!”  We went out for two hours and I 
came back as captain…n ever flew a single day as co-pilot so actually it was quite an 
advantage to have flown the Beech at that particular time.  Then I was off flying the 
Beech…within five or six m onths all of the C-46s came in and Jim Russell was made the 
chief pilot of 46 program and I became a senior instructor pilot in the light airplane 
program; on the dornier, the Apache, and the Beech.  Then he was having the same

you’re talking with the pilots and you realize that you’re flying sensitive government 
Contracts.

JM:  I didn’t even have my uniforms yet, I’d just brought my suitcase sat it down 
outside of ops, went for a two hour check ride and then I was given some maps by FIC 
and a briefing by FIC, when out and got in the airplane, flew to Da Nang and relived the 
pilot that had been at Da Nang for a week or two and flew it the direction of the CIA 
customer up there named Clancy.  That was when I began to realize just exactly what I 
had gotten into and I loved every minute of it!

JM:  I flew from Saigon to Da Nang empty and then I reported into Clancy and 
then I started flying in the northern part of the country from Dong Hod you know in the 
north right on the division of the North and South Vietnam down to…oh we m ight have 
gone as far south as Qui Nhon, but when we were flying out of Da Nang it was mostly 
north, Quang Tri, up in that area.

JM:  Yeah a Beech 18, yeah.

JM:  We’d carry… th e airplane was outfitted with ten seats; it had ten passenger 
seats in the passenger compartment plus crew too.  Although we only flew it single pilot, 
but it had a co-pilot station, you could carry a passenger up there to if you wanted.  You

could carry…it had a cargo door that was put  in by they company.  Really a neat 
installation at…it f it in right behind the air stair door.  The company had a…it had an air 
stair door, you’d let the door down and it became the stairs to get up in the airplane.  
Then by just a couple of quick fasteners you could pull a cargo door out that went from 
there back about three feet.  So you had that three feet plus you know two and a half feet 
of the door opening so you had a fairly sizeable cargo opening if you wanted to haul 
cargo.  Of course all the seats were quick disconnect, you could pull the seats up and 
configure it in cargo in a matter of minutes.  The airplane for all practical purposes was a 
basic Beech 18, but we had a few C-45s that weren’t converted to 10-2s and the C-45 had 
the old Hamilton standard two bladed prop and of course the collector rings were 
exhausted.  It only grossed about 86,87 hundred pounds so it as quite limited in what you 
could do with it.  The 10-2 had all the mods that you could put on a Beech.  It had the 
angle of instant exchange and the tail head squared off, wingtips, three bladed hartsfield 
props, individual stacks…it had all kind of mods and it was really quite a nice old 
airplane.

JM:  Pardon? 
SM:  In terms of…it was pretty m uch maxed out in terms of its performance? 
JM:  Oh yeah, yeah.  It was about all you’re going to get out of a Beech 18, but a 
Beech 18’s a pretty good airplane if you can fly it.  (laughing)  It’s a handful! 
SM:  What was your primary duties flying the Beech 18 especially in terms of 
customer support? 
JM:  It was primarily picking up agency people and taking them to the various 
field places they wanted to go and waiting for them and brining them back.  It was just a 
kind of an executive transport for the agency.  Now we did haul USAID military and 
other people from time to time that they’d put on, but primarily you were the agency’s 
direct support for getting from A to B. 
SM:  How much would you be briefed in terms of what was going on? 
JM:  Oh not at all!  Your primary customer whoever he might be would say, “Go 
to Dong Ha and pick up one man.”  You’d go to Dong Ha and pick up one man, he’d be 
waiting for you when he got there.  They coordinated everything by radio.

then come back and pick them up whenever they told us to come back and pick them up.   
SM:  Okay.  Do you know if you were supporting certain types of operations, for 
instances I know that near the Vietnamese-Lao border there was of course a couple of 
operations, road watch… 
JM:  Yeah. 
SM:  trying to watch the traffic coming down the Ho Chi Minh Trail, stuff like 
that.  Do you know if you guys flew in support of that type of operation? 
JM:  We flew customers in support of some operations.  I’ve even made some 
trips out of the Kontum province area over into Cambodia, but few and far between.  
Maybe the whole period of time I was there maybe on three different occasions. 
SM:  Okay.  How long were you flying in Vietnam?  How long did you fly? 
JM:  Well in November of ’66 I’d been flying the C-46 for a while and an 
opportunity came for a pilot in Bangkok and a customer type…let m e think of his name, I 
understand he became head of AIROPS at one time, he was kind of a Clark Gable look-
alike.  Good grief…he’s nam e will come to me.  I became friends with him early in my 
Beech days and we stayed friends throughout my stay in Vietnam.  He kind of put my 
name up for grabs for an opportunity to fly an infrared project in…it was based out of 
Bangkok, but it really flew out of Chang Air Force base in Singapore.  You had to have 
some sort of a slot in Bangkok to fly it because it flew infrequently.  So the only 
slot…th e first slot that opened up in Bangkok was for a BTB-18 and that was a Beech 
with a turbo prop engines.  Oh…Jack Beni field, Jack Benifield was the gentleman’s

name.  I kind of I think paid the way for me to get that slot and I said, “Yeah, I’d jump on 
that I’ll be with my wife in Bangkok, that will be a good deal!” 
SM:  Yeah. 
JM:  So I transferred to Bangkok to fly the volpar and I checked out in it and then 
of course I had to check out in the C-47 because the airplane that they used for the project 
was a HC-47.  It had…I’ve heard people refer to it as the Pan American wing.  It had 
sixteen hundred gallons of fuel in the wings were a standard C-47 at 800 gallons.  I 
believe the Air Force used it for search and rescue.  This one was regular stars and bars, it 
said ‘US Air Force’ and it had the stars and bars on it.  I could never figure that out, 
here’s a secret project, it’s got a civilian pilot wearing an Air America uniform flying a 
stars and bars airplane out of a British Air Force base… Now what the hell is this? 
(laughing) 
SM:  (laughing)  And this was the IR project? 
JM:  This was the early days of the infrared project and it was a joint project 
between the U.S. and the British military research and development.   
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  The people in charge, they’re had a wing commander really in charge of it, a 
wing commander Sumners, but the personnel were all civilian, high GS rating, PHD 
types that were the people that we worked with.  It was a fine laboratory.  It had of course 
they had a photo bay where you would actually you know photograph the ground and 
take these IR pictures.  Then they’d develop them and interpret them right on board in the 
laboratory. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  Of course this was the early days so they didn’t really know what they were 
looking at. (laughing) So they had to setup tests fields where they would setup a test area 
where they’d fire guns, run trucks, have a bunch of troops, all kinds of different things.  
You would fly grids over it and take the pictures and then the people would identify what 
it was they were looking at.  Of course we did it at various altitudes and we did it in 
various types of weather you know to get a good test on it.  Then when I got real good at 
that then I would go out and look for the communist Muslim factor that was giving 
Malaysia some problems in those days.

SM:  Oh…okay.  So I mean you trained I guess around Bangkok?  
JM:  No, no we never trained, never trained in Bangkok it was all done in 
Malaysia. 
SM:  Oh.  Oh it was all in Malaysia? 
JM:  Yeah, we would… 
SM:  Oh! 
JM:  The plane was based and we would fly it out to Chang Air Force base, 
Chang is now the main base I mean that’s the main civil airport in Singapore.  In the old 
days [?] was the main base and Chang was the royal Air Force base.  When we were 
there it was just an Air Force base and I’d stay at the, it was called the Ocean Palace 
Hotel I believe, it was an old British hotel right on the coast.  They had that land 
reclamation program going where they were tearing down the mountains and putting in 
the bay to get more ground and geeze I think the last time I flew that airplane there it was 
a mile from the ocean.  (laughing)  It was all flat land out in front of it.  We would takeoff 
just at sunset and the Brits would fly the project with us and they had Shackeltons that 
they were using.  I don’t know if you’ve ever heard of a Shackleton? 
SM:  No. 
JM:  That was quite an interesting airplane.  It was a big four engine…they used it 
as the submarine patrol bomber I think is what its primary mission was, anti-submarine 
patrol.  It had big liquid cooled engines with two four bladed counter rotating propellers 
on each engine.  Obviously it was a maintenance nightmare.  They had a fleet of about 
two dozen of them and I think if they had three of them flying they were happy.  It was a 
long-range airplane too and of course the HC-47 sixteen hundred gallons of fuel and for 
all practical purposes we’d burn a hundred gallons an hour so we would stay in the air 
about sixteen hours.  We would take off at sunset and come back about sunrise on those 
missions.   
SM:  What would you use or was there anything you could use to help stay awake 
besides coffee? 
JM:  Oh no…well it was actually fairly in tensive on the training.  When we were 
flying those grids we really had to pay close attention.  We had to know exactly where we 
were every minute you know and navigational aid were fairly primitive, we had some

kept us busy enough that we didn’t… staying awake was never a problem. 
SM:  Okay.  Now I’m curious if you can say what altitudes you typically flew at 
for those? 
JM:  Well I would say typically 1,000-1,500 feet above the terrain, but we have 
gotten down as low as 2 or 300 feet off the terrain if we had a moonlit night and we could 
see what the heck, you know, that we weren’t going to run into a ridge or a tall tree 
sticking up or something.  They wanted to try it as low as possible and then I think the 
highest I ever flew it was probably 3,000 feet above the terrain. 
SM:  Okay.  Now was this in part due to the restrictions on the infrared equipment 
in terms of techniques? 
JM:  Yes it was strictly the restrictions on the equipment.  The equipment was 
pretty primitive, it you know, it took them a long time to figure out whether it was troops 
they were flying over or whether it was the water buffalo or whether it was a gun being 
fired or the exhaust of a car engine or what it was, but they got pretty good at it.  I think 
by the time we finished the project…well of  course the equipment they kept upgrading 
too so the whole thing was a whole lot better towards the end.   
SM:  How long did you do this? 
JM:  We did it…Ed Olrick had started the project.  He had been flying for about a 
year and then when I came down to fly I flew it oh let’s see…from  early in probably 
January of ’67 and all of ’67 and all of ’68, so a couple of years.   
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  Now it was infrequent, sometimes we wouldn’t fly but go down for about 
three or four days once a month was what it started out at.  Then it got to the point where 
it was about every three months for a week, then it got maybe every four or five months 
for a week; you know just whenever they needed it.  Sometimes you might make a trip 
and then come back for a week and go right back for a day or two because something 
didn’t come up quite the way they expected it. 
SM:  What would you do in the interim, in those breaks between?

JM:  Oh in my regular job then was flying…shortly after I got there we got 
another DC-3; it was a DC-3C it wasn’t a C-47.  It was an interesting little airplane, it had 
belonged the United Airlines back before the war and it got involved in the…I can’t 
remember the name of the program.  It was the program where the airlines supplied 
aircrafts and crews to the military.  What the heck did they call that program…anyway.  
The airplane flew at military’s direction; it was converted to a DC-3C, which looked just 
like a C-47.  It had you now a cargo door and bucket seats what have you, but there was 
some slight differences between a DC-3 and a C-47.  I think the thickness of the 
aluminum and the fuselage and the strength of the gear and a few things like that, but to 
look at them you couldn’t tell them apart.  Anyway, this airplane flew Military Airlift 
Command I believe that’s what they called it for about, well through the war and at the 
end of the war I guess United felt ‘hell, we don’t need that old cargo plane back’ and they 
sold it to the Dutch KLM.  KLM converted it back to a DC-3 and flew it down in the 
islands for a number of years out of Indonesia and down in there.  Then sold it to the 
Indonesian airline, what the heck was their name…well anyway.  Then it flew for them  
until it was retired from service and then CAT bought it.  Took it up to Air Asia, 
converted it back to a DC-3C, but they had a requirement for a VIP airplane kind of an 
embassy airplane in Bangkok.  They configured it with twelve plush seats and a 
compartment that started about the trailing edge of the wing forward and they put two 
electric Beech air conditioners in the cockpit to cool it.  I mean they were mounted on the 
cockpit side, but they outlets were into this compartment where they blow air-conditioned 
air.  It was configured…it had two tables with two seats on each side of each table, that’d 
be eight and then it had four seats on the other side, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12.  It had twelve first 
class type seats in that compartment.  Then from the trailing edge back it was no 
insulation, it was regular C-47 with a bucket seat.  (laughing) So it was used as a courier 
type airplane, it could carry cargo in the back and a few extra passengers in the back and 
then it could carry twelve people in air-conditioned splendor in the main cabin.  Then that 
was given to me as my primary airplane, when I wasn’t flying project I flew that; 9-3-3 
was its number.   
SM:  Okay.  Who would you fly typically in that?

JM:  Oh gosh it could be anybody.  It could be agency, it could be military, it 
could be state department.  We had couriers setup in that airplane where we flew an 
airline schedule that went to all the military bases.  We’d pick up mail, drop off mail, 
pick up people and drop off people.  We went…we had a northeast trip that we went from  
Bangkok to, oh let me think of the first base.  It was a F-105 base, Ubon?  No…anyway.  
We went to this base that was oh 125 miles northeast of Thailand.  I guess it was Ubon, it 
was.  No it wasn’t either, Ubon was over by PAXICE on the Thailand side. 
SM:  Not Udorn? 
JM:  No not Udorn.  It was…the biggest one  was five days, most of the 105 raids 
launched on North Vietnam or launched out of this base.  It was central Thailand… 
SM:  Oh, um… 
JM:  “Dressy Lady” was the VOR that was on the mountain ridge just south of 
this base, I can remember it well.  We went into there and then we would go straight east 
out of there over the river to Ubon, which was F-4s that was where Chappy James and 
Robin Olds were.  Then we would fly north to the civilian Nakhon Phanom airport there 
was a Nakhon Phanom military, which was where the super secret listening base and the 
air commandos flew out of.  They flew the A-1Es and the nimrod B-26s out of there.  
SM:  Okay.   
JM:  But we would go over to the civilian field on the river and then from there 
over to Udorn where we would…that be the fi rst time we’d shut both engines down and 
we would have lunch, stay on the ground about and hour and then we’d go to a base that 
was about 75 miles straight south of Udorn.  It was a big base, but I never saw any 
buildings on it just a runway they were supposed to be building the base there.  Then 
from there we would go to Bangkok. 
SM:  Okay. 
JM:  Then the next one would be the northwest run.  We’d go from Bangkok to 
Korat the Ranch and then we’d go from there to Phitsanulok and we would land at Pitts 
Camp, which was up in the mountains and that was where Bill Lare had started his career 
with the Para troops in Thailand where they trained the Thai paratroops border control 
and then agency people had a base up there.  Then from there we would go to Chang Rae 
and then from Chang Rae to Chang My and then from Chang My back to Takhli and then

back to Bangkok.  They had a southern route where we would fly from Bangkok to
