Nakhon Se Tamirat and there were some agency people there and what they were doing 
there I don’t know, but it was a base.  From there we would go down to Thonclaw and 
Thonclaw was on the coast of pretty much the Malaysian border, I think about eighteen 
miles from the Malaysian border.  The Thais had thought that that was going to be a big 
resort area and the Thai government built a nice hotel down there, but that’s about all that 
was there except for another agency operation and we had a H-34 helicopter based down 
there that old Tom…oh heck what was his name?  He was our, I think he was our 
seniority number one in the helicopter program.  Tom Moore was it?  Anyway this old H-
34 driver he flew the troops agency people out into the boondocks rather then the 
Malaysian border to some sort of a radio I think it was station.  They stayed down there 
all day and at night he’d bring them…fly down, pick them  up and bring them back to 
Thonclaw.  Then we’d fly from Thonclaw back to Bangkok.  That was the southern run 
and it would be like Monday, Wednesday, and Friday you would make those schedule 
runs and then in between you’d fly wherever they needed you.   
SM:  Okay.  That’s quite an elaborate schedule. 
JM:  Yeah it was really and then when I was off on a project we had…Ed Orlick 
would fly the airplane if he had, he was the chief pilot in those days up until Fred Walker 
came down.  Then we had Verge Domino was based there and Walter Rosenfield and of 
course for a while we had, well actually for quite a while we had old George Ritter there 
too.  It was a nice base.  We had a caribou, we had a…well we had a couple 46s too.  The 
98-4 and 98-5, the two T-cap 46s that had flown over out of Okinawa on the Ryukyuan 
Operation.  They were configured with in passengers; I think it kept 54 seats in them, 
passenger seats with a galley and a toilet…a regular airline configuration.  When that 
operation in Okinawa folded they brought the airplanes to Bangkok and we just kept 
them limber, our maintenance people ran them up every now and then.  I flew the 
airplanes maybe, oh gosh I made maybe two or three runs up to Hong Kong with them I 
know.  I think we made one run down to Singapore.  In the year and a half that they were 
there I probably flew them a half a dozen times.

SM:  Now the flights that you made in Malaysia, the infrared project, did you ever 
get any feedback on how effective these overflights were? 
JM:  Not really, again I think that if I had been curious enough to pump the guys 
for information they would have told me, but it was always…the discrete thing to do was 
not to ask too many questions about what you were doing.  We had our instructions as far 
was what altitude, what airspeed, and all that kind of stuff and that’s all we paid any real 
attention to. 
SM:  Okay.  But I mean, the over flights in Malaysia actually became the military 
application. 
JM: Yeah, yeah, they actually would…wh en they heard there were guys operating 
a certain area they’d go try to find them so that they could, the Brits could send the SAS 
troops after them.   
SM:  Now do you know if these aircraft were ever used again later for operations, 
trail watch, trying to determine where North Vietnamese enemy concentrations were in 
Laos, things like that? 
JM:  That particular airplane wasn’t.  That was strictly an Air Force airplane that 
was assigned for that one project.  I could tell you that later on one of our pilots landed it 
short at Pitts Camp and wiped the gear out and banged it up, twisted the fuselage, tore a 
wing…really m ade a mess out of it and they sent a small team up there to get ferried and 
they carried it down to Bangkok.  In fact I went up and flew it out of there and brought it 
to Bangkok.  Then the worked on it some more in Bangkok and myself and one of our 
first officers Bill Cruthers ferried it to Tainan.  They were so worried about the gear and 
everything on it they said don’t even land anywhere just fill it up with fuel and go to 
Tainan.  So we flew it non-stop to Tainan.  I never saw it again, that was the end of…it 
was called 8-8-3 was the last three numbers; it was the U.S. Air Force airplane. 
SM:  Okay.  Now were…the tim e that you were flying these flights in Malaysia 
and… W hen you weren’t flying Malayan, where were you? 
JM:  I was in Bangkok flying a 9-3-3 was my primary airplane. 
SM:  Was Sandy there with you at this time? 
JM:  Oh sure, Sandy was in Ban…you know she was in Saigon for a short period 
of time and I sent her to Bangkok.  I was worried they were blowing everything up and

 JM:  Alright Steve.  The Air Force base that I forgot the name of was Khorat and 
that was the base that the F-105s flew out of in Thailand. 
 SM:  Okay.  Now in terms of new information or other things that I wanted to ask 
you concerning your time both in Vietnam when you were flying out of Saigon and other 
areas of Vietnam before you went to Bangkok and also while you were flying in Bangkok 
and perhaps even later when you were flying in Laos more specifically.  Did you provide 
support for Special Forces units in base camps? 
 JM:  Yes we did.  We supplied support in a number of different places both in 
Vietnam and in Laos.  I could get my logbook out and give you a specific date and 
instance, but it would probably have to be after our break because it would take me some 
time to find the logbook.  Where I made an airdrop to Special Forces people on the 
Bolivans Plateau in Laos where they had come under heavy attack by North Vietnamese

made a drop there that was very exciting!  It looked like World War Three going on 
down there.  I can give you the exact date and place, but those were Special Forces 
people who were training troops at what we call the PS strips in Southern Laos on the 
Bolivans Plateau.   
 SM:  Okay.  You said you also did supply some support to Special Forces in 
Vietnam when you [?]? 
 JM:  Well yes they…I think I m entioned that we flew some projects out of 
Kontum.  One of them was the landing strip northwest of Kontum that supplied a Special 
Forces camp and that camp was located several clicks away from the airport and the 
airport as I recall we had to land to the west and take off to the east because of the 
mountains.  I remember one occasion where they always radioed ahead to make sure that 
the Special Forces were there to secure the strip because there was no security at the strip 
it was just a strip with no facilities on it.  In one particular flight we went in and landed in 
the C-46.  Once you setup your approach you’re more or less committed when you were 
loaded.  We landed and pulled into the parking area and there was nobody there!  The 
Special Forces were detained for some reason in leaving, but shortly after we open the 
cargo do we receive some small arms fire from probably 50 to 100 yards away up the 
side of a mountain in some real dense jungle.  We received two or three holes in the top 
of the airplane, in fact three to be exact.  They were shooting from above us and the 
rounds came down through the top and into the cargo.  About that time the Special Forces 
showed up.  I did the only time I ever used a weapon in my years in Vietnam I…we were 
issued uzis to carry in the cockpit in case we did come under fire and I had crawled up in 
the cockpit and assembled our Uzi and put a magazine in it and I had fired one magazine 
out over the wing hatch up in the general direction that the rounds were coming from.  
After that there was no more firing!  I don’t know if I hit anybody or just scared them off! 
 SM:  Wow!  Now I was under the impression that as non-combatants because you 
guys were supposed to be civilian pilots that you weren’t supposed to be armed. 
 JM:  Well you know if you’ll recall in the early days of Vietnam, I think it was in 
1965, we had an airplane shot down in the delta.  The captain was Bill Pruner he had a 
Chinese first officer and a Vietnamese kicker.  At that time the company was really

adamant that we weren’t supposed to have any weapons with us, we were supposed to be 
non-combatant civilians and as such we would be a threat, we wouldn’t be threatened.  
Well that particular flight proved otherwise because they made an emergency landing 
into a rice paddy in the delta and the Viet Cong came out of the bushes and executed 
them in the cockpit.  Bill Pruner had been wounded and had radioed to an airplane 
overhead that he had been wounded and the airplane saw the people coming onto the 
airplane and they called in helicopters to try to get there.  One of our Hueys in their 
attempt to rescue them received ground fire and the first officer on that flight a fellow 
named Janick was shot through the wrist and it took quite a chunk of nerve out and as a 
result he lost the use of that hand.  During that fiasco they decided ‘well, you know…’ 
we were at risk not having weapons and so they issued us Uzis, the Israeli made fully 
automatic submachine gun type.  The nice thing about them they folded down into a 
compact little case and they gave us two magazines of ammunition I believe; thirty 
rounds in each magazine so I believe that’s sixty rounds.  We could draw those along 
with our survival equipment when we flew and I always drew one and had it in the 
cockpit.  Like I say all the years I carried it I only used it one time and that was on that 
particular flight re-supplying a Special Forces camp. 
 SM:  How about personal weapons, was that permissible? 
 JM:  Oh I carried a 45 in my flight kit all through Saigon and all through Bangkok 
and all through Laos. 
 SM:  Was that common amongst the pilots? 
 JM:  Yes I believe you could say that was the standard operating procedure, I 
think almost everybody had a weapon somewhere on them.  If for no other reason, if you 
went down you needed a weapon for survival. 
 SM:  There were more hazards then just the enemy. 
 JM:  Yes.  I’d feel a lot more comfortable having a weapon on me down in the 
jungle if I was jus trying to stay away not only from the enemy, but from some of the 
animals that inhabit the area.  
 SM:  Yeah.  Did you have any run-ins with any of the wildlife? 
 JM:  No…Well I’ll back up.  I liv ed in the setup Palace Hotel in Vientiane Laos 
when I first moved up there, it’s the better part of I’d say six months.  We had a parking

 JM:  We had another incident of a Krait that was rather humorous at the Pepper 
Grinder in Udorn where we loaded ordinance onto the 123.  One time George Ritter my 
best friend over there had a load of pallets put on the airplane and they were laying along 
side of the ramp in a grassy area and evidently this Krait had gotten up inside this pallet 
and when they put it on the airplane during the take off the vibration this Krait came out 
and scared the devil...the kickers in the back saw him and they were beating and 
screaming and he kept disappearing and they didn’t know where the snake was in the 
airplane.  It scared all of them.  So Ritter wrote up a Captain’s report to be careful when 
you’re loading things out of Pepper Grinder and he missed spelt Kraits.  Then all the 
wags added onto it on the bulletin board and scratched it out and spelled Krait with a K 
and a whole lot of other things.  Finally the last one was “Ritter’s Critter.”  (laughing) 
 SM:  (laughing) That’s funny!  Why was that called the Pepper Grinder? 
 JM:  That was just the name that the… It was one by the U.S. Ar my and the 
reason I guess is that the airborne troops rigged the parachutes there and put them on the 
loads when they loaded them on the airplane.  The Army gave it the name Pepper 
Grinder.  
 SM:  Hum, okay.  Well, how about other incidences involving wildlife that was 
not necessarily in a jungle environment like perhaps a more domesticated environment? 
 JM:  Well I think just about everybody, well not everybody, but the families 
anyway had pets.  I remember the Bob Hardels had some little tiny deer that were native 
up there like the key deer.  They’re only about, oh gosh, they were as small as dog; they 
had a pair of those for pets.  Oh God, many of the families had gibbons, we had a couple

of gibbons.  We had one pilot, Bill Curuthers, who was a PHD in entomology that loved 
animals and he had pet snakes and sea otters, he had a pair of sea otters, he had gibbons, 
he had a pet tarantula that was given to him by the wing commander Summners out of 
Bangkok.  He had all kinds of weird creatures that he kept at his place.  They called it the 
zoo in Vientiane.    
 SM:  Okay.  Well I understand you guys had a large cat too at one time. 
 JM:  We had a Malaysian golden cat, yes.  I had met a customer in the Nakhon Se 
Tamirat in southern Thailand that had had this cat…had a Malaysia n cat for I think he 
had the cat nine years.  It was a beautiful animal and it was as friendly…I m ean I know 
it’s a dangerous animal there, anytime you get a cat… this was m ale probably weighed 
slightly over 100 pounds.  A cat that big can be very dangerous, but that cat played with 
his kids, played with his dogs, it would come when it was called, it just a great pet.  I said 
‘Gah, I’d like to have one of those.  I like cats.’  He said he’d check the market and try to 
find one for me.  Well months went by and he couldn’t…he also warned me at that time, 
he said, “You want a male they’re not as high strung as the female.”  He couldn’t find a 
male, but he did find a female kitten and he finally told me it was there.  I picked it up 
one of the runs south and we kept the cat for a long time until we left Thailand in fact.  It 
turned out to be not like his, it wasn’t that friendly, but as long as you were careful 
around it and didn’t startle it you could pet it.  It would play with our dog, it was very 
friendly with the dog.  It did bite me one time, but it was my fault.  It came up and sat by 
the chair and I dropped the paper on it relaxing and just let my arm fall down and he bite 
me in the hand, which was another adventure in that it punctured my hand, the fang went 
all the way through between my thumb and forefinger.  My wife and kids were trying to 
pry its mouth open to get it out of my hand, but… Like I say he wasn’t attacking me I just 
startled him.  But anyway the next day I had a VIP flight to Kuala Lumpur and being that 
was the VIP flight, we had two captains to fly it and we also had a third captain that the 
chief pilot Ed Orlick, rode along with the VIPs in the back.  Going down I started getting 
sick, I started a headache and fever and by the time I got to Kuala Lumpur I was quite ill.  
I asked the desk clerk if he could recommend a doctor, where I could find a doctor.  He 
drew me a map where to find this doctor few blocks from the hotel and I walked down 
these back streets of [?] and found it.  It looked closer to a shack or a shanty then a 
  48 

--- PDF PAGE 49 ---
 
doctor’s office.  I was a little dismayed in when I walked in the front office…before I got 
into the office there was kind of a waiting room you might say and had shelves of bottles 
of snakes and lizards and all kinds of things in these jars, which kind of disturbed me.  I 
thought ‘what have I got here a witch doctor?’  When I met the doctor he was a very 
elderly Chinese gentlemen and he spoke English, that helped.  I went in to his 
innersanctum and I told him that I’d been bitten by this cat and he said, “Oh you have cat 
scratch fever.”  Well I’d never heard of cat scratch fever and I thought it was something 
you know…a m ythical disease.  He opened a drawer with loose pills in it and he took two 
or three different colored pills out, put them in a napkin, wrapped them up for me.  He 
said he had to give me a shot, he had a kerosene with him and I can remember the pop, 
the enamel all chipped off of it and dirty.  He dropped the syringe down in this pot and it 
didn’t even cover the syringe. The water ran inside it.  He heated it up and he pulled a 
little vial of something out that had aluminum foil over it so it looked like it might have 
been sterile…and he gave m e a shot.  He told me that I’d be alright by the next day so I 
went back to my hotel, but the fever was so high.  My teeth were chattering, I was 
freezing and so I turned the air conditioning off in the room and I got all… there was two 
twin beds in it, I took all the covers off one bed and put them on the other and got 
underneath and went out.  I think passed out might have been a better word for it.  Later 
that evening Ed Orlick and and the co-captain Verge Domino…we were all going to have 
dinner together and they knocked on the door and I didn’t answer.  They asked the room 
boy to open the door to see if there was something wrong and they opened the door and 
they said it was like a blast furnace in there.  I was being closed up in the heat of the day, 
it was about 110 down there and I was out of it, they said I was unconscious.  They 
couldn’t make any sense out of me at all so they called the British military hospital and 
they sent an ambulance and took me to the British military hospital and they got a doctor 
out of a party.  He was in his tuxedo when I saw him.  They’d gotten me cooled down 
and I was semi-conscious anyway.  I could talk and I told the doctor about this visit to the 
doctor and he said, “Oh God!  At the very least you’ve probably got Hepatitis.”  But he 
said, “I think the doctor was right in this diagnosis, I think it’s catch scratch fever.”  He 
said, “We can control that.”  So he gave me some more shots, kept me in the hospital

 SM:  How long did it take you to recover fully? 
 JM:  Oh, I’d say a week before I was really fully recovered from it. 
 SM:  Wow!  Did you continue to take medication or was it just the shots 
themselves? 
 JM:  Oh yes.  They gave me a prescription to last, you know like probably 
antibiotics that I took for about ten days, but I never had any ill effect that I know from 
that except the scar on my hand when the cat bit me. 
 SM:  Did you Contract Hepatitis from that dirty needle? 
 JM:  No.  You know every time I got sick after that I kept thinking ‘Here comes 
the Hepatitis!’  However my liver has always showed up that there was something wrong 
with it.  Even today, I just came from a doctor’s appointment and the doctor said, “You 
know, your liver isn’t functioning quite properly and it looks like…Have you ever had 
Hepatitis?”  And I told her about that instance.  I said, “To my knowledge I don’t believe 
I have.”   
 SM:  That’s really interesting.  How about malaria or any other tropical diseases? 
 JM:  Oh I did Contract malaria, but that came from Central America not from 
Vietnam, not from Laos. 
 SM:  Oh.  Did any other strange or exotic diseases? 
 JM:  Yes.  Both myself and my fine family came down with Dengue Fever in 
Thailand.  My wife had an episode eating seafood where she was poisoned and she swoll 
up to where she couldn’t breath through her throat and her eyes were swollen shut.  She 
was a mess.  That was all in Thailand! 
 SM:  What did you do? 
 JM:  Well we took her to the hospital and they gave her some shots and they told 
her that the fish hadn’t been cleaned properly. 
 SM:  Sounds like she went into anaphylactic shock. 
 JM:  Oh it was bad.  She’s not allergic to seafood so it was obviously something 
else.  Quite a reaction. 
 SM:  Yeah.  How long did it take you all to recover from Dengue?

 JM:  You know I really can’t remember.  I know it laid us low for quite a while; it

 SM:  Well, I would assume that that was pretty common for most the people 
to… ? 
 JM:  Yeah, I think it was all common over there.  Oh I did go through…I think the 
whole family went through a couple of spouts of amebic dysentery also.  Back in the old 
days they had a grounded cure that was arsenic.  They’d give you some low level doses 
of arsenic to kill the ameba.  Of course that stuff is cumulative so after two or three doses 
of that they can’t give you anymore of that.  
 SM:  Right.  Otherwise you’d die. 
 JM:  Yeah.  But they did come up with a non-grounding medicine for ameba 
while we were over there.  I know the last time I went through it it was non-grounding.  I 
could take the medication and fly.   
 SM:  Okay.  I take it you guys would mix eating on the economy and eating in…?  
 JM:  Oh yes we ate on the economy or ate at home, the only two places.   
 SM:  Was that also common or was there anything in the compound, the Air 
America compound a place where you could eat? 
 JM:  Well yeah, we had a restaurant at the airport you know where we could eat, 
but the only time we’d eat there is if I was there in between, you know at lunchtime 
waiting for an airplane to be loaded and I’d have lunch there.  Quite frequently I’d have 
breakfast there with an early morning flight.  But other then using it as a convenience 
when I was at the airport we didn’t use it. 
 SM:  Okay.  Was that westernized food in the airport or was that…?  
 JM:  Ah yes.  They had an excellent restaurant in Udorn, better then in Vientiane.  
When I RON’d in Udorn of course I’d eat three meals a day there if I was there during 
the middle of the day anyway.  Food was excellent, they called it the Rendezvous’ Club 
and it was very good. 
 SM:  The Rendezvous Club? 
 JM:  Yes. 
 SM:  Where there any other clubs that you would frequent, especially in 
Vientiane?

you… The Settha Palace I understood  it fallen in complete disrepair.  It was an old 
French hotel when we were there, but I had heard that it has since then completely 
renovated and is now a popular tourist hotel and has its own website on the web!   
 SM:  Okay.  Is this the hotel that…yeah the hotel that had been taken over by I 
guess some Indians, some Hindus? 
 JM:  I don’t know.  I don’t know who has taken it over, but it…It wasn’t, even 
when we were there, it wasn’t the biggest hotel in town or the newest or the best, but it 
had a certain quaint charm about it that I always liked. 
 SM:  It was popular. 
 JM:  Yeah, it was very popular with the pilots.

drinking, but more importantly because of cosmopolitan aspects of some…I understand 
that in some bars like the Purple Porpoise it was an attraction for a lot of different groups 
of people. 
 JM:  Oh yes…

 SM:  I was wondering if you could describe some of the, I guess the groupings 
that would show up at these kinds of bars, the Purple Porpoise and others. 
 JM:  Well you know the community…Vientia ne was kind of like a Casa Blanca 
during World War I it was neutral.  So the Russians had an embassy there, the Chinese 
had an embassy there, the North Vietnamese, the Pathet Lao, and as a result you had all 
different types frequently the bars and the hotels and the restaurants, but there wasn’t any 
terrorism because a terrorist… if he bombed a café he could just as well kill a Russian or a 
Vietnamese as he could an American.  So we were relatively safe within the city and as a 
result you rub shoulders with quite an interesting group.  We had a fellow, in fact I met 
him in California, it was the only time I’ve seen him.  I can’t think of his last name, his 
first name was Al.  He was assigned to the company as a co-pilot in C-46 and I was 
flying the C-46 at the time making rice drops and Al flew with me several times.  We got 
to be pretty good friends.  Al was a Russian by birth and he was a naturalized U.S. 
Citizen, but he spoke fluent Russian.  I didn’t know it at the time, but he was obviously 
working for the agency and Air America was his cover.  But he…agency propriety is a 
cover, it’s kind of loose control.  One time we were at the bar in the Settha Palace, which 
was separate from the restaurant and we were waiting…well it was in the restaurant, but 
it was in the corner of it.  We were waiting for a table to open up and we couldn’t even sit 
all the seats were full and we were standing there.  Al had his back to two gentlemen and 
he obviously was interested in their conversation and I couldn’t hear any of their 
conversation so I didn’t know what language they were speaking.  While we were talking 
all of a sudden Al broke out laughing and these two guys jumped up and turned and 
looked at us and Al said, “Let’s get the hell out of here!”  I left with him.  I said, “What 
the was that all about?”  He said, “They were speaking Russian and they told a very 
funny joke!”  He blew his cover!  (laughing) 
 SM:  Was there…Did you have conversati ons or interactions with any of the 
Russians, North Vietnamese, Pathet Lao…Did you ever… 
 JM:  I didn’t know any personally.  I believe there were some of the pilots that 
did.  I believe Ed Adams knew them, he lived I think very close to the Russian embassy.  
But I didn’t have anyone that I called a friend or acquaintance that were in that 
community.

 SM:  Well maybe not a friend or acquaintance, but maybe just someone that you 
happened to sit next to in a bar and all of a sudden you were talking to them and then you 
learned later that’s who they were? 
 JM:  No.  One time I was introduced to a gentlemen, I didn’t know who he was, 
by a piano player in the Settha Palace.  He said, “This is my friend, he’d like to talk to an 
Air American pilot.”  I said, “Okay.”  So I sat down with him and this gentlemen had an 
accent, but I couldn’t make out what it was.  He was asking me questions, he was 
particularly interested in the Pilatus Porter.  He wanted to know how we mounted the 
guns on them.  I said, “We don’t have guns on our pilatus porters.”  And he says, “Well I 
hear that you have guns.”  “No, no, no, no.  [?] we’re non-combatants.  We don’t have 
guns.”  But he was insisting that these airplanes were used militarily and I kept saying no 
they weren’t.   This gentlemen that played in the band or played the piano was swift, but 
he had a little background on him. He had been caught on some kind of a drug charge in 
Bangkok and the states and got into Laos, which was…without a passport, the Thais had 
his passport.  He was kind of a man without a country.  This Swiss ambassador [?]…  
This was a Swiss ambassador from Thailand that had came up there.  Evidently the Swiss 
had had a complaint that he Swiss Pilatus Porters borders that we were using up there 
were being used militarily and they didn’t want to be selling their airplanes for military 
use.  That was what he was trying to pry out of me, but that’s probably the only time I 
really didn’t know who I was talking to and ran across somebody who wasn’t who he 
seemed. 
 SM:  Yes sir.  What kind of instructions were you given in terms of talking with 
people? 
 JM:  I really was never given any…per sonally.  Maybe some people were, but 
nobody ever took the time to tell me not to talk to anybody.  Oh I mean we were given 
the various thing that what we were doing was nobody’s business and we weren’t going 
to talk to the press of course, but as far as the strike of a conversation with a friend at a 
bar where discretion was always the better…you know, what we were doing m ilitarily 
anyway in supplying arms and ammunition we always kept that to our self. 
 SM:  When you…W ere you ever told that if you were approached by someone 
say like the Swiss Ambassador, if you were approached by someone like that, someone

obviously trying to plug you for information…Were you ever to ld to report that, do be

 JM:  No I can’t say that I was.  Again I don’t know if I missed some special 
indoctrination or not, but I was never given any real specific instructions except use your 
commonsense and keeping what you’re doing to yourself, you’re a U.S. Contract carrier 
flying primarily for USAID or hauling rice and things like that.  Most of us didn’t need to 
be…I think that we kept that pretty much to  our self.  I didn’t see a whole lot come out.  
There were a few a stories in some of the adventure magazines about us hauling arms and 
ammunition during the Vietnam or during the Laotian War in the early days, but it never 
seemed to be real big time in the press that I can recall. 
 SM:  Okay.  Well would you say then that the only time that you were given 
specific instructions in terms of security is if and when you had a special project…?  
 JM:  That’s exactly right.  When we have a special project we were given a 
briefing and then again it’s need to know, we were only briefed on the project as far as 
what we needed to know to fly it.  But we were given a security pledge to sign that we 
would not divulge any information concerning that forever or maybe longer under 
penalty of imprisonment and etcetera.  So if I sign something I naturally was going to be 
very close, tight lip about that.  I wasn’t going to mention it.  If I didn’t I considered 
everything else, you know again commonsense would come in to it.  We went beyond a 
typical USAID charter airline.  I don’t think many charter airlines make air drops of arms 
and ammunition to troops on the field.  We used discretion in that respect. 
 SM:  When you had to sign a security agreement, that was for a specific project in 
a specific mission? 
 JM:  Yes, a specific project in a specific mission. 
 SM:  Okay.  Good enough.  Well what did you do after you finished the Malaysia 
project, the infrared project? 
 JM:  Well we…about the tim e that that was over the company just got the K 
model 123s.  The B models, which were just two R-2800s were replaced with K models, 
which had the two J-85 jets and better brakes.  There were several modifications that 
were made on the aircraft to make them a K.  They were given twelve of them and they 
put a bid open for additional crewmembers.  At that time Ed Orlick had gone to, well

he’d gone several months earlier to Vientiane and Fred Walker had come down to 
Bangkok.  I’d become very good friends with Fred Walker.  I think Fred was…Fred was 
one of the most memorable characters that I met over there and I had a lot of respect for 
him, I liked him I thought he was a real gentlemen and a heck of an aviator.  I did the 123 
project and I turned it into Fred and he said, “Well John you know you’ve got a good job 
here, a comfortable living, and your with your family…W hy do you want to go up there 
for?”  And I said, “Well, I want to fly that airplane and I’d like to get up to Laos, that’s 
where the real action is.”  And he says, “Well, you know your seniority of such there’s no 
question that you’ll be going up there if you take this.”  And I said, “Yeah I would like to 
go.”  Fred tried to talk me out of it but…m yself and George Ritter who’s my best friend 
and he was in Bangkok at the time and Fred Conley all did the program at the same time 
and of course we all went right up to Laos.  We got to Laos…the only IT at that tim e was 
Phil Schneider and Bill Cooper another friend of mine who had stayed in Saigon had did 
the program and he came up from Saigon.  One of the first training flights Bill was flying 
with Schneider and they were making touch and goes at the Vientiane airport and on one 
of the touch and goes they forgot to put the gear down and they touched down on the jack 
pads.  Did minor damage to the airplane, just wore the jack pads down.  They added the 
power and were airborne in an instant.  But of course the company took bending the 
aluminum real seriously and they grounded both Bill and Phil.  That stopped the training 
in the 123 program so they were real short of 46 pilots, a couple of pilots had been on 
home leave and a couple others got sick and they were desperate for 46 pilots.  I had 
flown a 46 in Saigon so they checked me out on air drops on the 46 with a rice drop and I 
started flying 46 until we got a new instructor pilot in the 123. 
 SM:  Did they… They never r einstated the two pilots? 
 JM:  Yeah they reinstated Bill because he was a trainee and they Phil also as a 
line captain, but they didn’t let him instruct anymore.  He went back and just flew the 123 
in the line and after the investigation and the slap on the wrist.  Bill Cooper then went 
back to training and he checked out in the 123.  In fact Bill and I were IPs in the airplane 
starting very early. They made both of us instructor pilots and check airmen in the 123.  
 SM:  Wow!  Okay.  Well were there any particular missions that you recall while 
you were flying…

 JM:  The 123? 
 SM:  The 123 and then the 46? 
 JM:  Well 46s…I have in m y log book an exact date of it, but it went back to the 
Saigon days.  I had a trip in a 46, the weather was bad, it was raining and a lot of 
thunderstorms around.  We had a short flight from Saigon to Da Nang and we were 
carrying almost entirely the trip was made up of medical supplies.  Shortly after takeoff I 
started getting a terrible headache and feeling nauseous and my co-pilot did too, he’s a 
Chinese first officer.  It wasn’t long before my first officer’s losing his breakfast.  We’d 
both eaten breakfast together at the airport in Saigon.  And I thought, “Uh oh, food 
poisoning!”  I radioed the company and told them that both the first officer and I were 
sick.  I opened my window on the left side…i n the 46, boy you really get a gust of air in 
there the way the airplane’s designed.  That cool air rushing in made me feel better 
almost immediately.  I cracked it, you know I didn’t leave it full open very long because 
it’s blowing us away, but I left enough air coming in there that as long as I was breathing 
that fresh air I was feeling alright.  But I was still nauseous and headache and we radioed 
ahead to Da Nang that both the first officer and I were sick and we suspected food 
poisoning and they said they’d have a doctor waiting for us when we landed.  They had 
an American doctor, I don’t know whether he was military or USAID, but when we 
landed the doctor was the first one on board the airplane.  The minute he’d come up the 
ladder, “Oh chri.”  He knew immediately what was wrong.  He said, “Ether!”  The 
airplane reeked of ether.  So they started searching the airplane and obviously there 
was…som e of the loaders that had loaded the airplane were Viet Cong and they had 
opened a can or a box of ether that was an aluminum can.  They’d punched holes in the 
tops of them with screwdrivers and put them in the radio rack of the airplane. 
 SM:  So the heat would… 
 JM:  The heat vaporized that stuff, but it was also the danger of a spark igniting 
and blowing up.  But they made us ill to say the least.   
 SM:  Man you were lucky! 
 JM:  We were lucky, yep, we were.  In Vientiane, I mean the difference in the 
mission between the 46 and Saigon and Vientiane was night and day.  The flying of the 
46 in Vietnam was primarily a USAID mission, point-to-point landings, but we did make

a lot of landings and a lot of short strips and a lot of bad crosswinds along the coast.  So it 
was a real challenge flying in the 46.  In Laos the mission was almost entirely airdrop 
rice, which was a completely different mission but again very, very challenging, very 
exacting.  I enjoyed the missions in both Vientiane and Saigon in the 46, but the 46 to me 
was I believe I might have mentioned it to you before; it was like the era of sailing ships.  
Old sailors say back in the days of wooden ships and iron men and I always thought of 
the 46 as that ear in aviation.  Gig, rugged airplane that you had to fly, but real good on it 
certainly.   
 SM:  Was one mission more challenging then the other, that is flying out of 
Saigon versus Vientiane? 
 JM:  I think that Vientiane was probably a little more challenging.  Getting down 
in some of those narrow valleys and planning these drops, boy you had to be very, very 
exact.  However, short strips and strong crosswinds the 46 is a real challenging airplane 
in that respect too.  They both had their challenges. 
 SM:  Yes sir.  In terms of the rice drops and I assume that we’re talking both hard 
and soft drops? 
 JM:  Well the 46 was almost all regular rice drops.  That’s where they put the one 
sack inside the other sack.  It required a very different technique and it required quite a 
level of skill to really be able to put ever sack on the DZ you had to pay very close 
attention to what you were doing.   
 SM:  Was there an altitude window that you had to keep in? 
 JM:  Oh yes there was a definite…T he idea was that the rice had to be free falling, 
it had to be falling with no forward motion.  In other words reach terminal velocity where 
it’s falling straight and flat.  If it had any forward motion it would hit the ground and 
tumble and then of course scatter rice.  The only time you could drop low was if you 
were dropping onto the side of a hill and they had some DZs that way.  Then you could 
come in lower and just plant it into the side of the hill and it wouldn’t roll, but most of the 
DZs were flat and therefore you had to be a very…You had to be where the rice was 
falling free and that was about 800 feet, 800 to 1000 feet was a good altitude above the 
terrain.  Then you wanted the airplane as slow as possible.  I would slow the airplane to 
about say 105 knots and that’s pretty dang slow.  You would lose your…as the airplane

SM:  This is Steve Maxner continuing the interview with Mr. John McRainey on 
the 19th of June 2001 at eleven twenty Lubbock time.  I’m in Lubbock Texas and Mr. 
McRainey is in McAllen Texas and we are continuing the interview on CD number two.  
Alright sir why don’t you go ahead and provide that additional information concerning 
those events that we just previously discussed. 
JM:  Okay.  On the incident where the ether was put in the radio rack of the C-46, 
I looked up in my logbook that took place on August 24, 1966 in Chinese registered C-46 
Bravo 9-1-0.  That was the trip between Saigon and Nha Trang.  Then I mentioned when 
you asked about…and heck I had it in m y logbook right here, I lost the page, I’ll look it 
up, but you mention about did we ever work with the Special Forces re-supply Special 
Forces and I mention one instance where we drop the ammunition to Special Forces that 
were being overrun.  I had the dad gung thing out here and now the page has flipped 
away from me…but I norm ally didn’t make very many notations in my logbook…here it 
is…unless there was som ething really out of the ordinary.  Like getting shot at was fairly 
routine unless it was out of the ordinary where they actually hit us or something, but on 
this one I made the notation heavy fire and as I recall it did look like World War III.  
They had called in Spectre gun ships, the C-130s with the mini guns, and there were A-
1Es and F-4s and everything you could think off and they’re trying to beat back the North 
Vietnamese were overrunning the strip and of course Special Forces guys couldn’t even 
fight their way out because they were out of ammunition.  We made the drop right on 
their little compound.  The whole strip had been overrun except for the landing or the 
parking pad, a small part of that, as I recall it was the northwest part of the strip.  We put

the load in there and they managed to get their ammunition together and fight their way 
out to the west towards Pax Khe, but that was on March 8

SM:  Was it typical for you to get shot at? 
JM:  Well yeah, well you wouldn’t say it was typical, but it happened.  When we 
were re-supplying places that were under siege…you know the cam e they played were 
during one season the North Vietnamese would push and then during the next season the 
Hmong would make their push.  So there were offenses and defenses and back and forth 
depending on the season and when, as long as Vang Pao’s troops were supplied they 
would hold the position.  They’d be on these mountaintops dug in real good and if we’d 
get food and ammunition to them boy they’d stay there.  But of course the North 
Vietnamese at the Pathet Lao would surround their position and of course their game 
would to be fight off or shoot at all the airplanes that came in there and hopefully keep 
them from re-supplying them so they could overrun them.  So during the seasons that 
those various places were being surrounded we could expect ground fire every time that 
we went in and they normally didn’t disappoint us, but that was one of the reasons I 
really liked the 123, we considered it the closes thing to a flying tank that we had over 
there.  We had armor plating in the floor of the airplane and we had…som e of the 
airplanes even had the side armor where you could, it was kind of a foam type plate that 
was put along the side of a seat where if you sat back in it they wouldn’t even get you 
from the side.   
SM:  Wow!  Was this some kind of a think…?  
JM:  It was about a three-inch thick foam mat as I recall. 
SM:  It would absorb the shock. 
JM:  It would absorb the rounds and the shrapnel if there was shrapnel. 
SM:  Wow! 
JM:  The 123 had…the fuel system  was designed, the main tanks were in the back 
of them as cells, a big, big bathtub like tank held over 700 gallons of fuel, and they were 
on bomb shackels where if you took a hit that was so bad that the tank caught on fire,

fuel tank, go to cross-feed first of course, and then drop the fuel tank and fly on the other

(laughing) 
SM:  How well would it glide though? 
JM:  I wouldn’t recommend that. 
SM:  No, but it did have glide capabilities? 
JM:  Yes it did.  The airplane was designed to be shot at, it was called the 
Provider and it was built by the Air Force to be an airplane that would re-supply troops 
and get shot at, but that would take an awful lot of ground fire to bring it down and it did.  
I was…in looking through m y logbooks I came across another, like I say I didn’t make 
many notations unless it was really out of the ordinary, but this one I did make a notation.  
It said return with load due MiG, and this was on January 15th 1972 and I was making a 
drop up in the northeastern part of Laos, very close to North Vietnam and these radar 
picket ships that monitored what they call “the Barrel”.  They called it “Barrel Roll”, 
which was Lao and the Air Force flew these airplanes at high altitude in a circle over 
Laos monitoring aircraft movements.  You know like if the North Vietnamese turned off 
they’d have them on radar immediately.  They call these airplanes, they had different call 
signs, but it seems to me that during the day it was Cricket, was the aircraft that we 
communicated with.  They would give you all kinds of advisories about, particularly 
enemy air action and they would coordinate search and rescue and what have you.  Well 
on this day my first officer was flying and Van Ingen and I were way up and we had a 
solid under cast underneath us.  We were looking for a hole so that we could get down 
underneath and get into our DZ, when Cricket called us and said, “Aircraft in…” and I 
can’t remember what area it was in, but it was well north of the PDJ and it was us 
obviously and we called back and gave them our call sign…let’s see what I was that 
day…I was flying 54-556, that would have been 556 and they said that a MiG, that three 
MiGs had just departed Vinh and they appeared that they were on an intercept for me!

Of course the North Vietnamese radar had painted us up in that area.  So I said, “Well 
I’m going to head south, see if they change course.”  I turn 180 and headed south and 
sure enough, they said, “Yes!  The MiGs have made an intercept on you and now they’re 
coming at us and we’re heading south!” 
SM:  Whoa! 
JM:  So they left the barrel and told me, “You’re on your own!”  All of us said, 
“Oh my god!”  those MiGs had taken off from Vinh they couldn’t have been more then 
seventy or eighty miles away, they’re going to be there in a heartbeat.  So we 
immediately put on our parachute, which we normally didn’t, well the kickers wore 
there’s back there but the pilots normally didn’t wear there’s unless they got into 
something like this.  We all put on our parachutes and we lit the jet and went to 100% on 
the jets and we went up to medo power on the recips to go as fast as we could and we 
headed west away from Vinh and we decided after a minute or two of that…here we are 
on top of a solid under cast and it’s a clear day out there and of course we knew the MiGs 
didn’t have radars themselves, but they were relying on the ground radar to fector them 
into us.  I said, “Those turkeys you know, they’re going to be on us in a heartbeat and of 
course we’re a sitting duck.”  So we have not choice, we got to get down in that under 
cast, the tops were about 800 feet.  We didn’t want to get down so low we were going to 
run into the side of a mountain, but at that time getting shot down was inevitable if we 
didn’t disappear.  So we went like a submarine and descended into the under cast into the 
clouds and I of course pulled the power back on the jets so that we weren’t going too fast 
underneath there and we stayed as close to the surface as we could, we didn’t want the 
fin, the ruder to be sticking up in the air to attract the MiG, but we wanted to be out of 
site.  Of course we’re sweating blood that we were down there in terrain that we were 
scrambling for the maps to see just how high the terrain was, but it was too close for 
comfort, but there was not alternative.  By then of course Cricket had left the area and we 
didn’t have any of their advice as to when to make the MiGs broke it off, but we stayed 
down there as long as we absolutely dared and then we popped up for a peek and there 
was nobody around so we headed south and back to Vientiane.   
SM:  Wow!  Did you ever encounter MiGs at all?

JM:  That was the only occasion that we had.  Now we never saw them, but we

us when we went down in the clouds. 
SM:  Had anybody else that flew for Air America or any of the other charter 
services out of Southeast Asia, do you know if they ever encountered MiGs? 
JM:  I don’t know you know.  I don’t recall of any stories, but I’d certainly be the 
last one to say that they didn’t because a lot of our missions were way up on the China-
Lao and on the Vietnamese-Lao border and they certainly could’ve encountered them.  I 
know that a lot of the rescue missions that our helicopters made way up there they used to 
have US fighter escort, what they’d call CAP, a MiG-CAP in case the MiGs came they’d 
have some help. 
SM:  Right.  Okay.  Just out of curiosity, you mentioned going up near the 
Chinese border, do you know if you guys ever provided support to nationalist Chinese 
forces over the border or even in Laos? 
JM:  Nationalist Chinese in Laos? 
SM:  Yeah, Northern Laos or just across the border into china? 
JM:  I don’t know…whe n you say nationalists you mean like Taiwanese forces 
over there? 
SM:  Well apparently there were some nationalist Chinese that when the 
Communist took over back in ’49 they went west and of course stayed there in the border 
area to harass the Communists Chinese. 
JM:  Yeah, I believe that most of those wound up in Burma and they had been re-
supplied back in the early days by Cat, but by the time we were over there I think they 
were considered to be drug smugglers and unfriendly.  I don’t think we were giving them 
a whole lot of help.  I had flown real close to the Chinese border dropping leaflets from 
time to time up in Yunnan province, but my only encounters…I encountered Chinese 
guns I know, but that was on the road that the Chinese were building from Yunnan 
province down to the Me Kong river.  We called it the Chinese road and the Chinese 
made an autonomous area about fifteen miles on either side of that road and they shot at 
anybody that came in that area, including the Royal Lao Air Fore or the Royal Lao 
government.  We had one occasion, you know Jim Ryan our chief pilot flying over that 
  63 

--- PDF PAGE 64 ---
 
road got his leg blown off…Oh, about a week  prior to that, maybe two, well not over a 
week prior to that, a fellow named Ray Selucy and myself were on a search and rescue 
for George Ritter, my best friend that disappeared on a flight to Lima site 69 alternate.  
We had done a little triangulation with taking the winds into effect and felt that he might 
have been up in the area of Pak Bing, which was were the Chinese had an airport and a 
large military encampment and big, big radar controlled guns.  I heard that the guns were 
85 millimeter and I had other people tell me they were 100 millimeter, but I can tell you 
first hand having seen them up close and personal they were very, very big.  Ray and I 
thought that if George was as far off course as he could’ve been, with the real strong 
winds out of the south they had that day we knew after the fact, he could have wound up 
near that area and there was broken clouds and he could’ve seen that Chinese runway, 
which was aligned pretty much like the runway he was going to at 69 alternate and it was 
a laterite dirt strip, which would have looked the same, and it was in a valley with 
mountains on both sides, which would have been pretty much the same, and we thought 
that they might of landed on that strip and they could’ve pushed the airplane back in the 
tree line, thrown a camouflage net over it and nobody would’ve ever spotted it on search 
and rescue.  Number one because they couldn’t go over that road to see it and we had had 
several days of extensive search, a lot of airplanes hit by ground fire all up and down that 
road, just outside the Atmois area and we hadn’t found a trace.  We’d checked out every 
single fire and we felt the airplane would burn because it was carrying white phosphorous 
106 recoilless rifle rounds and there should’ve been a heck of a fire if it’d crashed, but we 
couldn’t find one out there.  So we thought that ‘well maybe he did land there and he’s a 
guest of the Chinese, and we devised what we thought was a good plan.  He was flying a 
twin Otter at the time, and the twin Otter was a very nimble little airplane and fairly 
quiet, and we thought we’d get down behind the mountains on the east side of the road 
and do pass right over the airport, break out into the valley, over the airport, and out the 
westside before they even knew what was going on.  We had two kickers, well actually 
we had a flying safety guy, named Anthony, I believe Tony Anthony, and we had a 
kicker, I can’t remember his name, that had volunteered to go along, and they had 
binoculars.  I was sitting in the cockpit in the right front seat, we didn’t use the co-pilot in 
the Otter, and Ray was flying.  We let down behind this mountain and came in over the

road and when we broke out into the open those big guns had us boresighted like ducks in 
a shooting gallery.  When they opened up on us we forgot completely about looking for 
George’s airplane or anything else, but…The first thing we saw, and the kicker in the 
back said, “This truck’s coming down the road, you can see the dust.”  When you look 
through your binoculars all your peripheral vision’s gone and I took the binoculars down 
and I saw the columns of dust straight up in there air and I said, “That does not compute, 
if it was a truck it would be horizontal it wouldn’t be vertical!”  and then I could see it 
was these huge guns, it was a recoil from the guns blowing dust up, and then with the 
binoculars down you could see these big red rounds that looked like telephone poles 
coming up at us and we were real close…the minute that he saw them shooting at us Ray 
turned away from them, but now we were ninety degrees to them and just going from left 
to right and close to the side of the mountain.  The vegetation ran right up the side of 
those mountains right to the top, heavy vegetation, and the rounds were impacting into 
the side of the mountain and blowing tree trunks and rocks out, it was surreal.  Of course 
Ray was jiggin’ and jaggin,’ he was up and down and right and left trying to be as hard a 
target as possible.  I think the only thing that saved us, outside of dumb luck, was the fact 
that right up against that terrain I know those big guns get what they call proximity fuses, 
where when it’s in the proximity in the airplane it will go off and the shrapnel and the 
blast will get you even if it isn’t a direct hit, and those rounds were very close to us, but 
they weren’t going off.  Obviously the proximity fuses when you’re shooting into 
something dense like the side of a mountain, don’t work!  Very, very exciting moment or 
two while we were getting out of there. 
SM:  How much of a hazard were the tree trunks and debris? 
JM:  Oh they were all over the place.  I mean how we came out of it without 
getting shot down or knocked down by debris was a miracle.  We all felt like we’d grown 
a beard in the two or three minutes that we were under fire there.  I know that I didn’t see 
Ray for about ten years after that. There was a reunion about ten years afterwards, 
somewhere in California, and I had just had a real exciting experience in Mexico.  I had 
crashed and burned in a Lockheed Lodestar and managed to walk away from the crash 
and I thought that was probably the most traumatic experience I’d ever had in my life.  
Old Ray saw me and in the hospitality suite, “John!”  He said, “I bet you haven’t had as

SM:  So had you had a scare that rivaled that? 
JM:  Yeah the crash into the side of a mountain in a Lockheed Lodestar was pretty 
scary. (laughing) 
SM:  (laughing) Okay.  Where did that happen? 
JM:  Now that happened down in Mexico.  It was in the Veracruz area, south, and 
the west of Veracruz.   
SM:  Was this your own personal work or was this…?  
JM:  No.  It was an airplane that, I had trained the young man in the airplane, a 
fellow named John Willard, and Willard was an excellent pilot but he was totally 
unfamiliar with big airplanes, he’d been flying little airplanes mostly.  He bought this 
Lodestar and after we’d done some preliminary local training he wanted me to make an 
actual trip down to Mexico with him on it and he said he knew where the strip was and he 
was going to land on it, they would have the lights on it.  I knew John was a good pilot, 
I’d flown with him before, and so I was in the right seat and when we lined up on the 
strip I never saw the strip, and the weather wasn’t all that good, but I never saw the lights 
that he said he saw and we kept getting lower and slower and I kept telling him he’s too 
slow and I was bumping the throttles up and then finally he said, “No that’s not it!” and 
he started a steep turn because we were flying into high terrain, the minute he started the 
turn we hit a power, the airplane started to snap on a power on stall and the only thing 
you can do is lower the nose, which is bad when you’re flying in the high terrain, add the 
power, which I did, he was looking out the window, a look saying ‘help me’ and about 
the time the airplane was level and the vertical decent had been slowed to probably, I 
don’t know how fast we were going down, we were still going down mighty fast.  We hit 
flat in the side of the mountain and a little open clearing and of course the airplane 
disintegrated, but we both got out of it with minor injuries and it burned!  But it was a 
very exciting day. 
SM:  That is lucky!  How in the world did you get out of that? 
JM:  Well I crawled out the cockpit windows, the windows, both windows were 
broken out, the windshield was broken out and the side windows were broken out.  The

From there called the people that we were coming down to see and they got us back out

JM:  I guess that’s about as close to dying as I’ve ever come!

JM:  Well I won’t bore with any thing like that. 
SM:  They’re not boring, they’re quite fascinating, you’ve been very lucky.

group in Hawaii, it’s run by the Army, that has been looking for remains of U.S. service 
men, it’s also been helpful in finding our crashes that we weren’t able to recover the 
bodies of.  Several years ago, when I was still with AMR, I got a call from…oh gosh I 
can’t think of the gentleman’s name who was in charge of office out in Honolulu, and he 
said that they had a tentative crash site and he wanted to know what I…he knew that I 
was involved in search and rescue, he’d be talking to George’s son, Philip who was 
president of one of the MIA POW groups, and Philip had told him that I had sent an 
awful a lot of time researching it and I’d spent a lot of time in the search and rescue, and

he asked me where I thought the airplane would be and I gave him a five mile circle 
around Pak Bing is where I thought it would be.  They said they thought they had a 
tentative crash site and as soon as they could get permission from the Lao government 
they were going to go in and excavate it.  Well about two years ago, they called again, 
it’s probably been two and a half or three years ago.  No, two and half because I was 
president of the Air America Association and he called me and said that they had gotten 
in and he gave me the exact coordinates and wanted me to put them on the map and see if 
I thought they matched and of course they did.  They had gone in and done a preliminary 
excavation, and they had found a crash site, they couldn’t identify the aircraft, but the 
cargo was, they found some 105 recoilless rifle rounds.  Well I called them 106, that’s 
what we called them, but they are the same rounds.  A 105 round is used in the 106 
recoiless rifle, which is a 105 rifle with the fifty caliber-spotting rifle on top of it that has 
the same trajectory as the big round.  So they fire a small round and if it’s on track then 
they fire the big round behind it, but the fact that he found a crash site with 105 rounds 
I’m fairly certain that he’s found the crash site.  But then they told us that now the had the 
preliminary and that that was it, before they could go in with the forensic people and 
really excavate the site it would be about two years, that they were about two years ahead 
of the forensic groups.  So I was say right about now they should be in there doing a 
forensic investigation on that crash site.  Hopefully they’ll find some remains because I 
know Barbara, his wife, and Brett and Phillip and George Jr., and Vicki the daughter, 
have never found out what happened to their dad.   
SM:  You don’t remember the person at the joint task force accounting that was 
handling the case? 
JM:  Hold on…let m e ask Sandy.  Gosh I should remember his name, he was so 
helpful and I talked to him a dozen times. 
SM:  Do you know if it was… 
JM:  Pardon?

JM:  Oh gosh yeah, without question, you can’t move that kind of gun around in 
the jungle you know.  That is a big heavy gun, it takes a railroad car almost to move that 
thing.  The heaviest thing that I encountered, you know dropping at the sites up country 
and out, was occasionally they’d move a 37-millimeter out, and that’s big and bad 
enough, but it’s a long way from an 85 or a 100-millimeter.   
SM:  You mentioned that, of course a lot of the military activity that occurred in 
Laos was depending upon the seasons, and I just wanted to confirm the seasons in terms 
of what was the wet season, dry season, smoky season. 
JM:  Yes.   
SM:  Were there particular seasons that were capitalized upon by the Pathet Lao 
versus the Lao under Vang Pao, did they, one or the other group usually capitalize on one 
particular person? 
JM:  Oh yeah, well they liked to move in the dry season because they’re 
equipment wouldn’t bog down, so they’d make they’re big offenses in the dry season. 
SM:  Both sides? 
JM:  Yeah, well we had the advantage in air support so we could move in the 
rainy season when they had a harder time moving.   
SM:  So the enemy would move more in the dry season? 
JM:  I think they moved more in the dry then they did the rainy, yeah.  The rainy 
season presented a lot of problems, although there were offenses on both sides at any

SM:  Okay.  How about the smoky season, in fact both the support and also the 
operations and the offensives or defenses? 
JM:  Well the smoky season, if you’re on the ground you know your visibility was 
limited even on the ground, but it wasn’t as bad as being in the air, it was like flying in a 
bottle of milk.  Our visibility, forward visibility would be a half a mile or less in some 
cases, but we had some slant visibility, you know when you’re looking down and forward 
you might be able to pick out a ridge line three quarters a mile or a mile away, but 
straight down you could see the ground fairly well.  So that gave us, again it was like 
looking through binoculars all the time because we didn’t have any peripheral vision, and 
we had to know that countryside very, very well because we had to be able to look 
straight down and pick something out that we could positively identify and then let down 
off of that you know on a heading that we knew was terrain that we were going to run 
into anything on, until we could work our way down to our DZs or our landing strips, and 
it presented a real challenge.  Of course we had a lot of accidents because of that, we had 
a lot of operational deaths flying into the side of mountains and doing all sorts of bad 
things because the visibility was so restricted.  But I mean, you can’t quit flying if people, 
if they’re out of ammunition or they’re out of food, you know you’ve got to get it in.  We 
had a lot of peer pressure, we knew if one guy wouldn’t do it that the next one would, so 
almost everybody did otherwise they couldn’t face their friends that night when they 
bellied up for the bar at happy hour.  There was a lot of peer pressure.  You knew you 
were flying with some really first class pilots out there and you wouldn’t admit it to the 
world or even to yourself that you couldn’t do anything they did.   
SM:  Was there also a sense of you had to accomplish the mission because if you 
didn’t the guys on the ground [?]? 
JM:  Oh yeah, naturally you’re always concerned with that poor son of a gun on 
the ground.   
SM:  Yeah, you get to belly up at the bar afterwards, they’re still there. 
JM:  Yeah, you couldn’t live with yourself if you just turned around and didn’t try 
really, really hard.

officers? 
JM:  Oh I think very early.  It didn’t take a rocket scientist to figure that out.   
SM:  Okay.  Did you ever, I understand that there were some times when, and I’m 
not trying to bring up anything negative, but I mean the thing is is at times there might 
have been conflicting interests in terms of what the customer wanted, what the customer 
thought he needed, and what the pilot could provide safely.  Did you ever encounter…?  
JM:  Well yeah, there was.  You know old Glearum on the History Channel tape 
handled that very well and it was exactly the way I felt.  I never had a customer push me 
into something I didn’t want to do, in fact the ones that I worked with I found they 
always told me where the bad guys were, gave me all the advice they could, and then said 
you know, here’s what we want to do, we want to get this load in there and how would 
you suggest we go about it.  That’s generally the way they would handle it.  I never had 
one of them try to tell me how to fly my airplane in there.  I’ve had them ride with me.  I 
remember one particular place that we had a 123 that had been flying at the direction of a 
customer out of alternate when one of the sieges was under, one of the strips was under 
siege, and they’d been dropping too high and the chutes had been going to the enemy, 
and the customer was really upset.  He wanted to see if there’s somebody else that could 
get the load in and of course you don’t like to try to show up another a pilot, but then on 
the other hand you want to get job done for the customer.  I was an instructor pilot in the 
airplane and Jim Ryan asked me if I’d go in and talk to the customer, who was, let me 
think, Shep Johnson was the customer, and Shep said, you know, “Here’s where we want 
the load, and yeah they’re shooting at you, and yeah they’ve got 12.7s,” but he said, “I’ll 
ride with you in the jump seat.”  And I said, “You don’t have to do that Shep, but you’re 
welcome to come along if you want to.” And he rode the ride in, he went in with me 
while we dropped.  So I had a lot of respect for that.

SM:  Was that common for them to go ahead and jump in with you?

all were brave enough to do it in a heartbeat, there was no question about it, they’d do 
anything that we did, but you know they had other things to do, but Shep felt strongly 
enough about it he wasn’t going to ask anybody do anything that was crazy, but he said, 
“I’ll sit in the jump seat if you go in there.”  I said, “Yeah let’s go!” 
SM:  Do you think that was in part of an attempt on his part to I guess convey to

he must feel secure enough, if he’s willing to jump on that plane he must be fairly secure 
in knowing it’s going to succeed and there’s not going to be too much harm.

in real dire need of supplies and he not only wanted to assure me that, you know, that he 
felt that way, but also he wanted to make sure that load got on the ground and in the right 
place too.  So he went along to where he look right out the back, he could look over his 
shoulder and the jump seat could see the parachutes open and where they landed.  So it 
gave him a little, it gave him a warm fuzzy feeling to see that the troops got what they 
wanted or what they needed. 
SM:  Yeah, peace of mind. 
JM:  Yep.

that was part of one of our scheduled flights, but for projects I probably flew a half a 
dozen projects out of the ranch. 
SM:  Are there any that you can discuss or are they still all classified?

this is has been mentioned in a couple of books that I’ve read recently and I know the 
books are wrong, and I know the agency knows the books are wrong, but I don’t know if 
the agency has ever told the world what it really was, so I’m not going to say what it was, 
but there was a drop made out of the ranch on the Ho Chi Minh Trail in a very hot area

they were just long range. 
SM:  Interesting. 
JM:  Sit in the airplane for hours, some of them with internal fuel, so you’d fly 
and fly and fly and nothing real exciting about that. 
SM:  Would those typically be drops? 
JM:  No, no.  The ones that I flew were not; we’ll just put it that way. 
SM:  Okay.  How about reconnaissance flights, did you do any kind of recon, I 
mean besides the IR? 
JM:  Oh from time to time I have.  We’ve done some road recon from time to 
time.  I flew a trip out of Bangkok in the C-45 and I don’t know who the men were, I 
don’t believe they were based there, but they wanted to spend the night, in fact we did 
spend the night at the Nakhon Phanom Military Airport.  That was kind of a humorous 
experience.  It was one of the super secret base I guess in Southeast Asia where all the 
electronics eavesdropping and everything was at and they had the A-1Es and the nimrod 
B-26s flew out of there, air commandos and…We had done som e reconnaissance in Laos 
and southern Laos in the Atapoo area and it was about the time Atapoo fell.  Well, yeah, 
Atapoo had already fallen in fact when we did that.  I was coming out of Laos and I came 
across the river, we’d been down low so I just stayed low and it was just before dark, it 
was just starting to get dusk, but you know in the tropics that old like the road to 
Mandalay where the dawn comes up like thunder, it gets light in an instant and it gets 
dark in an instant, so there isn’t much twilight, but this was just before dark.  I had 
crossed the river at probably two or three hundred feet and I started to climb up to traffic 
patter altitude in Nakhon Phanom and entered, I went ahead and called the tower and told 
them that I was east of the field and entering down wind for landing and boy they were 
really shocked at that.  They said, “Oh, who are you?”  I just gave them my tail number, 
which is all we had and they wanted to know what the nature of my business.  I said, 
“I’ve got some people that want to spend the night there and they’re expected,” and 
there’s silence and they finally said, “Okay, fly the pattern and over fly the runway.”  Of

And so I landed and stopped on the runaway and boy the jeeps came flying up the road 
with security guards with guns and they look over the airplane, the airplane was painted 
gray.  That airplane was kind of an odd ball, in fact I believe it was almost all of our 
Beeches were 10-2, I believe that was a straight C-45 out of Bangkok.  But, I was 
wearing my uniform, my epaulette and everything and they thought I was Navy.  It’s a 
gray airplane you know, so they were kind of satisfied and followed us up to the ramp 
and parked us and then a car came and met the airplane and it was the customers on the

course we’re not going out until the next morning, but no body told me anything of where 
I’m going to stay.  So went in to talk to the duty officer, “Well hey have you got a place 
for me to stay tonight?”  At first he thought I was a Navy Captain with four stripes and 
he’s really all, but the he could see I’m too scruffy to be a Navy Captain.  (laughing)  He 
finally asked, “Who are you?”  I said, “Air America.”  And of course then the eyes rolled, 
‘oh Air America,’ well, yeah they got a place to put me up and they showed me to a room 
and then I went to the mess hall to eat and boy everybody there, when the word Air 
America got around I had all kinds of company wanting to know how to get a job with 
the company when they finished their tour.

JM:  No, no, I met my old [?] in the 11th Airborne division Carleson out of 
Bangkok, but no I was flying a C-46, I’d taken a load into Da Nang and there was a US-
Aid group there and they had some people entertainers that had been in a USO type show 
in Da Nang, there were several women, all in all I’d say it was thirty five or forty people, 
and they wanted a ride back to Saigon.  So we loaded them in the 46 in the bucket seats, 
  76 

--- PDF PAGE 77 ---
 
and there was a military guy that had just finished his tour of duty and he had missed his 
flight back to Saigon and he was really worried that being he missed his flight he was 
going to missed the scheduled airline flight out of Saigon back to the states.  So he asked 
if he could bum a ride and I said, “Well yeah, it’s kind of crowded in the back, if you 
want you can ride the jump seat in the cockpit.”  He said, “He’d really appreciate that.”  
He was a young captain and he’d just finished a tour of duty out of Da Nang for the Air 
Force and [was] going home.  So anyway, as we fly the trip back we got a carburetor 
problem in the airplane, it was something with the automatic mixture control, it was 
running lean and every now and then it would backfire and run rougher then the devil.  
Scared the daylights out of the people in the back and the US-Aid man that was in charge 
strolled up to the cockpit and told us, “Gentlemen, I don’t want alarm you, but I think you 
have a problem with the right engine.”  Like we didn’t know!  We told him everything 
was under control, no problem.  We pulled the mixture back to rich and then run it back 
up and everything would settle down and it would run for a while and maybe fifteen, 
twenty minutes later it’d back fire again and we’d richen it up.  Anyway, we played with 
it, we were never very far from a landing strip all the way down the coast, and as we got 
down the coast we cut over into the central part of the country and I think we flew over 
Ban Me Thuot and straight south from there to Saigon.  About Ban Me Thuot it really let 
go, it backfired several times and fire’d come out of the stacks and we couldn’t calm it 
down so we had to feather the propeller.  We feathered the prop and no big deal; I mean 
we were nine thousand feet we could glide to Saigon almost and it as no emergency.  
Anyway, this guy comes up in the cockpit, Joe Cool, “Gentlemen I don’t want to bother, 
but really I think that there’s a problem with that right engine.” And of course the 
propeller’s standing at attention out there and the Chinese co-pilot and this captain are 
about having a heart attack laughing at him, “Yeah, we know, we know.”  Anyway, we 
landed Saigon uneventfully and I’d say ‘good-bye’ to this young captain figuring I’ll 
never see him again.  Well about three years later I was flying a Volpar out of Saigon one 
night, in fact I was flying it up, I was going to Tay Ninh with it, they had had a wing spar 
problem and they wanted to x-ray the wing spars.  It was an AD that came out of the 
states that prompted this.  I was leaving Bangkok late in the afternoon going to Udorn, 
then I was going to pick up some company cargo at Udorn, and the next morning flying

hazardous.

 JM:  Yes.

 SM:  I was wondering if you knew the target audience for those leaflets because 
of course the indigenous Lao didn’t have a written language and so was it pictures for 
them or…?  
 JM:  Well pictures and language.  I’ve dropped leaflets that were printed in two or 
three different dialects.  I know they had little sections where there’d be like maybe 
Chinese and Thai and then…I th ink there was a basic Lao language also, but, at least I 
think it was the Lao language that was written.  I’ve seen lots of looked like Thai written, 
but I thought it was Lao; it was in Laos.  It might be that the printed word was used from 
Thailand, I don’t know.  Not being a historian I haven’t looked into that, but… 
 SM:  Well I guess for the hill tribes, the Hmong. 
 JM:  Oh I’m sure that very few of them could read or write anything, but we 
dropped leaflets for different reasons.  They were propaganda leaflets of course trying to 
get soldiers to defect and then we dropped leaflets I know in the search and rescue with 
George Ritter we dropped leaflets to offer a gold reward for information leading to the 
recovery of the aircraft or crew.  Those were printed in two or three different languages 
all on the same leaflet with a picture of the airplane.  I’ve heard of counterfeit currency 
being dropped, but I don’t know anything, I was never involved in that and I’ve never 
really heard anybody verify that so I’m not sure if it was just a rumor or not.   
 SM:  Well, are there any other operations or types of operations that we haven’t 
discussed yet that you can think of? 
 JM:  Well our day to day operations of course were very varied in everything 
from still landings on a short strips and offloading sometimes heavy equipment.  I’ve 
carried everything from trucks, bulldozers, you name it at one time or another we 
probably carried it.  The old motto: anything, anywhere, anytime.  But the STOL 
operations for the light aircraft, of course I wasn’t involved in the Helio program or the 
Pilatus Porter program, but they landed…and of course everything’s relative you know, if 
you’ve got an airplane that’s designed to land on 300 feet that’s one thing and then the 
one that’s designed to land with an absolute minimum of 1000 or 1500 or 2000 the 
problems were pretty much the same, airspeed control and just plain ol’ stick and rudder 
flying.  The strips though were hard to imagine an airplane landing on some of them.

They were built on ridgelines with bends in the middle; sometimes the angles that they

 SM:  When you say like a ski jump, you mean on a downward slope?  
 JM:  Yeah you would land uphill and take off downhill on a slope that… oh gosh, 
I don’t know what the steepest one was, but I know there were a lot of them probably 
exceeded seven or eight degrees; pretty good inclination. 
 SM:  Wow!  What would you describe as the most challenging strip that you had 
to fly into? 
 JM:  Well in the 123…you know every airplane  was different.  I can think of back 
in the Beech program in Saigon the shortest strip we had was a place called My Tho Nieu 
and it was a laterite strip built up with rice patties all around it.  It was absolutely you 
couldn’t land one foot short or go one foot long or either right or left without being in 
water.  It was 1600 feet long and for a Beech-18 that was, you could go unloaded and on 
empty, but that was a pretty short strip.  The 46 we were limited to 3000 feet, but we had 
some 3000 foot strips that when you added crosswind and what have you really, you 
really had to work to get that thing in and stop and the same with the 123.  We operated 
out of Samton, which due to a several different circumstances was a fairly challenging 
strip for us.  Number one the approach wasn’t all that good, you kind of let down in a 
bowl and you were turning until on short final.  They airfield had a good inclination to it 
and of course a short drop off at the end.  It had a short level spot and then it went up hill 
at a pretty good angle.  I can’t remember what the total length of it was, I think it was 
probably a couple thousand feet, but it was short.  During the PDJ [Plain de Jars] when 
Vang Pao took the PDJ back from the Communists and we were supplying the troops out 
there.  There was a strip on the north edge of the PDJ that had been 3000 feet long, but it 
had been cratered by a large bomb right in the middle, a real deep bomb crater and so 
there was only half of it that was useable.  We walked it off carefully, went in there in a 
light airplane to see if we could get in with a 123.  The strip was 1600 feet long, but there 
was real good approaches.  There was flat approach over the PDJ and you could touch 
down right on the end with heavy breaking and reverse you could get it stopped alright.  
Then taking off you would back up with your tail out over the edge of the PSP and you’d 
go to full power in the airplane, release the brakes, and you’re wheels would kind of dig

unusual about a drop I’d make a little notation.  On that one I just put heavy fire because 
since it was like I say it was quite a sight that night. 
 SM:  Do you know what Special Forces group these guys were assigned to? 
 JM:  No I don’t.  In fact I’ve often wondered who they were.  In fact I’ve asked 
Leigh Hotucek’s husband is a retired Special Forces and he’s active in that Special Forces

 JM:  Well I know that, I’ve been told on some of them they were used for roads, 
some of them were used for extending the runway…gosh I can’t, a num ber of different 
reasons that they used them.  I’ve hauled just about every type of construction material 
you can think of.  I know that one time I hauled a load of reinforcing rods; that re-bar that 
was real long.  I imagine the dad gum things were fifteen, eighteen feet long and they 
were like great big hairpins, they were curved.  I would assume they’d just cut off what 
they needed, but that stuff was so hard to handle you had to have real heavy gloves and 
the poor loaders were getting their hands all cut up loading it.  Then I took it up to Dong 
Ha, which was right on the DMZ almost up there at the northern most airport and it was 
like unraveling a puzzle trying to get that stuff out, it was all tangled up.  You know when 
they cinched it down with the tie down straps it was hard to tell which one you should
