 SM:  Was that one of the more difficult things you transported? 
 JM:  Well yeah that was a load that was sure hard to unload, probably the most 
difficult loads to unload.  One time in South Vietnam, it would have been in 1966 in the 
fall, I took a load of cement into the Dalat Lin Khan, the lower airport.  We had two 
airports at the Dalat, one at Kam Lee and I think the lower end was called Lin Khan.  At 
that time the airport wasn’t secure at night and they didn’t try to keep the troops out there 
and they would withdraw back into their fort or their garrison.  Then in the mornings 
they’d go out with mine sweepers and check the runway and the roads and they would 
reoccupy the airport.  I had been shuttling cement in there all day and my last trip in I got 
in just before dark and everybody had left!  We stood around, got on the H-F radio and 
called and said, “Hey where is everyone?”  “Well they’re gone.”  Well it was just me and 
the Chinese first officer and we were sitting on the main ramp at Lin Khan and we started 
dropping that cement sacks to the ground.  Well about every third one broke, we had a 
huge stack of cement with paper sticking out of it, it looked like a great big pile of sand, 
fine sand laying out there.  We were told to go ahead and unload it so we dumped it on 
the ground, but as we were finishing unloading the dad gum thing it started to rain!  We 
had to swing the tail, you know stand on one brake and swing the tail sharply to get 
around the dad gum stack.  We’d literally built a little mountain right up against the 
airplane.  We taxied out and left and I heard latter that I’d made a monument up there, 
everybody joked about my monument at Dalat because it was just one big great cement 
pile. 
 SM:  Which of course the rain hardened. (laughing) 
 JM:  Yes it did, solidified it! (laughing)

 SM:  Well perhaps more I guess because of your experience as a pilot anything 
that made the aircraft more difficult to handle in the air or was there anything that you 
had to fly? 
 JM:  Well we’d been overloaded on a number of occasions where they would 
make a mistake in the weight of the equipment they put on board.  I had that problem 
with a bulldozer.  The weight of the bulldozer it was I believe around, without the blade it 
was supposed to have been something like 15,000 pounds or 16,000 without all of the 
accessories and they would be hauled separately.  But they loaded it on thinking it was 15 
or 16 thousand pounds and it turned out to be kilos.  So you can figure that that one was 
grossly overloaded airplane.  We should have caught it our self, but we caught it on 
takeoff and climb out of course because the airplane performed…( laughing) it wasn’t a 
performer, it was just barely flying.  We came back making a real slow power-on landing 
of course, we landed without any trouble and slowly came back and off loaded it, but that 
was a severe overload.   
 SM:  Okay.  Now in terms of the policies and in particular against the… The war 
in Laos was, compared to Vietnam, smaller scale, emphasized having American advisors 
on the ground advising and working with indigenous military forces…co mpare that to 
Vietnam you had huge American forces kind of the American military running the show 
if you will and things didn’t seem to be going all that well in the United States in 
Vietnam.   How do you think things were going in Laos? 
 JM:  I think for the…I think they did an amazing job holding as many, I think I’ve 
heard as many as five North Vietnamese divisions were held at bay for what a decade and 
a half.  I think they did a tremendous job with a handful of Americans and a relatively 
small number of local troops.  But the difference is that…with the ex ception of just a few 
major bases up country, they fought a guerilla war, they fought the kind of war that the 
North Vietnamese were fighting in Vietnam.  It’s one thing to be constantly on the move 
and nobody knowing where you’re at and to be in a garrison situation as to how many 
casualties you’re going to take and how hard it is to fight a war.  I believe that, well of 
course I’m just one of thousands, hundreds of thousands that believe that we fought the 
wrong kind of war in Vietnam we should have been fighting our own guerilla war over 
there, but by bringing in large numbers of troops and putting them in garrison situations

we made a tremendous target for the guerilla troops.  As a result we took an awful lot of 
casualties.   
 SM:  Were you getting a lot of information on a lot of news when you were flying 
out of Bangkok and Vientiane?  Were you getting information about the war in Vietnam 
very often? 
 JM:  Oh sure we had radio and television in Bangkok and of course we all ready 
Time and Newsweek and of course the Bangkok Post was American language, English 
language newspaper.  I think we were as well informed as anyone about what was going 
on in Vietnam.  I’ve read a number of books about it and thought a lot about it sense and 
I’m sure that has a historian you’ve probably read…oh let m e think of the gentlemen’s 
name, Sheehan’s book A Bright Shining Lie. 11

 JM:  I think that he probably was as close as anybody as having analyzed the 
situation, at least the way I saw it.  I think he was right.  It disturbed me, I saw enough of 
how ineffective we were.  We were using the South Vietnamese army and how we were 
being taken advantage of the South Vietnamese that I couldn’t tell the good guys from 
the bad guys, I guess that’s what I’m saying.  I could give you a for instance.  When I 
was flying to USAID load into Kontum one time. We off loaded it, the trucks pulled up, 
deuce-and-a-half pulled up and loaded all these supplies.  They were mixed loads of food 
supplies primarily, there were some sacks of rice, but there were cans of soup and there 
were cans of beans and there was all kinds of just general food stuff in the load.  They 
loaded two trucks with that stuff and the trucks left and we started up and taxies out.  I 
took off and as I was circling, I was told to turn out to the left so I was turning to the 
north as I was circling around I saw the two trucks winding out in the countryside going 
out to a USAID warehouse.  There was guys standing up in the load I the back throwing 
cases of stuff along side the road that they’d come by and pick up later obviously.  I was 
just wondering how much of that load ever got to the darn warehouse.  I think that was 
just a picture of what you could expect with almost everything we did in Vietnam.  We 
probably only got ten cents on the dollar, of every dollar we spent over there we got that 
much benefit out of it.

 JM:  Oh gosh, I’ll tell ya’ no it was like urinating in the ocean to raise the tide.

airport.  The USAID guy that was supposed to meet the airplane wasn’t there, there were

hanging around the airport and I waiting, and waiting, no load, no truck and I said, “Well 
if we dump this stuff out on the ramp it’s all going to be stolen.  Again it was the same 
kind of food stuff, there was rice, there was canned goods, and a mixture.  They said, 
“Well boy we know somebody that would like it.”  There was an American doctor for 
catholic relief hospital that took care of the Montagnards and she had a real common 
name and I can’t…that was the only tim e that I really met her and it was either Smith or 
Jones, it was a name like that.  They said she is at the tail end of the food chain, she 
hardly gets anything and she’s really doing a great job.  And I said, “Well can you guys 
get me some trucks to haul this stuff out there?”  and they said, “You bet!”  So it wasn’t 
long before the GIs brought a couple of deuces and a halves out there and we loaded all 
the stuff on.  They said, “Come with you, we’ll show you where it’s going.”  They took 
us out to her house and of course they couldn’t believe what we were giving them, but I 
think that’s probably the only load I delivered in that area that we really got a dollar for a 
dollars’ worth of material.  It didn’t go to who it was supposed to go to, but it was well 
used.   
 SM:  Yeah.  It certainly fits in with the humanitarian mission. 
 JM:  Yeah.  Yeah, as far as I was concerned it was the best load I ever delivered 
in South Vietnam.

It was everywhere.  It was…and it’s a ho rrible thing.  You know you see so much of it 
that you become…I’ve always tried to be  open minded as I can be about people you 
know, it’s real easy to paint with a real broad brush and say all people are bad for this

 SM:  Well of course this touches on a rather sensitive subject also concerning Air 
America and operations in Southeast Asia and of course given the popularity of that 
movie with Mel Gibson and some I guess writings from certain authors.  Air America has 
been accused of participating in some of the illicit activities.  So I wanted you opinion on 
those controversies and those issues, especially about [?]… 
 JM:  You mean about the drugs? 
 SM:  drugs and things like that. 
 JM:  Ah, you know I flew out of every major base that we had in Southeast Asia, I 
was physically based at like Saigon, and Bangkok and Vientiane and I flew so often out 
of Udorn you could almost say I was based in Udorn.  I spent almost as many nights 
down there as I did in Vientiane.  I flew every multi-engine program, almost every one.  
And I never in the whole time I was over never saw, never had the slightest indication 
that anybody involved with the CIA or with Air America was smuggling drugs for their 
own personal use or personal gain or for any other reason.  I just saw not instance of it.  
Now having said that I’ll say that there’s now question that there were people on our 
airplanes from time to time that had the stuff in their pockets, but we just didn’t have the 
where with all to search every soldier that got on our airplane or every local that got on 
our airplane for drugs, particularly when we’re evacuating people and moving people in 
and out as fast as we did with the quick turn arounds we had.  So…but you know if you 
want to use that as a criteria well then major airlines hauled more drugs then we did 
because the people that were on the backs of those things had drugs too. 
 SM:  Absolutely.  Well I understand and maybe you can either clarify or maybe 
not that some soldiers some of the Lao were actually paid with heroin.  Did you ever hear 
of that or ? 
 JM:  No I never heard of it or saw anything, but I heard that Vang Pao had a stash 
of it that he kept at Long Chin and that was strictly rumor, but I had heard that.  It was 
like, not that they used it or he used it, but it was just something that they used as kind of 
their Fort Knox, so they had to have some capital, but I don’t know whether that was true

 JM:  Yeah, Chris Robbins wrote a book in the early days…and nobody would talk 
to Chris except peripheral people, disgruntled wives, the kickers, very few pilots, non that 
were held in high regard gave him any, any help at all on this book.  But he did I’ll say on 
his behalf, he did some research and he had a whole lot of things that were right, but he 
told a whole lot of stories that were just that, stories embellished.  It was kind of kick the 
tires light the fires, gave the impression that we were cowboys.  The movie carried it to 
the extreme you know, where Robert Downey Jr. is flying the right seat of a 123 and tells 
the captain to bail out.  Can you imagine that? (laughing)  And when they were hit the 
captain is pumping something on the left hand side of the seat.  I saw film with Ed 
Adams and Ed and I both have got thousands of hours in the 123.  He looked at me and I 
looked at him and Ed said, “What do you think he’s doing?”  I said, “Well the only thing 
he could be doing is pulling the gust lock on them!”  (laughing) that was the only thing 
that was over there.  But it was…you know… Th ey really garbaged up what could have 
been a hell of a good movie if it was done right.  We really did have a bail out and weird 
Neil was really a character there’s no question about that and Weird did tell the co-pilot, 
in fact it was Bill Caruthers, my friend Bill was the co-pilot on that flight that bailed out.  
Bill…Bill h e told Bill to bail out and Bill went back and he came up and said, “Yeah I 
left my camera!”  Of course old Weird Neil was having a hard time controlling the 
airplane, evidently it’d had some control cables shot out and had real major problem and 
he was trying to circle over the only friendly position in the immediate area so that when 
the troop bailed out they would be in a safe area rather then in the enemy.  Of course that 
unnerved Neil you know, ‘Get the hell out!’  Then after of course the last one went out 
Neil had to go all the way to the back of the airplane and jump.  Neil said that the 
airplane arrived to the ground about the same time he did.  It was very exciting if it was 
done right it would have been a lot more interesting I think then the movie.   
 SM:  What about some of the other antics? 
 JM:  Well the coloring book.  Neil did that.  He did all sorts of things for shock 
value.  He carried a coloring book and crayons in his flight kit and he would…he loved to 
get new first officers brand new to Laos and he would give them the controls, it’d be their 
leg and he would just intently color very carefully the whole flight and the next leg when 
it would be his leg to fly and the co-pilot’s leg to rest he’d hand them the coloring book

 SM:  Oh okay. (laughing)  How about the…let’s see, in the movie they showed 
pilots jumping from aircraft to aircraft and not just fixed wing, but area pilots jumping 
from rotary… 
 JM:  Rotary wing to fixed wing. Yeah.  No, no, no.   
 SM:  Fixed wing to rotary, back and forth. 
 JM:  Nope.  Nope.   
 SM:  How much did that happen to your…?  
 JM:  I don’t think rotary wing to fixed wing ever happened.  There was some 
rotary wing pilots…we had a couple of opportun ities for rotary wing that had fixed wing 
experience to go to fixed wing or back and forth, but then anytime you bid a program like 
that, particularly rotary wing to fixed wing you’re going to bid it and go through the 
training program and probably a change of base involved and it’s going to be a month or 
two process.  It’s not going to be a matter of walking across a ramp and getting in the 
other airplane.  Now however, from one type of helicopter to another type of helicopter it 
could be that they were checked out in more then one helicopter.  In fixed wing, ah heck 
I’ve flown as many as three or four different aircraft at one time where I was current and 
qualified in all of them.  In Saigon I know at one time I was qualified in the 10-2, the 
Apache, the Dornier, and the C-46 and I went back and forth from time to time when they 
needed me in one or the other I’d go back and forth.  In Bangkok I flew the BTB-18, the 
DC-3, the C-46, and the 10-2 and stayed current in all of them.  In Vientiane I stayed 
current in the 123 and the C-46 just about he whole time I was up there going back and 
forth.  Whenever they’d need me in one or the other I’d go back and forth. 
 SM:  So that kind of…within the airc raft specific specialty you could jump 
between aircraft?  That is fixed wing aircraft you could go from aircraft to aircraft, same 
for the helicopters? 
 JM:  Yeah if you were current and checked out in it.  Now having said that, we 
had a seniority system and you had to bid on airplanes.  It was just alike an airline…and 
you bid a base.  You know you hear Civil Air Transport, Southern Air Transports, Air

Asia and Air America and you think ‘oh well these are all different companies,’ but we 
were…all the pilots were on the sam e seniority level.  Seniority number one of course 
was Bigone who flew a Convair 880 and later the 727 out of Taipei and then the junior 
pilot was probably a co-pilot on a C-47 or a caribou or something in Saigon.  As you got 
senior and they needed pilots in a particular program they put it up for bid and you would 
bid the program.  If you wanted to fly the Porter program you’d bid the Porter, if you 
wanted to fly the 46 you’d bid the 46. When you got senior enough to hold the slot then 
the company would put you through the ground school and check you out, but when you 
did bid a program or a base you had to stay there for at least a year.  You know they’re 
not just going to keep retraining you constantly unless they need you in another program 
and then of course they’ve got the right to put you in it if they needed you.  So if you bid 
a program and you’ve been checked out in it and then after a year you bid another 
program [and] you’re checked out in that as long as you keep going back and flying the 
other airplane occasionally to stay current… and all it takes is three takeoffs and landings 
in [the] preceeding ninety days and of course it takes going to the ground school at least 
once a year and it takes a flight check at least once a year to stay current.  If you do all 
those things then you’re qualified in two airplanes and you can add it to three, four, and 
on up the line; it’s how much you want to put up with.  The reason I flew so many 
airplanes in Saigon is we considered…I was an IP in all t hose airplanes, an instructor 
pilot and a check airman.  They didn’t want a check airmen instructor pilot designated for 
a program that only had two programs.  We only had two Dorniers and we only had two 
Apaches in Saigon.  So they just lump it together and call it the light airplane program 
and if you were the IP and check airmen they did it in all three.  Of course you had to go 
through the process of staying current in all three.  In Bangkok that’s where the stand 
board was.  That’s where Don Cofstaff who was the head of training and if you want I 
can tell you an interesting story in Bangkok with Cofstaff. 
 SM:  Oh yeah, by all means. 
 JM:  Don Cofstaff was outstanding, probably as good a pilot as I’ve ever flown 
with and technically one of the most astute aviators I’ve ever met.  I believe it was TWA 
that he was the head of training for before he came Air America, but he was hired to be 
the head of our stand board and to write all of our manuals and standardize our training 
  92 

--- PDF PAGE 93 ---
 
and flying and he did a heck of a good job of it.  He wrote some of the best manuals I’ve 
ever seen before or since.  Descriptive pictures of how to do all of the maneuvers exactly, 
what was expected of you on a check ride… The guy was a genius!  But Don…his 
offices where in Bangkok and he had men that he hired for each program.  He had a man 
that was the head of the training and the C-47 and DC-4 that was Don Cofstaff, I mean 
Ed Linebach.  He had a fellow that was in charge of the Beech 18 and the BTB-18 that 
was Rocky Myers.  He had…oh he had one fo r every airplane.  While he was in Bangkok 
and I was in Bangkok we didn’t have that many pilots down there, I got to know Don real 
well and I had been flying a VIP flight in a Volpar to Wa Hin the resort area and I call it 
amounted to is a general that wanted to play golf.  I’m hanging around the hotel, I didn’t 
have anything to read, and I was bored out of my mind, I’d been down there for a couple 
of days.  So I went out to the airport and I got my flight kit out of the airplane and I 
started…you know I had a few paraphernalia to read in there, but the primary thing was 
the flight manual for the airplane.  I knew the airplane well, but with nothing else to do I 
sat under a palm tree and I read that flight manual cover to cover about four times!  
Playing games in my mind…norm ally you would remember a few general specifics about 
your instruments, but you didn’t take the time to remember verbatim about looking at the 
instrument.  The maximum, the minimum and the normal range and things like that, but I 
played those kind of games head.  One the flight back to Bangkok as soon as I get at 
Bangkok, who meets me at the ramp but Don Cofstaff with three gentlemen from the 
FAA!  Don said that these men had came down from Japan and the company had put him 
in to be a designated airmen, a designated flight examiner and of course he had to take, 
the had to show the FAA that we had the proper training programs and that he could give 
the check rides and evaluate the training.  So they were there to evaluate Don evaluating 
one of us and he said, “Now I’m going to give you an oral on the airplane.”  So he got the 
flight manual and we went to his office and the three FAA guys and Don started asking 
me questions out of the manual and I just started rattling off and Don started…when he 
say that I knew the airplane very well he got more and more technical and I kept rattling 
them off.  Finally this FAA guy said, “Give me the manual!”  He looked and he started 
asking technical questions and I kept rattling them off.  He said, “Well Mr. Cofstaff it 
appears you have an excellent ground school!”  (laughing)  And Don loved me, he was

 JM:  Yeah he was getting up there.  In fact I think that’s probably the reason he

we did have some registered.  9-8-4, 9-8-5 were registered and we had all of the Beech 
18s were n-registered; I don’t believe we had any of those foreign registered.  Some of

 SM:  Was that the only encounter you had with FAA in a regulator…?  
 JM:  (laughing) I had a real funny one.  I had a flight to Tainan out of Bangkok.  
We were flying from Bangkok to Da Nang, Da Nang to Hong Kong, Hong Kong to 
Taipei, that was the route of flight and those were all tech stops it wasn’t that we 
were…we had som e company cargo I think to offload in Da Nang, but most of them were 
just to get fuel and go on.  A FAA examiner that had supposed to have done a route check 
with Pan-American between Bangkok and Hong Kong and then pick up there other Pan-
American flight from Hong Kong to Bangkok had missed the first flight and now it was 
obvious that he was going to miss the second flight that was late in the day coming the 
other way.  You know there was Pan-Am one and two that went around the world, one 
went eastbound one went westbound.  Anyway, he was frantically checking all the airline 
counters seeing if there was any aircraft going to Hong Kong that he could get on.  When 
he went to the CAT counter they said, “Well we’ve got a C-46 freight airplane going up 
there.”  Oh it tickled him to death because it would get up there early enough in the 
evening that he’d be able to catch the Pan-Am flight that was coming back the other way, 
the westbound flight.  So he gets in the airplane, in the jump seat, and I’ve got a Chinese 
first officer and we taxi out and we take off and the Chinese was one of the new, 
relatively new Chinese pilot and he was a little nervous he’d never flown with me before.  
He’d fumbled on the check list, he’s English wasn’t quite as good as some of them and 
this guy started picking on the co-pilot and really irritated the devil out of me.  He was 
acting like he was on a check ride with us.  Oh, early in the flight I’d had enough of him 
and I turned around and said, “Look Mister.  I don’t know if you noticed that when you 
got in here, but this is a Chinese airplane.  This airplane has got a big B on the tail, not an 
N, and I have a Chinese pilot license and this gentlemen sitting next to me has a Chinese 
pilots’ license and you have absolutely no authority here.  And if you don’t leave this co-
pilot alone I am putting you off in Da Nang!”  At that he got a little militant at first telling 
me I was also an American and had a U.S. pilots’ license and tried to intimidate me.  I

airplane out of Bangkok.  I was still based in Saigon.

had a lot of real close friends that were down there you know flying.  It was pretty 
exciting period of time for them so there were lots of Tet stories that came out, but 
being… I w as still in Bangkok at that time so it was… I couldn’t tell you a whole lot 
about it except second hand information.   
 SM:  Okay.  But you did hear about it relatively quickly? 
 JM:  Oh yeah we heard about it quickly yeah.

 SM:  Of course shortly after Tet it would have been about May of 1968 President 
Johnson made the decision not to run for reelection.  What do you remember hearing 
about that and what did you and other colleagues thing about that? 
 JM:  You know I was rather detached I guess from American politics at that time 
as far as presidential politics.  I normally would have been very interested in it, but we 
were so busy in our day to day mission and it seemed like our whole world was what we 
were doing that it really didn’t mean much to me at the time.  I missed everything that 
poor ol’ Johnson went through with the flower children.   
 SM:  When Nixon won the election in ’68 and of course he came in with this 
secret plan to win the war…did you notice any changes or any differences after Nixon 
took power in 1969 and he started implementing eventually of course what became to 
Vietnam the vietnamization and basically started winding down the war in Vietnam.  
What happened in Laos during that period say from 1969 to 1972? 
 JM:  ’69 and ’70 was the wildest period I think they ever had up there.  We were 
going full bore.  In fact ’69, ’70, and  ’71, ’72 was absolute go, go, go; it was the busiest 
time I can ever remember in my years with Air America.  Particularly ’69 when they got 
the PDJ back it was wild up there!  ’70 was too, it was…lo ts and lots of things happening 
and of course if you look at the plaque we lost an awful lot of people during those years 
too. 
 SM:  How long did Vang Pao hang on to the PDJ? 
 JM:  Oh it was less then a year. 
 SM:  It was? 
 JM:  Yeah. 
 SM:  Were you part of the… I w ould imagine they had to evacuate? 
 JM:  Oh yeah, yeah, yeah.  We take them in, bring them out.   
 SM:  What was that evacuation like? 
 JM:  I don’t remember it being much more then just a routine operation.   It 
wasn’t one of these last minute things like the Fall of Saigon with the guys beating down 
the gates when they saw the handwriting on the wall, they got out. 
 SM:  Okay so it was not a rushed…?

 JM:  The ones that I was involved in was not a route, no it was more or less

 SM:  Okay.  When did things start winding down in Laos for you as an Air 
America pilot? 
 JM:  It never did from the time I left!   
 SM:  It never did? 
 JM:  In 1973, actually yeah it was ‘73; the U.S. Military left Vietnam, pulled out 
completely right after the prisoners were released.  The U.S. had a cutoff time as to when 
they could give anymore material weapons or support to the Vietnamese, South 
Vietnamese.  As a result they upgraded their Air Force with 130, which meant that the 
123s, 119s, a whole lot of the other aircraft were obsolete and the Air Force decided they 
wanted those back for other friendly governments like Thailand, Laos, the Philippines 
and wherever and they wanted those airplanes back and of course the South Vietnamese 
had several squadrons scattered around the country.  The U.S. weren’t supposed to be in 
there anymore so they brought some crews from Air America from Laos over to ferry 
those aircraft to Clark Air Force base in the Philippines.  They asked for volunteers for 
this and I can’t remember exactly how I got hooked into it, I think Jim Ryan asked me to 
go on it.  It was supposed to be a voluntary thing and the idea of living out of your 
suitcase TDY back and forth to Clark wasn’t very appealing to any of us.  I think they 
were having a hard time getting the crews to volunteer.  I think it was Jim that asked me 
if I would go over and ferry as many of these airplanes as I could and take a co-pilot with 
me whoever I wanted.  The thing that we were lacking was maintenance support.  I recall 
they were supposed to use VNAF for maintenance support and they were poor.  But I 
went over; I flew with Bill Caruthers at first.  Bill was the one that bailed out of the 123 
with Weird Neil when they were shot down in the Bolivan.  Shortly after that he had a 
real narrow escape in a 123 with George Ackley up in Long Chin.  A T-28…as they were 
getting ready to taxi out for takeoff, they were getting ready to pull into position, a T-28 
landed without any hydraulics, no brakes, and went roaring off the end…he didn’t want 
to run into the karst, which was like a brick wall at the end of the runway, it was a sheer 
cliff.  He swerved the airplane into the parking ramp and ran into the side of the 123.  He 
would have hit right in the cockpit, but Ackley saw him coming and added full power and 
  98 

--- PDF PAGE 99 ---
 
it pulled the airplane up just far enough to where it went under the wing, sheared the 
propeller off of the airplane and sheared one of the jets off, and crashed into the fuselage 
under the wing tearing a great big hole in the fuselage and killing… I don’t rem ember 
how many were killed, but there were two or three killed that were sitting in the jump 
seats in the airplane.  Of course Caruthers thought that thing was coming right through 
the cockpit, he had already got his seatbelt off and was almost in Ackley’s lap when the 
thing hit!  Now that would’ve been the second close call that Caruthers had had in…oh 
heck in less the ninety days!  So when this ferry mission came up where they were just 
going to ferry airplanes across the South China Sea that sounded like what he wanted and 
he volunteered to go.  I remember we went just before 1973 I think we got over there in 
December as I recall and we were there it might have been right after Christmas because 
it was New Year’s Eve one of the first flights we took out of Tan Son Nhut we had a 
123…they were outfitting them , the VNAF were putting the drop tanks on them and the 
internal fuel too so that we could fly non-stop from Saigon to Clark.  They were hooking 
up the drop tanks and it required a physical check that they had to take this plate off of 
the wing that was way out outboard towards the tip, but it was a plate where the 
connections were made from the drop tank to the line that ran into, through the wing into 
the main tank.  You were supposed to pressurize them, there was an air compressor in the 
pylon and they would pressurize the line and check for leaks and then after that check 
was made they would screw this inspection plate back down onto the wing.  The 
inspection plate was flush and it was mounted with little beveled machine screws that 
screw down into the spar and there’s quite a load on that plate because it’s almost an 
integral part of the leading edge of the wing.  On takeoff you’ve got a lot of negative 
force over that.  Well what had happened they had a shift changed and the airplane had 
just been signed off that the airplane had checked out all right, but the mechanic that was 
putting the plate had putting the leading edge screws down the front of the plate all the 
way down…this plate was probably thirty inch es long and ten inches wide or twelve 
inches wide.  So the screws on the leading edge of it were all in, but around the edges and 
the backside were not and it’s time to go to lunch he gets off the wing and leaves.  The 
next shift comes, the airplane’s been signed off checked okay, they release the airplane.  
Caruthers and I get in the airplane and taxi out, takeoff and on takeoff just as the airplane

rotated the control wheel…in a 123 the cap tain has got one hand on the nose wheel 
steering and the other hand, the right hand on the recip throttle, the co-pilot has his left 
hand on the jet throttles, which are toggle switches below the recip throttles and his right 
hand is flying ailerons and holding the nose forward, holding forward pressure on the 
yoke so the nose wheel’s tight on the ground and then using the ailerons to steer for 
cross-wind.  So both pilots are busy on the takeoff role.  The procedure is that at rotation 
the co-pilot calls out the rotation speed, the captain takes his hand off of the nose wheel 
steering onto the yoke and then hits the mic button, which is right there with his thumb 
and says, “It’s my yoke.” And the co-pilot releases the yoke.  The captain rotates and 
then you’re airborne and then after that of course you start through your after takeoff and 
climb check button.  At the point that I rotated the airplane the control wheel snapped out 
of my hand and the airplane started a violent roll to the left almost drug the wing on the 
ground and both of us grab the yoke and as hard as we could hold it off to the right we 
were probably still in close to a seventy degree bank!  The airplane was out of control!  It 
was jerking back and forth, the wheel was jerking right and left like crazy!  It was hard to 
hold onto.  We declared an emergency of course, we hit the mic and said you know that 
we were having a control failure and declared emergency.  I, being an old C-46, C-47 tail 
dragger pilot you’re real, you’re always aware of asymmetrical thrust and what it can do 
for you.  You start to lose directional control you close one throttle on landing or takeoff 
with a C-46.  First thing that came to me I just reached up and pulled the right recip 
throttle back to the stop and the airplane yaned out of course this tremendous amount of 
drag on the right side and it raised the left wing up to about a thirty degree bank.  Now 
we had a little control, but has the two jets and the other recips started to accelerate the 
airplane the roll started to get steeper so.   By manipulating the throttles we managed to 
maintain, oh somewhere between a twenty and a thirty degree bank and we were making 
a big skidding turn.  Every time I tried to add power back in to the right recip of course 
the airplane the roll to the left would get greater.  So we made a big skidding turn around 
the airport and I tried to land on the main runway, the left runway, but I was at about a 
forty-five degree angle as I came across the end of so I couldn’t land on that one.  I 
continued to turn and about half way down the right runway I was lined up, but although 
still in a steep bank but to heck with that.  I just chopped all the power and planted it on

wrong.  What had happened that plate on takeoff had lifted up like a giant spoiler and of 
course it disturbed the airflow over the aileron and the aileron was snapping back and 
forth like crazy.  It was a wild experience.  
 SM:  Now what would happen when you…when som ething like that happened?  
Was the mechanic…you know, what happened?  
 JM:  Oh we went back and they immediately got up on the wing and they could 
see the plate was bent then; it was bent up like a spoiler.  There was no question what was 
wrong you could see it from the ground.  It was funny there was an Air Force colonel that 
was left there as a liaison man for the VNAF that was real red in the face because you 
know, this had happened on his watch and I understand he was up for brigadier.  He 
wrote a nasty letter to the company saying that the pilots didn’t check their controls 
before takeoff and the he suggested that we use the Air Force method where one man on 
the ground showed whether the airline was up or down and all this baloney.  Well it had 
nothing to do with the controls, you know absolutely nothing, the controls were 
functioning normally.  Of course our chief pilot wrote back a nasty letter to him telling 
him that he suggested he’d learn a little bit more about airplanes before he made those 
kinds of suggestions.     
 SM:  (laughing) Oops!  Okay.  But did the mechanic…did you ever see the 
mechanic that made the mistake? 
 JM:  No.  There’s a bunch of them, there’s six or eight of them in a group you 
know and as to which guy was actually responsible I’m sure everybody was saying ‘it 
wasn’t my job.’ 
 SM:  Who did the mechanic work for? 
 JM:  VNAF, they were Vietnamese.  They were the Vietnamese Air Force. 
 SM:  Oh.  My goodness!  Okay.  What other close calls like that did you have, 
anything? 
 JM:  Oh I’m sure there are others, but I don’t want to bore you forever.  We could 
talk about that kind of stuff for a long period of time.

 SM:  Anything else that stands out though? 
 JM:  Well.  We had a… I was checking out a new captain, in fact it was… not 
Larry it was Terry, Terry Fraiser who lives up in the Seattle area now; I just saw him at 
the reunion.  I was checking him out on his final ride as captain.  During out checkout 
procedures you have your normal training you know, which is all your emergency 
procedures and steep turns and stalls and etcetera and then after that you have to fly… if 
that satisfactorily passed then you’re upgraded to captain.  Before you were turned lose 
on the line by yourself you have to have twenty-five hours of IOE, initial operating 
experience, with an instructor pilot check airman in the right seat.  So I was flying the 
right seat with Terry and this was his last day.  He had his twenty-five hours he got in that 
day and we’d been flying a lot of STOL operations on the Bolivan, it’s a muddy dirty 
strips and the airplane was splattered with mud.  We came back in to alternate and we 
were going to pickup some people on alternate, we were going back to Udorn, it was the 
end of his checkout and he was officially a captain.  We came into alternates, we called 
for gear down and we got the indication on down wind that we had three gear.  The 
indicator said, “Down.” And we called for the pin and there the kickers physically put 
pins in for the gear you know to lock them down.  Then there’s one for the nose wheel, 
which is the long rod that goes in on a bulkhead below the cockpit, the cockpit’s on a 
ladder elevated from the floor of the airplane.  That nose wheel pin goes forward several 
feet into the nose wheel and then there’s a big round pin about an inch and a half in 
diameter that goes into each main gear strut through the main trust and into the strut.  The 
kicker called that all the pins were in.  Well on touch down, we touched down real 
lightly; he made a beautiful landing.  The gear horn came on showing ‘gear unsafe 
warning’ and the sensor indicator on the right main gear showed unsafe.  I said, “What 
the hell is this?  Well the pins are in what difference does it make?”  I thought it was just 
a micro switch problem, but then the airplane started leaning to the right and it was 
starting to go off to the right and of course we used differential power [and] kept it on the 
runway.  The right main just slowly folded up into the well.  Again the airplane drug on 
the jack pad and a little bit of skin damage on the bottom, but the propeller never touched 
the ground.  There was no damage to speak of to the airplane except the right main gear 
is folded up in the well.  We’d always been told up until that time that it was impossible

to put the pin in if the gear wasn’t all the way down and Lockheed.  If it was a little bit 
out of being Lockheed you couldn’t get the pin in.  Well this gear was so far from being 
down that when they put the pin in it was in front of the strut and the airplane on 
touchdown tried to, the gear tried to come down and the extension, the hydraulic 
extension that pushed it down tried its best to hold it, but of course it couldn’t support the 
weight of the airplane and it slowly folded up into the well.  Actually the pin is what kept 
it from going down all the way!  But it was just one of those things.  Of course the 
company send their flying safety people up and their accident investigators like Doug 
Dreyfus that just passed away, and they jack the airplane up, put the gear down and flew 
it back to Vientiane, put it on stands in the maintenance hanger and duplicated the same 
malfunction and it was mud and dirt that had choked around a couple of micro switches 
and gave us a false indication and it also jammed the gear and of course the real culprit 
was putting the pin in in front of the gear.  So after that the fix on it was that the flight the 
kickers had to carry flashlights and shine a light in there to make sure that the strut was 
down when they put the pin in.   
 SM:  Oh okay.  But it was an honest mistake. 
 JM:  Oh yeah it was an honest mistake.  Oh yeah it wasn’t the kickers fault.  He’d 
put the pin in thousands of times.  They’d all been told, hell I’d been told as an instructor 
pilot you couldn’t get the pin in if it wasn’t all the way down and Lockheed.   
 SM:  Any… But no one would be reprim anded in any instances like this? 
 JM:  Well no.  No not as long as they can ascertain that it wasn’t your, that you 
didn’t make a mistake. 
 SM:  What was the relationship like between the different types of employees 
working therewith the pilots, kickers, administration?  Bureaucracy is I think tend to…  
well internal conflict inevitable… 
 JM:  I know what you saying.  I tell you this, there naturally are going to be minor 
irritations between them.  The biggest irritation between flight crews and maintenance 
was in write ups and write offs.  If there’s something wrong with the airplane the pilot is 
supposed to write it up to where the mechanic can understand what the devil is the 
problem and then the write off is supposed to be what they did to fix the problem as the 
mechanic and from time to time they’d get irritated with each other because their write

offs or their write ups were garbled to where it was hard for them to understand.  But I 
can tell you this, I never met a flight crew member with Air America that didn’t think that 
our maintenance was the best in the world.  I’ve flown with a lot of people before and 
after and I can say that our maintenance people did an outstanding job.   We beat the hell 
out of those airplanes all day long, we’d bring them back looking dirty and hang dog and 
maybe have ten or twelve discrepancies of things that we broke during the day from the 
rough use that we had on the airplane and of course a lot of those airplanes were really 
getting up in years.  The next morning you’d pick that airplane up and you thought it 
came out of the factory!  It was sparkling clean, everything that was written up was 
written off, and it was fixed and it flew beautifully!  I had nothing but respect for 
maintenance.  You know you can get some problems… I re member Weird Neil one time 
had a large rat, came out of the cargo in the C-46 and then hid some place and they 
couldn’t find it.  He wrote up that there was a large rat terrorizing the passengers in the 
cargo compartment and the write off on that one was ‘large rat counter terrorized by 
entire RMD department.’  (laughing)  But that’s the kind of stuff you got.  I remember 
one time Walter Rosenfield came in and the airplane that he was supposed to fly the 
propeller wasn’t on it and of course…the logbook should have never been released to 
operations, but due to a little bureaucratic snafu it was.  So he just took the logbook and 
wrote up that the right, left engine will over… the airplane not  air worthy left engine will 
over speed on takeoff.  Of course it would over speed the minute you started because you 
know prop is like your counter weight on it.  Of course your RMD says, “How does he 
know the engine’s going to over speed?”  They got a little hot under the collar until they 
realized there was no propeller on the end. (laughing) 
 SM:  (laughing) Okay.  Kind of almost pranks…?  
 JM:  Yeah actually I think we got along very well and of course as far as the 
kickers were concerned I thought those guys walked on water.  I had nothing but respect 
for them.  They are hard, hard working guys.  You know loading that heavy equipment, 
laying on your back, and tying trucks down… we  carried 155s, 105s, trucks, trailers, all 
kinds of really difficult stuff to load and tie down and those guys always figured out a 
way to do it and do it safely.  They always balanced the airplane right, they really did an 
excellent job.  Then of course they would rig the parachutes and they were indispensable

 SM:  How about going up the chain?  How much interaction did you have with 
management? 
 JM:  Well of course we had interaction with the chief pilot on a daily basis, you 
know at least your base chief pilot. 
 SM:  How about administration, going up higher? 
 JM:  Well you know, Grundy was a real hands on man.  Did I mention to you 
about the one instance that I had with Grundy? 
 SM:  I don’t think so.  
 JM:  In Saigon, I think I did, but maybe not it might have been somebody else we 
were talking about recently.  I was landing on a strip in Vietnam that had a big exercise 
going on with 130s flying in and out and the parking area was separated from the runway 
by two taxiways that had culverts underneath them and PSP, which is perforated steel 
planking, to the parking ramp.  The 130s have got a narrower footprint then the 46s and 
they’d pushing the dirt out on both sides of the PSP and our gear on a 46 was very wide.  
We had to swing wide and we only had about twelve inches to center it on each side of 
the wheels to stay on the PSP.  As I was taxing across the PSP gave way on the right side 
and the airplane started to lean into the right and I thought ‘oh God!’  So I immediately 
shut the engine down and positioned the props so it wouldn’t hit the ground.  The 
airplane kept sinking and sinking.  The Chinese co-pilot screamed, “Bu How!”  the 
minute your airplane started sinking, which is very bad in Chinese.  When the airplane 
wound up, my God it was leaning at an horrendous angle to the right, it was almost down 
in that gully, but it wasn’t scratched, but boy was it out of service.  So I took pictures 
with my Polaroid I had with me that the gear was centered, the left one was twelve inches 
right… you could see a little skid  mark where it’d moved about two or three inches to the 
right as it skidded sideways because the airplane was sinking down into the ditch.  They 
had to send the mechanics up from Saigon with all kinds of jacks and materials that 
would jack that airplane up and rebuild that ramp until they got the airplane out of there.  
It took several days of hard work to get it out.  Well while all this was going on my local 
chief pilot, which was George Calhoun, had flown up there in another airplane [to]

investigate it.  “So god John you did everything you could you know, you did a good job 
of shutting the engine down, position the prop so that nothing got damaged.  Don’t worry

happened and when I got to Tainan…if you’re going to be up there for a few days they 
always send you to Taipei to get your link training updated.  So I went up and did my 
four or six hours of link training and I was in the officers’ club having lunch at noon and 
the systems chief pilot Don Tears came in.  He saw me and did a double take, came over 
and he said, “Are you up here to see Grundy?”  I said, “No, I’m here on maintenance test 
flight, I mean check flight, the airplanes down in Tainan and I came up on the inter-island 
flight, I’m going back tomorrow.”  He said, “Oh God!  You haven’t gotten the word.”  I 
said, “No, I guess not.  What’s up?”  He said, “Boy is Grundy mad at you.  He wants to 
see you right now.  You better go to the headquarters and see Grundy right now!”  “Ga’ 
okay.”  So I went down to the CAT building and went to see Mr. Grundy and in the 
meantime he hadn’t received the customer, the company correspondence with all the 
pictures and the letter from George Calhoun on his investigation, all he knew was that 
there was a 46 stuck and some idiot pilot taxied the airplane in a ditch.  He had just 
finished reading the letter when I came in and I said, “Mr. Grundy you wanted to see me, 
McRainey?”  He looked up and he said, “Not anymore.  That’s all good-bye.” (laughing) 
That was the end of it. 
 SM:  Okay.  How about George Duell, did you ever meet him? 
 JM:  Yes.  [I] met him in Washington D.C. I think really in the early days of 
FEPA, Far East Pilots Association; some of our negotiations back there.  It seemed to 
me… I can’t rem ember when George Duell left completely.  He might not have been in 
an official capacity even then because I believe Paul Velty was really in charge, was 
really running the show at that time.  But I found everybody that I met to be gentlemen 
and first rate people.  I thought the world of Paul Velty and the old vice president [of] 
flight operations was Jim Walker I believe.  I think Jim committed suicide years ago, but 
he was a fine gentlemen, I liked him.  I thought they had a good management team.   
 SM:  This will end the interview with Mr. John McRainey on June 20th.

st of June 2001 at approximately eleven twenty Lubbock time.  I’m in Lubbock 
Texas and Mr. McRainey is in McAllen Texas.  Sir why don’t we go ahead and follow up 
a couple of questions about your time in Southeast Asia real quick, I was curious, at what 
point did you know that Air America was shutting down? 
 JM:  Well I left before I really knew it was shutting down.  We… you know the 
company had gone on so long dating way back to the old china days that we all thought 
that while the mission might change the company would probably continue on.  We 
thought it was a long project job.  We realized Southeast Asia wasn’t going to last 
forever, but we thought there’d be other missions that would probably keep at least some 
of the pilots employed.  (moments of silence)  You there? 
 SM:  Oh yes sir. 
 JM:  Oh okay.  In other words we weren’t looking at it as all the flying stopping 
like it did with the Fall of Saigon.   
 SM:  Now when you did leave had anything… I guess the question is when 
exactly did you leave?  What dates did you leave? 
 JM:  I would have to look up in the… 
 SM:  How about approximately? 
 JM:  In my book.  It was the summer of ’73. 
 SM:  Summer of ’73. 
 JM:  Right.  And the military had already pulled out of Vietnam and we knew the 
handwriting was on the wall. 
 SM:  Yeah.  Well did anything change after the Paris Peace Accords were signed 
in ’73 in terms of your activity in Laos? 
 JM:  Well within the company a number of things changed in that you know CAT 
had had that crash in Taiwan a few years earlier and lost the flag to China Airline and 
they cut way back on…well they just cut CA T out.  Then Southern Air started backing 
off of its operations up in Tachikawa and Yokota and started sending pilots down to 
Southeast Asia.  Some of the real, real, real senior people, people that had been flying the 
jets up there for years wound up back in Southeast Asia.  I checked out Don Peters,

 SM:  Okay.  How about the number of aircraft that were available, was there any 
shuffling or any selling of aircraft at that point? 
 JM:  You know a lot of our aircraft were bailed military aircraft.  I didn’t see any 
change in the manning of Southeast Asia up until the time I left.  When… like I say  my 
last assignment was just ferry all the VNAF airplanes that had been left all over the 
country in South Vietnam to Clark Air Force Base.  So I wasn’t even flying company 
airplanes I was flying you know aircraft that had been bailed with an Air Force.   
 SM:  Okay.  Now did you ever work with Vietnamese pilots in Laos out of 
Bangkok? 
 JM:  Vietnamese pilots?  No. 
 SM:  Yeah.  Did any transfer over at all? 
 JM:  When you say transfer over… you m ean pilots that worked for us, for Air

 JM:  No.  The third country nationals that worked for us actually you know were

been some.  We had a sub Contract, China Airlines provided two 123s they were B-
models that flew out of Saigon, but they weren’t really company aircraft.  I think we 
sponsored them in the country, but I don’t…you know, they weren’t on our seniority list 
or anything.  Now the only foreign pilot, well we had Chinese pilots, a lot of Chinese 
pilots.  Most all the first officers on the C-46s in Saigon were Chinese and the C-47.  We 
had a few Thai pilots.  In fact I trained I think the very first Thai pilot we got, there were 
two of them.  I’m trying to think of their names, one of them was Gumlarp I remember 
and then there was another real short one, dang his son was a very high official in the 
Thai government…let me think of his na me…Sompong.  Sompong was his nam e, that’s 
all I knew him by that, just Sompong.  Both of those came out…there was another one 
two, I think his name was Pradus, they came out of the Wah Hin Flying Academy, which 
was a flight school using Cessna 150s that was I believe a Thai government entity.  These

three young men had gotten their flying experience out of there and the company hired

probably twenty-five or thirty hours of flying time in the DC-3 and one of them then… 
Sompong stayed on and flew as co-pilot on the DC-3 for I’m going to say a year or more 
in Bangkok.  The other two went up to Laos and wound up flying co-pilot in the C-46s 
and I believe it was Gumlarp that was killed in a C-46 drop over lima site 32 I believe it

it to a landing strip and the nearest one that would have taken the C-46 would have been 
probably…there was a landing strip on the left side of the PDJ that would have been very 
suitable, I think that was 108, but I’m not positive, I think it was Monsui.  In route the

 SM:  Okay.  No other third country forces, Koreans…?  
 JM:  Oh there were lots of third country forces working in other jobs.  We had 
Vietnamese kickers, freight specialists.  In fact a good friend of mine he was, he lived 
right next door to me, Jim Russell used to be the chief pilot of the Beech program and 
then became the chief pilot of the C-46 program, he had a house boy that he got a job 
with Air America.  He called him Punja and that fellow flew…the whole tim e I was in 
Vietnam I know he was flying as a kicker in the 46s and I believe he flew right up until 
the end with the company.  There were lots of them in operations and traffic and 
maintenance and various other areas, but we just didn’t have any Vietnamese pilots that I 
know of.  
 SM:  Well, I guess another interesting area was can discuss real quick.  How was 
it having your family over there?  What was it like?  I mean, what were the challenges of 
having family so close? 
 JM:  If you’re going to stay there year after year after year I think it’s very 
beneficial to have your family there.  Long separations from your family are just not 
good.  When I say long I mean like, like we stayed over there for eight, ten years or

longer.  The families enjoyed it, the living in Bangkok was excellent and Sandy and the

very, very depressed at first, but the children loved it almost from the beginning.  They 
could have their horses right in the back yard where they had to go to the riding club in 
Bangkok.  The children they rode their horses throughout the area, they played with 
American children that worked for USAID, Embassy, or Air America or the customer 
and built lifetime bonds.  They go to the reunions and meet friends that they grew up with 
every year.  I think it was a unique experience.  We all became a close knit family; 
everybody seemed to know each other you know.  If you were in a big city like Bangkok 
of course you didn’t necessarily live close to each other, you could be scattered out over 
quite a big area, but in Vientiane the city was small enough that you knew just about 
everybody.   
 SM:  Did you know some of some of the riskier activities that the kids were 
engaged in? 
 JM:  Well after the fact we learned some of the riskier activities they were 
engaged in.  When we were there we didn’t realize some of the things they were doing.  I 
know the kids used to go to the Me Kong River; I’d have had a heart attack if I had 
known that because it’s a very treacherous river.  They tell about one time they got 
caught up in an old woman’s fishing net so she came after them with a cane pole.  
(laughing) They would ride their horses out, you know really out too far.  They would be 
out on the outside city limits in areas that the Pathet Lao could even be in so we were 
concerned after the fact when we learned that. 
 SM:  (laughing) When did you learn that they got fired at?  
 JM:  Oh I never know they got… I don’t be lieve they ever got shot at, I hope not, 
but I mean they were out in areas they could have been. 
 SM:  Okay.  (laughing)  Let’s see.  What about travel?  I assume that you… 
 JM:  Oh we did lots of travel.  Now the company had scheduled time off as well 
as our annual leave and home leave.  Every three years you got three months home leave 
and then you got annual leave besides.  We would do the flight scheduling at more or less 
the pilot’s…if he wanted to fly a day and be off a couple of days that was fine, if he 
wanted to do all of his flying in a relatively short block of time and then take off and get

schedule and you could leave the area.  So we would travel in Bangkok, Hong Kong,

back.  Schedule your time where you’ve finished up the end of a month and you had the

 SM:  And of course the kids were able to enjoy that as well? 
 JM:  Oh yes.  My children have feed the wallabies and the emus in Australia and 
they’ve visited the zoos in Frankfurt and they’ve done all over! 
 SM:  Now as you were… the m onths before you left and I guess really from say 
January, February until you left in ’73.  First I guess did you engage in many 
conversations with other pilots and other personnel concerning Paris Peace Accords? 
 JM:  No, not really.  We were flying in such a very small group.  Generally the 
only Air America person I saw during those last few months would be my co-pilot.  
Because of the condition of the aircraft we didn’t carry a full crew.  We didn’t have an 
airfreight specialist or a flight engineer or anybody like on board.  We carried just the 
pilot, co-pilot, with one-man life rafts and parachute with the idea we could bail out of 
those piece…their junky if they fell apart en route.  So it was just the pilot and co-pilot 
and we stayed together.  We would pick up an airplane in-country, stay with it until it 
was…you know they would assign us to an airp lane in-country and we’d stay with it until 
it was ready to be released.  We would fly it to Clark and there we would catch whatever 
transportation we could get back to Vietnam.  Sometimes it was military transport like 
the Flying Tiger line, not the freight line, but there was another outfit over there called 
Flying Tiger Line that flew government Contracts.  There was World Airways over there 
that flew government Contracts.  Continental, Pan American…whoever we could get on 
or a lot of times we would get on military aircraft.  I know one time we flew over on a 
little T-39 what they call a Scatback, the civilian version was the Saberliner.  We flew 
back on a 141 one time.  Whatever we could get on.  Then we would come into Saigon 
and be assigned to another airplane and take it back to Clark.  We might sometimes

remain in town waiting for the airplane as much as a week, sometimes we’d go out the 
very next day.  So we just lived out of a suitcase for quite a period of time there and the 
only people we came in contact with was just the two of us, the two pilots. 
 SM:  Okay.  Well, as you left what did you think about what the United States 
was doing in Southeast Asia?  In particular did you feel like you had accomplished very 
much?  What did you think was going to happen? 
 JM:  Well I can’t speak for everyone, but personally I was rather dubious that the 
South Vietnamese would be able to stand alone.  It didn’t surprise… a ctually what 
surprised me I guess is that they lasted as long as they did.  I thought that they’d be over 
under the North Vietnamese in due time, before very long.  I didn’t have any confidence 
in the South Vietnamese military at all.   
 SM:  What did you think, what did you feel when they did fall in ’75? 
 JM:  Well it was a disappointment you know.  The way I’ve always looked at it, 
you had Russia and China the large communist bloe countries supporting the North 
Vietnamese with weapons and logistics support and you can’t just allow the schoolyard 
bully to beat up on everybody without trying to stand by them like the United States has 
done so many times since World War II.  We haven’t really been very good allies for 
standing by someone when the going gets tough and we didn’t it again.  I was 
disappointed.  I’ve been disappointed in our state department, oh gosh, every since I 
traveled overseas as a civilian.  I don’t have any more confidence in them I guess then I 
had in the South Vietnamese military to be able to defend itself.  There was a time, you 
know you like to think back during World War II an American pilot could be shot down 
even in Germany sometimes and be protected or France.  Anywhere they were respected 
and taken care of and not too many years after World War II you’d be run out of town if 
they found out you were an American almost anywhere.  It’s disappointing.   
 SM:  Again as you were getting ready to leave was there a since that if South 
Vietnam fell then Laos would be close behind? 
 JM:  Well yes, we felt… the dom ino theory was I think pretty widely excepted as 
being valid.  We were afraid that they would all go.  There’s not question to me that Laos 
and Cambodia at least would go.  I had a heck of a lot more confidence in the Thais.  I 
had a lot of confidence in their Air Force and their military that they would stand and

fight.  I also had a strong feeling the U.S. would probably stand beside them although we 
haven’t been real good for standing, when the going gets tough we’re the first ones to 
break and run it seems.   
 SM:  What did you think when the United States started making progress in 
normalizing relations with Southeast Asian countries, Vietnam, Laos, Cambodia? 
 JM:  Well it’s inevitable.  I didn’t have any negative feelings about it.  I didn’t 
feel that we would ever become at least not for many, many years become real close like 
we did with the Japanese and the Germans after World War II.  But I did get back to 
Vietnam when I was with AMR.  I soon as they would allow Americans in I was over 
there.  We couldn’t write Contracts yet, it was before they would allow you to enter in 
any business agreements, but we went over to test the waters to see what the 
opportunities might be and I made several trips to both, well they were one country, but 
up north and went to Hanoi, Vinh, and to Saigon, which Ho Chi Minh City now, but all 
the locals still call it Saigon.  I made probably a half a dozen trips over a period of three 
or four years. 
 SM:  What time period was that? 
 JM:  That would have been starting about…oh gosh…probably about ’91 m aybe, 
’92, early nineties anyway. 
 SM:  Okay.  I didn’t realize you went back for these trips. 
 JM:  Oh yeah when I was with AMR I can tell you some interesting stories about 
that too.  I was with, I was a development of, I started out as the managing director of 
flight operations and I put together our certificates of all the companies and built the jet 
charter company, but then I transferred to the airline services side as the director of 
international project development and I traveled in mostly third world countries.  
Occasionally I’d get to go to a nice place like France or Pairs or London, but generally I 
was in Vietnam or Corsica or Turkmenistan or Azerbaijan or places like that in 
Vietnam… and Russia, lots and lots of traveling in Russia.  I was one of the first 
Americans to get into Petrobulam, not Petrobulam I’ve traveled there too, but into…oh 
good grief, [?] when they opened it up to the world, you know it had been a closed city 
even to the Russians.  Did a lot of traveling primarily in the Russian farie in Siberia.

 SM:  Man!  That’s really cool!  Now when you were making these trips this was 
in order to try to establish business ties? 
 JM: Yes.  What we were trying to do is establish a third party airline connections 
for various services that AMR could provide.  Like maintenance, flight operations, traffic 
passenger service, freight handling, things like that, fueling.   
 SM:  This all occurred after your time in Central and South America? 
 JM:  Yes.  I started working for AMR in November of ’87 and I got a one year 
leave of absence to fly for the war on drugs in 1989 The company let me go allegedly for 
a year, but they called my back after about eight months to be the chief pilot of the 
transport program out of Peru.   
 SM:  Well we need to talk about this too if it’s okay. 
 JM:  Oh sure. 
 SM:  This is really…you’ve gone quite a bit after Southeast Asia. 
 JM:  Well I started an aviation company for ten years.  The first thing I did I 
formed McAllen Aviation in McAllen Texas, which is a fixed base operation.  We were a 
full service, full line viper dealer.  I had everything from the, you know, the multy 
engine, nib line, and down to the little viper tomahawk.  We had a large flight school, did 
a lot of training of Mexican pilots up here.  I had what the Mexicans said was the largest 
legal 135 Mexican certificates.  I had eighteen aircraft on my Mexican charter certificate.  
We had a large maintenance department.  We were a service center naturally for viper 
and up to and including even a Cheyenne service center, which was very difficult to get 
in those days.  They had what they call a viper aircraft corporation where the only people 
authorized to be service centers, but being we were so ideally located for the Mexican 
and they were having to fly all the way to Memphis, which was the nearest one they 
allowed me to be one.  I went and bought all the equipment and parts and the train 
personnel to be a Cheyenne service center as well.  Then during those times the FAA 
designated me as a FAA pilot examiner and I gave private [?] and transport pilot ratings, 
I also gave all the written test for everything.  Anything from mechanic… 
 SM:  This will end the interview with Mr. John McRainey on the 21st of June.

nd of June 2001at approximately 10:05.  I’m in Lubbock Texas and 
Mr. McRainey’s in McAllen Texas.  Sir why don’t you go ahead and describe what it was 
like leaving both Air America and Southeast Asia for you and your family. 
John McRainey:  Okay.  I’d promised my wife, oh years earlier probably six or 
seven years earlier, that when the girls reached high school age we’d get them back to the 
states and get them in normal high school and then when the kids set off to college we’d 
go back overseas.  It was…she went hom e… Sa ndy left about a year before I did, maybe 
a little over a year.  She did come back and visit on one occasion for a couple of weeks, 
but it was a very difficult thing for me to do to resign and leave.  I sincerely hoped that in 
some future day I could come back, but it was a tough decision and it was a tough 
adjustment.  When I went back to the states I had no idea what I was going to do.  I’d 
thought about starting some business for a few years and shortly after I returned a 
gentlemen that had to the Rio Grande Valley and whom I bought a home down there 
from and several citrus groves told me that he and his wife were getting divorced and he 
wanted to know if I would buy his wife’s interest in the company, which included some 
real estate and half of a United Farm Real Estate Agency.  I told him I knew absolutely 
nothing about real estate and I knew nothing about farming and ranch, but he convinced 
me that it’d be a thing to do so I bought half of his company and I went to school and got 
my real estate license, my brokers license, even went to an extension school at Texas A 
and I in Weslaco and took several agricultural courses so that I’d now a little about the 
farm and ranch business.  I worked in the farm and ranch business for a little over a year 
and rather enjoyed it as a matter of fact, I kind of enjoyed getting out on the country… 
primarily we sold citrus groves and I had owned citrus groves for years and been an 
absentee grower and I took a citraculture course at the university and I got to the point 
where I felt very comfortable in the citrus business and so it wasn’t all that difficult to 
transition.  After about a year a FBO at the McAllen airport came up for sale, it was a 
sheriff’s sale for taxes, and myself and my partner Joe Carpenter looked into it.  We had a 
good friend; I had a friend that was a local doctor by the name of Bob Pate, who had put 
himself through medical school crop dusting and had been an Air Force pilot and a 
civilian instructor pilot for the Air Force.  He was quite an accomplished aviator himself.  
  115 

--- PDF PAGE 116 ---
 
We became very good friends and he wanted to go in on this business.  I had another 
friend that I’d sold citrus groves to, a fellow named Earl Chaffy that was very interested 
in it.  So the four of us bought a FBO.  We hired a manager for it, which was a good 
friend of mine Jerry S Jimwhatelse who was also with me throughout the Air America 
years and whom I had flown with in Minneapolis.  We were both flight instructors 
together; we both flew for the Creamets Company.  Jerry was living in Florida with his 
wife and kind of bored so he came down with his wife Poppy and became our manager.  I 
stayed in the real estate business a little longer, but Jerry’s wife really rebelled against 
south Texas.  Poppy thought that south Texas was more third world then Laos and she 
wanted to go back to Florida.  Jerry being a good husband he finally caved in and went to 
Florida with her and I found myself running the FBO.  At the time that I went over to run 
the FBO or shortly after I made a deal with my partner where I bought him out, I bought 
Joe’s interest in the FBO out and he bought my interest in the real estate company out.  
So I became the largest single owner in the FBO and we leased some additional land and 
built new buildings and about tripled the size of it and the business was going very good.  
Back in the late seventies and early eighties business in south Texas was booming!  There 
was a strong emerging middle class coming out of Mexico, there were a lot of private 
aviation in Mexico, almost every rancher and every businessman had an airplane, and I 
found our business was thriving.  I got the delusion that I was a good businessman, but 
about ’79 Earl Chaffee decided he was going to move to Florida and I bought his interest 
in the company out.  I split a small portion of it, ten percent I sold to Jack Fitch who was 
another old Air America pilot that was working for us in the FBO.  So Jack became a 
minor partner in the company.  Then it became just Jack and Bob Patet, myself and 
myself being the major investor and we ran the company up until 1984.  In 1984 a 
wealthy Mexican who had done quite a bit a business with us, he had a jet charter 
company, his name was Salvador Esqino, wanted to buy the company from us.  He 
bought and sold jet aircraft and he thought that our outlet there was the perfect vehicle for 
selling airplanes both in Mexico and the U.S.  He made us a good offer in cash and 
bought us out!  So I sold the business and thought I was going to retire in 1984, but I still 
had my pilot examiner’s ticket.  I’d been an examiner there for the whole Jim I had the 
FBO and I thought ‘well I’d give some flight checks for privates who do ATP and I

old friend of mine that I knew ran a proprietary for the CIA, Jim Ryan, and I had a 
Bonanza straight tail Bonanza.  I was living in Melbourne Florida and he told me yes that 
he did have an opening and that if I’d come up he’d show me around and we’d go 
through the processes of getting on with him.  So I flew up to his base in North Carolina.  
I looked the operation over and something about the area…it was a sm all town in central 
North Carolina only about, oh fifty miles from my hometown where I was born.  
Something about it really depressed me.  It wasn’t the same.  They say you can’t go back 
and I said “Jim you know living in this small town…” and it wasn’t an Air Am erica type 
operation.   It was primarily a support and training operation.  I said, “This isn’t what I 
had in mind.  Do you have something like the old Air America days you know where we 
worked hard and play hard and get off around the world doing something exciting?”  He 
said, “Well John there’s something that’s going on that you would fit into perfectly and I 
know the people that are running it would want you, but I have absolutely nothing to do 
with it.  If you take it you just better watch your six o’clock!”  That kind of concerned me 
a little.  I couldn’t understand why he was so adamant in telling me that he had nothing to 
do with it.  He said he’d put the people in touch with me and for me to you know…they 
would contact me in my apartment in Melbourne.  So I flew back to Melbourne 
wondering ‘what in the devil is this all about’ kind of cryptic, Jim and I have always been 
very open together [and] he wouldn’t tell me anything about the program or the project, 
but he was adamant in telling me he had absolutely nothing to do with it.  I was 
somewhat encouraged that I knew he wouldn’t even put people in touch with me if it was 
anything nefarious like I knew darn well it had nothing to do with drug smuggling or 
anything like that.  So shortly after I got back I got a call from an old friend of mine a 
fellow that I had flown with in Saigon starting in 1965, we were both in the Beech 
program together.  Later we were both IPs in the 123 program together in Laos.  Fellow 
named Bill Cooper, we called him Coop.  And Bill said, “John, tell me could you get in a 
123 today and fly it without a check list, without a manual?  Do you think you could

safely get in a 123 and ferry it out of the United States to a Central American country?”  I 
said, “Well, yeah I think I could do that, you know I had thousands of hours in a 123, but 
why would I want to do it.  I’ve got check lists and I’ve got manuals for 123s.”  He said, 
“You do.  Well get your manuals and go to Miami at Southern Air Transport, pick up a 
123 and fly it to a place called Illapango the airport in San Salvador, the capital of El 
Salvador.”  Next thing I knew I was ferrying a 123 to El Salvador and I still didn’t know 
what the devil was going on although a couple of flags were going up.  I knew that… you 
know you read the newspaper you know about the Contras and then it dawned on my 
why Jim was so adamant about saying he had nothing to do with it because the Boland 
Amendment, which was a typical garbage legislation you know you can’t get it through 
unless you tag it onto a bill to shut the country down, finally passes after being defeated 
many times.  A few years earlier and there hadn’t been any aid except non-lethal and 
going to the Contras and I’d been following that with quite a bit of interest.  So by the 
time I saw the airplane in Southern Air and met several old friends that I hadn’t seen in a 
long time from Southern Air, we had some old Air America pilots that were still flying 
down there and of course George Stubbs our maintenance man was the director of 
maintenance down there, but they didn’t have anything to do with the 123 other then 
provide Contract maintenance service on it.  I was pretty sure that that was what this was 
all about was the Contras, which didn’t bother me at all.  Politically I thought it was the 
right thing to do.  I saw a major threat in the way I evaluate it anyway, the Sandanistas 
were more or less stole the revolution from the larger part of the population and went 
communist under Daniel Ortega and immediately started building the largest army in 
Central America.  In fact their army was bigger then all the rest of the armies combined.  
He was openly saying that this was going to be a revolution without borders and I knew 
that they were re-supplying communist troops in Guatemala and Honduras and El 
Salvador and they were spreading their mischief just like Castro had all over Central and 
South America.  So I felt politically it was the right thing to do.  But anyway.  I found 
myself involved in it.  Bill Cooper, who in the SECORD’s book was called the chief pilot 
of the program, considered himself the project manager and the organizational chart that 
he drew up made me the chief pilot.  He wanted me to do the training and hiring of pilots 
and scheduling of pilots and just provided the functions of a chief pilot and he was going

to take over the management end of it and running the hostels and the rental cars and

there.  They hadn’t made a drop to the Contras in Nicaragua yet.  They were trying to get 
ready to and they had a group of pilots that had been hired through Richard Gad a retired 
Lieutenant Colonel in the Air Force that provided services for the agency as far as getting 
crews and airplanes and what have you together.  The people he hired, although there 
were some that were very good, for the most part just weren’t suitable for the job.  They 
weren’t qualified in the aircraft and they really hadn’t done any of that type of work.  One 
of them and I don’t think I should probably mention his name because he did go to work 
for the agency later on, was an excellent aviator and very competent and I had a lot of 
confidence in him.  He was also a West Point grad.  The rest with the exception of one 
fellow who’s name I will mention.  He’s a very good friend.  He wound up… I 
introduced him to my secretary in later years when I was with AMR and he wound up 
marrying her so I think we’re friends.  Ernie was an excellent aviator.  He wasn’t checked 
out on neither the caribou nor the 123, which were the primary aircraft we were using.  
We had two of each, but I didn’t have any trouble training him.  Everyone else we 
replaced primarily with ex-Air America people.  We hired… They had one gentlem en 
down there, an ex-Air Force pilot, who had brought a friend of his down too who up until 
that time had not made a drop.  Without their permission, I hate to mention their names, 
but we kept both of them as first officers and they were confident.  One of them had 
spend five and a half years in the Hanoi Hilton that’d been shot down over North 
Vietnam as a 105 pilot.  Of course they felt just like I did that this was the place to be and 
this was the right war to be involved in so they believed in what we were doing.  In fact 
that was the only reason that I believe any one of them were there.  We hired Jerry [?].  
Jerry’d been everywhere else with me so I brought him down there and Jake Raywell and 
Frank Hines.  I had a young fellow that worked for me at McAllen Aviation that had, he 
worked on the line pumping gas and parking airplanes and what have you and then he 
learned to fly and got his instructors rating and did some instructing and became a charter 
pilot.  Then he went to Oklahoma to an accelerated school and got his A& P license as a 
mechanic and came back and worked for me as a mechanic.  His reasoning was that the
