wanted to know the FBO business from A to Z because he intended to own one some 
day.  His name was David Johnson.  A real fine young man; young, had a couple of kids.  
He kept after me…He knew I was down there and he kept after me to hire him.  I finally 
hired him and regretted it almost immediately.  Not that he wasn’t very competent young 
man, but the rest of us down there were older men, we were all in our fifties or sixties in 
case of Cooper and you know it’s one thing to have a man at the end of his life get 
involved in something dangerous like that, but it’s such a waste for a young fellow with a 
couple of small children to get involved in it.  For that reason I wouldn’t let Dave fly 
south.  We had a maul, which is a short take off and landing four-place airplane that we 
used to tie the various bases together for logistics; moving people back and forth and 
small parts back and forth.  We had a base that we flew out of for all the northern drops 
called Aguacate in Honduras right down on the Nicaraguan border.  The SECORD and 
North had purchased some property down in Costa Rica and built and airstrip just south 
of the Nicaraguan border on the coast in Costa Rica.  So we were going to make that a 
major base for staging after to drop in the southern front, which was the more dangerous 
area where this fighting was going on and it was presented the biggest problem to us 
because of the distance that it was from Ilpongo down there.  So Dave flew the maul 
between those bases, moving pilots and parts and whatever was necessary back and forth 
while the rest of us made the drops.  He was really anxious to make the drops, but I 
just…I would have never forgiven myself if anything would have happened to him and 
we all agreed that what we were doing was so dangerous that it was inevitable that we 
were going to lose an airplane and a crew.  In fact just before Bill Cooper and Buzz 
Sawyer were shot down with Hasenfaf we had taken a poll.  We were playing Cameroons 
and old Southeast Asian dice game that we played.  One of the Air Force pilots asked me 
how long, he says, “Let’s take a poll.  How long do you think it’s going to be?  How long 
are we going to go before we lose an airplane?”  We’d come close so many times.  We 
had some real problems in the operation in that it wasn’t a CIA type operation where you 
had unlimited logistics and maintenance and support supplies and FIC with flights 
following and all this.  It was a bridging operation, which that’s what we were told it was 
by SECORD and his deputy, a fellow named Bob Dutton.  They were hoping that we 
could just keep the Contras alive until that Boland Amendment could be repealed, which

it had already been voted but Tip O’Neil was sitting on it on the legislation as long as he 
could.  Once it had been repealed the agency could go back in and take over and the hope 
of SECORD was that he would just sell the assets that they had there to the agency and 
then they would run it as a small proprietary and get us heaps of people in the airplane, 
like a mini Air America.  That’s what Bill Cooper had really hoped for and in fact that 
was his primary reason for being there.  So what we were trying to do is just keep the 
Contras alive, get enough of supplies down to them to keep them going until that dang 
Boland Amendment could be repealed and the agency could get involved again.  
However without the agency, without any good intelligence on where their guns were, 
where their big troop movements were, we had no way to really do any intelligent 
planning.  And it became almost a game of chance rather then a calculated risk, which is 
what we took when we were with Air America.  We had a hard time getting parts and 
support in because we had no government help in importing aircraft parts so as a result 
we’d go to Miami…in som e cases when we couldn’t get them down fast enough we 
would buy the parts, put them in out suitcase and bring them in and pay more deed say 
they were for out automobiles.  Well you’ve got a generator for an airplane you know 
how big that is, it’s obvious it’s not for an automobile, but we managed to get things in 
that way.  However there were things tied up that we needed desperately in customs that 
we never received.  As a result we flew the aircraft…we took airplane s on trips that if we 
were in Air America would have never taken off with them.  We were just coping.  We 
were trying to get the job done with the minimum of what we had.  Radios were 
terrible…SECORD spent a lot of money sending a guy down to install a civilian radio 
package in one of the 123s and they took out the military radios and put in the civilian 
radio and they were not compatible.  The…let m e see if I can give you exact 
terminology…it had som ething to do with the wiring in the airplane and then jack boxes 
that we were using that the impedents of something was different.  So what would 
happening is we could transmit clearly once or twice but every time we transmitted it got 
worse to the point where we lose our communications totally.  Then due to kind a sloppy 
job of installation, there wasn’t a trip we flew that we didn’t lose one of more of the 
navigational radios, which weren’t of a whole lot of use for us on the south end but it did 
help us to get back into Ilapongo.  Quite often at night or in bad weather you needed to

make an instrument approach so we needed those radios, but I would say more often then 
not they weren’t working.  So it was a challenge to try to get through it.  We all knew that 
everything was pretty well stacked against us and that sooner or later we were going to 
lose an airplane.  During this gallons humor where we decided…or everybody gave their 
opinions as to how long it would last, the longest was thirty days the shortest was a week 
and I think it was about eight or nine days later that we lost the airplane.  So we were 
kind of in the ballpark for when it was going to be.  We did the best we could and it 
didn’t give me the feeling or the sense of accomplishment that Air America did because 
with Air America everything was A.J. squared away and the crews were expected to be 
every bit as good as the maintenance and the logistics and the FIC and down there we had 
some excellent aviators with lots of experience but we had absolutely nothing else going 
for us.  We were like…I felt like street urchins begging for enough to get the darn…even 
money, we’d have to pay the Salvadorian Air Force cash for the fuel and getting money 
back and forth was a problem.  In fact Sandy became a money moll for us.  We’d get her 
an airline ticket to run back and forth carrying ten thousand at a time, which is the 
maximum you can legally bring out of the country without declaring it, so that we could 
make two or three trips flying.  Every time any of us were back up there for any reason 
we always brought the thousand in cash back.  Of course we had to pay for the houses 
that we rented, we had to pay for the food and of course we had cooks and maids and 
drivers for the cars and we had rental cars… they had a lot of expense and we were 
always broke.  (laughing)  It was a real challenge.  However, I never had the satisfaction 
that I did with Air America.  You felt…you know you knew you were out…another thing 
you knew you were and out and out mercenary.  We were completely outside the loop as 
far as any support from the U.S. government and we didn’t feel too good about that.  We 
felt we were doing the right thing, but we knew that we were.  That was the only thing I 
guess.  And it didn’t disturb me when Hasenfasf told everyone that he worked for the 
CIA or he thought he worked for the CIA.  I had played Cameroons with Hasenfasf 
and…you know, night after night day after day a nd we all talked about hoping that the 
agency would take over and then things would be so much better.  We’d have logistics, 
maintenance, FIC and it would be like the old days.  He knew that, we all knew it, but to 
cover his tail you know he said that he was working for the agency.  Which I didn’t

blame him for; when they’re about to stand you up against the wall you can say anything

did must have been right, don’t worry about it.  We got a lot of negative press, but don’t

However, I don’t know whether to blame Gene or his family, he wound up suing General 
SECORD; he wound up suing the Southern Air Transport; anybody that had any money I 
think and he should have won a lottery ticket for all of that.  I wound up being a witness 
for SECORD and for Southern Air against him, which I felt bad about because I had 
flown with Gene down there and of course in Laos with Air America.  I always though he 
was an excellent kicker, I always kind of liked him.  I was disappointed that we found 
ourselves on opposite sides of a courtroom.  But it was an interesting experience and I 
think we served some useful purpose.  We…well it’s a dem ocratic society and it would 
have been if it hadn’t been for the Contras so I guess all in all that I feel poor ol’ Bill 
Cooper and Buzz Sawyer who were killed never got any recognition.  They were just a 
couple of out of some Air Force general made an absolutely asinine statement to the press 
that this was a…how did he put it…oh you know like a childlike group of people that 
didn’t know what they were doing; they had information on them linking them with 
North and what have you.  Well we never tried to hide anything because we knew we 
were mercenaries and we didn’t want to implement or implicate the CIA.  We felt a sort 
of loyalty to the agency and I said absolutely we had nothing to do with it and so did all 
the other pilots, Gene was the only one who said it was a CIA operation.  If it had of been 
it would have been a hell of lot better then it was.   But the Air Force guy… You know 
when you’re flying with the latest radars and INSs and night vision goggles and 
everything else and you accomplish a dangerous mission that’s one thing, but try it on a 
Pientenpol 8 with half of the avionics gone and no navigational aids.  It’s a different 
ballgame and it takes a far superior aviator to pull it off and that idiot that’d probably 
never done a covert operation in his life had no idea what he was talking about.  In fact, 
the gal from 60-Minutes, Leslie Stall…I was listening to the hearings when they were 
interviewing Bob Dutton and Leslie Stall was doing the interview.  They showed him of 
course the actual hearings and then during the break she’d make comments.  And we call

over the isthmus and at night it was solid thunderstorms and early in the morning it was 
all fogged in.  So trying to make the drops undercover darkness there were a lot of 
negatives to it.  You like the idea that there’s some cover, but the negatives out weighed 
the pluses.  For instance, the airplanes were not the best maintained.  I’d brought back 
aircraft with feathered props; I’d drug loads all the way from southern Nicaragua back to 
Alpongo at a hundred and five or ten knots is just above a stall because I didn’t want to 
dump the load out in the middle of the jungle and brought the load back because they 
were valuable with an engine feathered or a propeller feathered.  So Dutton actually went 
along to see our problems and of course he saw them in a hurry and he was explaining 
during the hearing the problems that we had and at the break Leslie Stall scoffed, “Huh, 
thunderstorms in the day and fog in the morning.  Sounds like the gang that couldn’t fly 
straight to me!”  it was such a slap in the face to all of us and particularly to the man 
that’s dead down there who’s an old carrier pilot and had flown years for Air America; 
probably had more covert operation experience and that type of operation then probably 
any man alive.  And here’s this airhead blonde you know make these flippant remarks 
that really, really irritated me.  Every time I see her the hair stands up on my back and my 
neck.  But that was the end of the Contras operation.

that was?

SM:  Okay, good enough.  I didn’t know if you didn’t mention it because of that 
or… 
JM:  Yeah that’s the reason I didn’t mention it.

SM:  Okay, that’s what I thought.  Okay.  Let’s see.  When did you start, what 
year if you can remember the month that would be good too, when did you first ferry that 
aircraft, the first 123 to El Salvador? 
JM: It was in the early, early I’m going to say probably April maybe even March 
of ’86 and of course that shoot down was in…oh m an, I really feel bad that I can’t 
remember when Bill was killed, but it seemed to me it was September/October. 
SM:  Of ’86? 
JM:  Of ’86 yeah. 
SM:  When did you… That was your fi rst mission, was flying that 123? 
JM:  Yeah I ferried the airplane down and the next day the next mission was we 
flew up drums of fuel down to the base in Costa Rica to set it up for operation. 
SM:  Okay.  So you basically helped set up what became the support network for 
the Contras, the air support network? 
JM:  Yeah.  Of course we never really got to use that airfield very much, that’s a 
whole new story.  I could go on for hours about all those details, which would bore you to 
tears, but we… yeah we immediately started wo rking.  The next thing I did was actually 
starting to train the pilots.  I checked out Bill Cooper in the 123, not Bill Cooper.  Bill 
Cooper had already flown it, although we did fly together some.  I checked Buzz Sawyer 
out in the 123 and I checked Frank Hines out in the 123, neither one of them had ever 
flown it before.  I checked out the co-pilots in the airplane and I checked out quite a bit of 
flying.  We even did some…we had som e logistics problems that required almost 
recalculating the manual.  For one thing we didn’t have 115 and 145 fuel we had a 
hundred low lead.  We had to re-do all the power settings and then we started clogging up 
fuel lines with a material that we determined was the reticulated foam baffle that had 
deteriorated while the airplanes were sitting out in the bone yard.  They got so bad that 
they would clog the lines completely!  Of course then your jets and your recips are shut 
down and so as a temporary measure we took all of the baffle out of the airplane, which 
was a…you can’t believe what a difficult job th at was in the field.  We could only work 
through small openings and we got all those baffles out and flushed the tanks out first 
with water from the fire trucks of the Salvadorian Air Force and then with fuel and then 
we’d put it all back together.  Then we discovered that the engineers had a real reason

calling it a baffle, it was a baffle and the minute you got down to about a half a tank and 
those long bathtub, 700 gallon tanks, if you raised the nose all the fuel ran to the back of 
the tank and the nose pitched up instantly, if you lowered the nose it all ran forward and 
the nose went down.  It became a real nightmare handling the airplane so we decided that

Southern Air, reinstalled fresh reticulated foam baffles in the tanks.

here? 
JM:  We’re talking about two 123s and two C-7s.

JM:  Oh about a week or ten days. 
SM:  How much time did you spend on the ground in El Salvador, and anywhere 
else in Central America? 
JM:  Oh the months that we were there, you mean when we weren’t flying we 
were on the ground, but… 
SM:  Right. So you were there, I mean it was kind of like your with service in 
Laos.   
JM:  Oh yeah.  We ate on the local economy, we ate at the safe houses that we 
had of course, but then we would go downtown and eat at the local restaurants for a 
change of pace.  We just lived on the El Salvadorian economy and when we were in 
Aguacate we had to take almost… We ate at the military mess hall where the troops 
where training down there, where they were training counter troops and the food was 
terrible.  So we would take things like canned sardines and crackers and peanut butter and 
bread and stuff like that and that’s what we would eat when we were down there. 
SM:  Now how long did you stay total in El Salvador? 
JM:  Well I’m gonna say probably six or seven months in-country. 
SM:  Okay, so from March until the end of ’86? 
JM:  Well I was down there after the shoot down.  If you recall there was a lot of 
press about the 123.  I’d flew another 123 up to Southern Air to get an engine changed 
and they had gotten the engine changed, but then of course the shoot down came and the 
airplane was sitting on the ramp and of course it got into the press and the press is out 
  126 

--- PDF PAGE 127 ---
 
there taking the pictures and they were saying that Southern Air was an old CIA 
proprietary.  It was really embarrassing to President of Southern Air Bill Lincoln who 
wanted that airplane the hell out of there, which I don’t blame him.  So we went up to get 
the airplane, but no body knew where the heck it was going to go.  At first we were told 
to take it up to a small strip, a general aviation airport in Pennsylvania where the guy that 
was kind of the logistic, who made the payrolls and what have you…I can’t think of his 
name…but he called his com pany Corporate Air Services.  We were going to park it at an 
airport of his up there and I said, “You know, if you’re trying to be low key parking a 
military type transport on a small general aviation airport, that’s going to be, there’s 
going to be the press all over it almost instantly and the local police and everybody else.  
That’s going to stand out like a sore thumb.”  They agreed and said, “Well, let’s take it 
back to Aguacate and leave it there.”  So now we were under so much scrutiny from the 
FAA, from the state department, from everybody was on our case.  So we decided what 
we’d do is we’d sneak out to the airport about two o’clock in the morning and take off for 
Aguacate, but I had this Air Force pilot friend with me on that trip.  I said, “Well what 
ever we do we’ve got to do it 100% legally!  We got to file a legal flight plan.”  Now 
Aguacate didn’t have any identifier to even file a flight plan to you, it was just a strip 
built by the military down there along the dirt.  It was seven thousand foot dirt strip.  So 
we had to pick the nearest airport to it that had a legal identifier that we could file an 
international flight plan on and say we were going there and then when we got down 
there we would call the local center and cancel out and go on direct to Aguacate.  But I 
wanted to make sure that everything was done legally and of course I put my legal name 
on the flight plan and then of course that was all over the newspapers immediately 
because it was public record.  We went out to the airport early in the morning and I had a 
light colored sport jacket as we were heading to the airplane, I thought ‘geeze this thing 
will get greasy and dirty and all we’re going to do is leave it and come back 
commercially.’  I had my commercial airline tickets in my pocket and I had my passport, 
but John and I both decided we didn’t want to wear our sport jacket so we went back to 
my car and left our sport jackets in the car.  Well his passport and tickets were in his 
flight kit, my passport and tickets were in my jacket!  That was a major problem, I left 
them in the back of my car in the parking lot of Southern Air.  We get in this 123 and fly

it to Aguacate and when we get down there we were met by Dave Johnson in the mall to 
ferry us over to Ilopango where we’re going to get on a commercial flight and go back to 
the states.  When I realized I didn’t have a passport or tickets was when Dave says, “Well 
where are your bags and stuff.”  I had just a little overnight shaving kit thinking one 
night, I said, “This is all I’m carrying.  We’re going right back.”  He says, “Well, okay.  
You’ve got your tickets and everything.”  Then it hit me like a ton of bricks!  My god I 
don’t have any airline tickets and worse then that I don’t have a passport and here I am in 
the country.  I came in on a general deck, which is legal, but now I’m going from this 
country to El Salvador…I’ll be in E l Salvador illegally.  We pondered that and I thought 
‘well, no options now.  What I’ll do is go to the embassy when I get there and tell them 
my stupid story and see if they can get me a letter to get me out.’  So we got in the mall 
with David and flew to Ilopango and when we landed at Ilopango I thought the world’d 
came to an end.  They had that horrible earthquake down there that killed hundreds and 
hundreds of people.  You might recall it in ‘86; the city of El Salvador was almost 
destroyed.  Clouds of dust building up in the air, the roads were disrupted; I mean it was a 
mess, both of our safe houses just crumbled into rubble.  The house, the building, a 
bodega we called it, a shed that we stored all the ammunition and arms and stuff in was 
partially collapsed, the ammunition and everything was scattered all over, but it was a 
place to get you in out of the weather.  So we all spent the night in that bodega and now, 
we said, “Well now I’ve got a reason for not having a passport.”  The house is destroyed, 
the U.S. Embassy is destroyed, we just bought some airline tickets I had the cash and 
went to the airport and played dumb. I told them you know, “My passport is gone.”  
“Well go to the embassy.”  “The embassy’s destroyed!”  I made a nuisance of myself and 
the immigration people said, “Go on through!”  And they let me through.  So I managed 
to get out of there without a passport. 
SM:  Did you guys lose anybody in the earthquake? 
JM:  No.  We didn’t lose anybody in the earthquake.  They all had the hells scared 
out of them.  Jerry [?] told a horror story, said they thought that house was falling down 
around them as he ran out in the yard.  But no, we all got out of it with no injuries.  It was 
an exciting period of time, I wouldn’t want to relive it though. (laughing)

SM:  Well, while you were doing all of this, when you moved to Florida I assume 
that you moved with Sandy and the family.   
JM:  Well you know, I don’t know if Sandy told you this, but Sandy and I were 
divorced for several years.   
SM:  Oh no, I didn’t know that. 
JM:  Oh yeah, Sandy and I were divorced during this whole period of time, in fact 
long before I sold McAllen Aviation.  But like I told you that she was bringing money 
down to us, we had started dating again.  But one of the reasons you know I was always 
gone in aviation totally and here I am doing the same thing, but Sandy was a gutsy little 
broad, she’d come down there and bring them money and would stay in a nice hotel in El 
Salvador and it was kind of like the old times I think that brought us together.  And well 
in fact we decided we were going to get remarried during that Contra deal.  We remarried 
on the same day that we were married the first time.  We were married in September 4, 
1954 the first time and we were married in September 4, 1987 the second time. 
SM:  Now was that done to make it easier for you? 
JM:  Well we thought that we’d keep track of our dates that way.  I could never 
forget my anniversary. 
SM:  (laughing) That’s what I mean. 
JM:  Yeah that’s funny. 
SM:  I didn’t realize that, but it’s nice that you guys… 
JM:  Yeah it all worked out fine and we’re back together and we just had a 
four/five year vacation from each other. 
SM:  Yeah.  Now when did you, let’s see, concerning your time again down in El 
Salvador Central America did you work…as far as when you spent time of the ground 
was it pretty much exclusively El Salvador? 
JM:  Yeah.  We did spend a week at a time at the ranch, I mean not the 
ranch…Aguacate.   
SM:  But there was a ranch down there too right? 
JM:  Well we call that the ranch.  Yeah the ranch…no I think that, yeah I think we 
called Aguacate the ranch and I think we called the strip in Costa Rica the plantation.

SM:  Well who else did you train besides U.S. pilots?  Did you train any other 
foreign national pilots? 
JM:  No.  Now there was a book written by a guy named Terry Reed who claims 
that he trained Contra pilots and that he knew me and he knew Bill Cooper and that he’d 
met with us and we’d partied and all that…There’s not a word that he said that’s true.  
Time Magazine checked him out and said that he’s got a hell of a story to tell, but the 
only bad thing about is that not a word of it is true.  He’s a pathological liar.  This book 
has gotten wide dissemination.  I’ve had friends call me from all over and say they’d read 
about me in that book and of course that we were involved in drug smuggling is what he 
claims.  But I never met the man.  What he did…there was what th ey called a chronology 
of events on the Contra deal.  We were in every newspaper, magazine, ah god every time 
I turned around I saw my name in a publication some place.  To put all this together 
somebody wrote a paperback book called A Chronology of the Contra Affair and it listed 
every time anything about the Contras appeared in a newspaper or a magazine or a book.  
It was listed in there.  So a person could take that chronology, read through it and get 
enough out of it about all the major players like Bill Cooper and myself, Ramón Medina, 
Felix Rodriguez, all the central players…and he wove this…I think the guy was kind of a 
Walter Mitty spy wanna-be.  He convinced his wife he was training Contra pilots and 
flying missions for the CIA…I’m  going to have to say this is my opinion, but I believe 
the man was a drug smuggler.  The reason I say that is that his allegedly very good friend, 
was the drug smuggler who had been convicted and was killed in New Orleans at a half-
way house and who owned the first 123 that SECORD managed to get for our operation.  
Now that man flew one mission for the agency.  The agency on a plea bargain with him, 
the DA and the agency got him to fly a trip to Nicaragua with hidden cameras in the 
airplane filming high-level Sandinista people loading drugs in that airplane to prove that 
the Sandinistas were in the drug smuggling business not the Contras.  That was a 
successful mission and for that I don’t know whether they were going, they probably 
going to give a few years off his sentence, but the guy was going away for a long time 
there was no question about that.  This Terry Reed claimed that this guy was his best

wouldn’t have smuggled drugs for their own gain not to sponsor the Contra war, 
would’ve surprised me.  Evidently he got a lead on some of them and it wouldn’t be too 
hard find out who all they were they file flight plans and what have you and he did some 
checking and he said that every Contra pilot was a Class A cocaine smuggler.  Now 
whether I can’t say that all of those Sandanista pilots were involved in drugs, but that 
maybe some of them were and possibly all of them I wouldn’t be a bit surprised.  But 
now he thinks that this is the entire Contra operation and that it’s CIA run.  Now those 
guys would occasionally come up to Ilopango for R and R and they would park their 
airplanes at the CIA ramp or the CIA hanger and you know being that the airplanes had 
been given to them by the government it wouldn’t surprise me that even though the 
agency wasn’t supposed to have anything to do with the operation at that time, it 
wouldn’t surprise me that they would keep the airplanes for safe keeping.  But anyway, 
with this information he determined that all Contra pilots were drug smugglers.  Well you

number of independent Central South American and American companies to fly non-

but to fill up the warehouses in Aguacate and then Colonel Bermudas’s group would or 
us would drop them into Nicaragua.  Well all of these groups became CIA Contra groups 
and they definitely proved in congressional investigations that some of these groups had 
been involved in the drug trade.  One of them landed a DC-4 at Homestead Air Force 
base and got caught.  But they were flying a non-lethal aid into Aguacate and then they 
were going of and doing their own thing and getting what ever they did afterwards.   
SM:  Basically they were using flying the non-lethal aid in its air cover for their 
drug activities. 
JM:  This guy, Terry Reed, claimed that they had a secret airstrip just outside of 
Mina Arkansas, not the Mina main airport, but just outside it that they trained the Contra 
pilots and they named all the people from our operation that were up there helping and 
there was no training going on… W hy in the hell would we train pilots in Mina, Arkansas 
when we could train them at Ilopango or Aguacate for crying out loud?  It was just 
absolute nonsense.  Then he said that the reason that the agency got him in the first 
place… he’d obviously worked in machine manufacturing in some little way and he 
became a manufacturing expert and that they hired him in Mina Arkansas to take parts 
from the Mina army guard for M-16s and he would manufacture the serial number pieces 
like the receivers, use the guard parts and combine them, and make weapons for the 
Contras and then that we would fly drugs in the 123s, pick up the weapons and take those 
back to Central America.  I mean it was so far out.   Number one, I dropped a lot of 
weapons to the Contras, I never dropped anything but AK-47s.  They were bought on the 
international market and they used them because that’s what the Sandinistas used and if 
you’re fight a guerilla war it’s real nice to be able to use the enemies ammunition and the 
enemies weapon.  So all the guns that were dropped were AKs, there was never a M-16 
down there.  They guy is just full of it right up to his ears.  This guy Castillo who might 
have seen some of these guys, he lumped all the Contras together.  He lumped the North

down there trying to catch the bad guys, he gets the lead in on a few bad guys and learns

the CIA that’s sponsoring it and the next thing you know he’s blown it into a big 
conspiracy and he literally can’t see the forest for the tree.  The man was not a very good 
investigator and here he is still…every tim e, about once a week there’s a letter to the 
editor in the McAllen paper that this idiot has written about some political thing.   
SM:  Did you ever met Oliver North? 
JM:  Only met Oliver North one time and that was at a book signing long 
afterwards.  I introduced myself and he knew me immediately and we talked a little bit 
and asked about Dick Secord and…  I wish him well, you know, I didn’t know Ollie from 
Adam.  I have to say one thing for the guy he sure got things done though.  He captured 
the terrorist in that incident that almost ran into a major incident in Italy where they land 
in the Italian kept the Americans from capturing the terrorist, but he did things anyway 
whether… I know they call him  a lose cannon, but god damn I’m so tired of these arm 
chair generals that won’t do a god dang thing that when you find a lieutenant colonel that 
will do something it’s heartening.   
SM:  How about Richard Secord?  How well did you know him? 
JM:  Oh I’ve known Dick for along time. I consider him a friend.  He’s an 
honorable man, he did what he thought was right, he saw there was some money to be 
made, but my god Dick Gad and all kinds of people that had been helping the U.S. 
Military in the peripheral made lots of money I don’t see any reason why he shouldn’t 
have made some money doing something that was really good for the country.  Of course 
he never did it just cost him money. 
SM:  Well you mention that obviously the CIA maintained a hanger there, more 
then likely at the time they needed to keep their hand in things to a degree. 
JM:  Their job was to keep track of what was going on.  They knew everything 
that we were doing, but they weren’t helping us or aiding us in any way.  In fact the guys,

pilot.  They were going to fuel out of the ranch out of Costa Rica, but they got down there 
and they had real high oil consumption in one engine and right after making the drop they 
had to feather the propeller.  In the middle of the night, in nasty weather, they though 
well the only option they had at that time was to go into…oh hell the capital of Costa 
Rica…anyway they went into th e international airport down there.  Now they had this in 
emergency you can contact this guy at the embassy and the guy was the CIA station 
chief…let m e think of what his name was… if you follow, all you have to do is look in 
the Contra things he was the only one that got his retirement and everything else.  The 
poor guy was doing his job just as a citizen and as a state department and nothing else.  
They came in there with an airplane with an emergency and he arranged to get them fuel 
and turn them around and get them out of there.  Now you’d do that for any American.  
Well unfortunately these guys were Contras and it cost him his job.  I felt bad, I felt sorry 
for him.  Poindexter, everybody else got off, but not him.  
SM:  Why did they choose him to be the scapegoat? 
JM:  Well you know here they had these big senate hearings; they’ve got to have a 
coonskin on the wall as old LBJ used to say.  They needed…som ebody had to pay for 
this.  It came down to probably lowest guy on the totem pole that had the least to do with 
the whole thing.   
SM:  All the responsibility, but no authority.   
JM:  Yep, yep. 
SM:  What…in term s of the CIA and how they were still participating or active 
in… 
JM:  Well in the early days they used to actually make the drops before the 
Bolling Amendment.

JM:  They would monitor what the heck what was going on, I mean it’s their job 
to do that, but they didn’t take any active participation. 
SM:  Now how much do you think, how much of a role do you think they played 
in direction and then also providing you guys with information?  How would you know 
where to make drops? 
JM:  Well we did that all internally.  We did it…Colonel Bermudas was the head 
of all the friendly forces in Nicaragua and he would radio…It’s funny you should ask that 
question because when I was called in for hearings the actual operational people was 
closed door hearing and we took our depositions from an army of government lawyers in 
private so that we wouldn’t compromise any friendly foreign governments that might 
have helped us, who did help us along the way.  So they were in private, but one of them 
was…a particular night drop they were intere sted in and they thought this guy in Costa 
Rica gave the coordinates; I got my coordinates from Felix Rodriguez.  Now Felix was 
retired from the CIA and he wasn’t with them, he was working for the Salvadorian Air 
Force tracking down terrorist.  That was his big deal, he developed a helicopter anti-
terrorist operation in Vietnam that he used in El Salvador and very successfully.  He 
managed to capture one of the real head terrorists that was smuggling arms and 
ammunition into the communist troops in El Salvador.  A real gutsy guy and fiercely anti-
communist, he lost his country to Castro and he hates communist with a passion.  But he 
lived in a little BOQ in the Air Force base, they gave him the rank of Lieutenant Colonel 
in Salvadorian Air Force and he had permission from the U.S. government to do this.  In 
fact you know he had had a couple of conversations with the vice-president Bush at that 
time and of course the hearings tried to make that out that what he was talking to Bush 
about was the Contras, but it wasn’t, it was about his anti-communist operation down 
there.  Everything he did for us he did…of  course he’d of helped anybody that was 
fighting the communist, he’d of helped the Attila the Hun, but he knew what we were 
doing and he was there to help in any way he could.  He sat up in his COQ every night 
and monitored the HF radio incase we got in trouble down there we could call him and 
tell him what was going on.  He met the airplane, when I’d come in whether it was the

SM:  Well you know, just talking about I guess the behind the scenes role that 
CIA played and of course where you got your information. 
JM:  Oh, no, no, no.  We never got out information, if we’d had gotten 
information from the CIA I don’t think we’d every lost that airplane.  We never knew 
anything, they didn’t give us any intelligence briefings at all.  The only thing we would 
get was from what Colonel Bermudas would learn and he had some secret sources, he 
managed to get a little bit, he could tell us where some of the radar sights were, what 
altitude we could go to without getting picked up, but there was no real good intelligence.  
Bermudas told me one time to really be careful crossing the river and never cross at the 
same time because I made a trip to his little fortress, which was actually south of 
Aguacate, in fact Felix RodriguEASY flew me in there in a helicopter.  Bermudas was 
worried about us on the southern front because they really were geared for bear down 
there and he said be careful coming across the river because my intelligence tells me 
they’re floating anti-aircraft guns on barges and positioning them on the Nicaraguan side 
all along the river and as you come across you can get shot down so make sure that you 
never cross at the same place and then when you cross come back in a different place.  Of 
course that was good intelligence and we certainly did that, but that’s about the only kind 
of intelligence we ever got.   
SM:  Okay.  And how about interaction with CIA personnel?  Anything at all? 
JM:  Nothing, absolutely nothing.  Oldest and closest friends ignore us.  
SM:  Okay.  How about after the fact? 
JM:  Oh afterwards, yeah it’s an open book after the fact.  We were all buddies 
again. 
SM:  Right, okay.  How much shooting occurred during these operations?  When 
you were flying these missions how often would you get shot at? 
JM:  Well we would try our darndest of course to use the cover of clouds as much 
as possible.  We tried…we’d pick our way fr om one thunderstorm to another.  It’s the 
only time in my life I’ve deliberately looked for thunderstorms so that we wouldn’t be 
venerable to ground fire because we were particularly worried about the SAMs, the

they were just operatives of the agency had hired on a Contract basis to do various things.  
One of them was a very, very interesting guy, his name is Raphael Quintero and if you

so ‘heck, Carlos the Jackal I’ll sleep well at night after doing that job!’  He took the job.

was looking for him.  Now this is in the book either I think it was

got out and waged war against Castro from then on and spent a number of years in a 
Venezuelan prison.  Never convicted of it, but accused of blowing up the Cuban airliner 
that killed I think it was a soccer team that was on the darn thing.  But he was down there 
and he worked…oh he did a num ber of things, translating, working on the safe houses, 
riding herd on the cooks and the maids and things like that for us.  Then of course we had 
local people doing the cooking, doing the cleaning, driving the automobiles and what 
have you that were Salvadorian.  But Ramon and Raphael were both Cuban, those were 
the only third country national that we had worked with.  Oh wait a minute now, we did 
use a radio operator for translation to help find out way to the drop zones that were 
provided by Colonel Bermudas that were either El Salvadorian or Nicaraguan.  They 
were considered Contra soldiers.

JM:  Well the Americans that worked with us we all knew through the grapevine 
through the Air America Association.  We’d all known each other for years like Bill 
Cooper… Now one of the ones that was really close to us that wasn’t ex-Air America

people both of them.  They’re both retired colonels and patriots. 
SM:  Okay.  Well, how did you get paid and by who? 
JM:  We got paid by…that was Bill Coope r’s job to get the money.  The guy that 
would give us a base paycheck was corporate air services would send it to Bill Cooper 
and Bill Cooper would give them out, but the actual pay for making the drops was made 
in cash by Bill Cooper. 
SM:  And the money came from? 
JM:  Well it came from SECORD’s office.  He set up a bank account and he setup 
one bank account right in El Salvador, but we could only get Salvadorian money we 
couldn’t get U.S. green on that one, but we did use that for rental cars and things of that 
nature.  But the cash would all be brought down like Sandy and her money moll days.  
All of us would bring ten thousand apiece drawn on a bank account at the general setup 
in Miami.

established?  I mean did you…how did…?  
JM:  Well it was very, very lose.  Almost all of us were… At that tim e I had just 
sold my business and I considered myself independently wealthy until I found out how 
fast money can go.  (laughing)  Money was the last thing that was on my mind.  When we 
got down there we did sign an agreement with I think it was called Corporate Air

SM:  What were you told about secrecy and confidentiality? 
JM:  Well you know naturally we were going to go out and tell the world what we 
were doing down there, but the world knew and we really didn’t, we weren’t very tight 
about it.  You know if it had really been conscious of careful about it we would never 
have carried any identification with us or anything like that, but we did.  We carried our 
normal billfolds and our… In fact we had ta lked about what you would say… I was q uite 
upset that they had captured Hasenfaf.  He was close to the border, I mean my god in one 
day he’s walk and he’d had been in Costa Rica and we could’ve flown down and picked 
him up, but he hung around the crash site.  He made himself a hammock and hung around 
there until they captured him and it’s like he was in shock or something.  I was really 
disappointed in him in that respect.  He was an ex-Marine pathfinder, if anybody ought to 
have been able to escape and evade he shouldn’t have been able to and if had headed 180 
straight south for oh I’m going to say 25-30 miles, my god he could have done that in a 
day.  He’d had been at the river, cross the river you’re in a neutral country and then go to 
any hotel, restaurant, or what have you and make a phone call to El Salvador and we’d be 
down there within a few hours to pick him up.  He didn’t do that.  In his defense, he was 
very, very close to Bill Cooper.  Bill was like a father to him and had been seen the 
Laotian days and seeing his friend go down in flames like that I’m sure that got to him. 
SM:  While we’re on the subject, why don’t you go ahead if you will describe the 
circumstances of the crash.  What was that mission in particular for?  Was the crash site 
ever investigated?  Did they ever determine why the aircraft…? 
JM:  Well we know why the airplane crashed.  It was hit by a surface air missile. 
SM:  Okay, that will do it. 
JM:  Yeah.  It was hit in the right engine and it obviously probably came right up 
the stack of the recip and blew the engine apart and the propeller ran away.  When 
Hasenfaf explained it to me he said, “It sounded like a World War II movie,” you know 
that loud whine you get.  Well that’s a runaway prop and the airplane of course loaded

heavy and he was fairly low altitude.  I believe he was only, he was going into the drop 
zone, he was only two or three thousand feet and didn’t have the jets on the line yet.  I 
had a firm…I taught all my pilo ts never to descend below to five thousand feet without 
starting the jet because if you do need it, and that’s where you’re going to need it, it takes 
too long to get them on the line and you might not have time.  That was a firm rule that 
we had in Laos too, we always started the jets descending through five thousand feet.  We 
were critically short on fuel on a lot of trips because we had to do a little searching for 
DZs and what have you and of course with the jets burning you’re burning a lot of gas 
down there low.  But Bill was so concerned about fuel that he’d hold off to the last 
minute.  He’d be almost in the drop pattern before he’d start the jet.  Of course if you get 
then you’re not going to have time to get them on the line.  Now with a runaway prop and 
a heavy load the airplane was going down at a fairly steep angle and according to 
Hasenfaf they were both busier the hell.  You could see the hands flying around the 
cockpit, they were trying to get those jets started, but they obviously didn’t get them 
started before it hit the ground.  Now if the jets would have been on the line he could’ve 
climbed the airplane out of there, slowed it down as slow as he could fly and maintained 
altitude and flown in on into Costa Rica and made an emergency landing, but without the 
jets you’re going down hill and you’re going down hill fairly fast.  So that was just one of 
those things, the ground caught him before he got the jets on the line.  I don’t imagine 
they were…You know it wasn’t an uncontrolle d crash, but it was a crash in a heavily 
forested area so they certainly didn’t survive.

JM:  Well Hasenfaf bailed out.

JM:  Oh yeah he was getting ready to make a parachute drops and actually had his 
parachute on.  He just went to the other door and jumped out right across from where the 
fire was coming in. 
SM:  Oh goodness.  Okay.  Now do you know what they were carrying? 
JM:  They were carrying ordinance and weapons.

he darn sure had been shot at before, and he was very confident kicker/load master…he 
was a good man, but he for some reason or another he didn’t…the training wasn’t not 
intense enough to be rote with him otherwise he’d a got out of that area A.S.A.P.   
SM:  So the missile took out the engine, didn’t take off the wing? 
JM:  No, no.  It hit the engine and of course the airplane was on fire, there was a 
lot of flames coming out, it’s obviously fuel line had been severed and I’d imagine the 
whole accessory section probably separated from the engine and the propeller was just 
spinning free and he had a bad situation.  Now if the jets would have been on the line he 
could have climbed out of there, gone to cross feed, dropped the nacelle tank and the fire 
would have gone out and flown to Costa Rica.  But sometimes you’re more concerned 
about running out of fuel or getting shot down I guess you know, whatever you think 
most likely to happen. 
SM:  Now when did you find out the specifics of the crash and that fact that…?  
JM:  About where they were hit and by what?  When Gene Hasenfas called me 
after he got out of prison just right around Christmas time. 
SM:  Christmas of 19? 
JM:  86.

JM:  I was back in my apartment in Melbourne, yeah.

JM:  No that was the end of the operation we didn’t do anymore flying, they shut 
it down.  I was…when the airplane w as actually shot down I was in Florida with, I had 
flown the airplane up there the press was photographing so intensely at Southern Air.  
Sandy had brought money down to us and was going to go back with me on that airplane 
when I was ferrying it back for an engine change and the night before we were supposed 
to go back we got an emergency call.  Raphael Quintera and Felix Rodriguez called me at 
the hotel and said that they had this emergency drop and they had called Bill and Bill was 
not feeling good and he said to call me at the hotel and that they needed an air drop as 
soon as possible and a location on the southern front and could I make it.  I said, “Well 
yeah, I can make it.”  Sandy was there with me and I said, “Well, you can stay with the 
guys at safe house during the next day and watch the,” this was Saturday I believe and the 
Cowboys were playing the next day on Sunday, “so you can watch the Cowboys game on 
satellite T.V.”  So she went to the house with Bill Cooper and all the…she knew them  all.  
She knew Bill, and Jerry, and Jack Weerel from Southeast Asia days.  So went to watch 
the Cowboys game with them and then the other, the two colonels I took one of them.  
The first time I had ever flown with him, the one that had been a prisoner of war.  We 
went out to the airport real early in the morning to make the drop as soon as possible and 
we found we had a back jug.  The airplane didn’t check out, we kept messing with it…the 
weather was bad that day, it was raining and we didn’t get off the ground until…oh gosh, 
three or four o’clock in the afternoon I guess when we finally got airborne.  But it was 
important that they get the stuff so we kept at it until we got the airplane flyable and we 
took off and flew down there.  We made the drop, we saw the signal fires they had and 
we dropped on them and by the time we got back of course it was really getting late and 
was raining and the weather was nasty, and we were minimum fuel.  I remember that last 
night was a bad night and of course Sandy and all the people were really worried at the 
safe house because we should’ve been back hours ago, they didn’t know about all this 
maintenance problems on the airplane.  When we finally got in old Felix, the good ol’ 
guy he is was waiting along the runway and he drove us home so everybody was glad to 
see us.  The next morning Sandy and I were going to fly the 123 back and I started having

SM:  What kinds of things typically would get tied up? 
JM:  Oh you know major engine parts.  I remember we didn’t have an APU for 
one of the airplanes, you know they had an engine, an auxiliary engine that we could start 
and get a 300 amp generator on the line to help start the recips.  That damn thing went out 
and of course it’s big and bulky and we couldn’t get it in the country in a suitcase so we 
tried to get it through customs and the thing was shipped down there and I bet it’s still in 
customs to this day.  We could never get it cleared through, they wouldn’t let us have it. 
SM:  Did you ever encounter any kind of corruption, black marketeering, 
anything like that? 
JM:  No I can’t say that I did.  It could’ve existed except that the commader of the 
airbase was a close personal friend of Felix Rodriguez, his name was General…oh heck, I 
think he was about the number two or three ranking person in the whole country under 
the president…Bastille, General Bastille.  He seemed to be a pretty straightforward guy.  
He, through Felix’s efforts he got a security pass to get in and off the base without getting 
shut down all the time.  There was one brief period when there was a little growl between 
Felix Rodriguez and General SECORD that they shut down the operation, but Bastille 
wouldn’t let us fly, wouldn’t let us on the base.  I always managed to be neutral.  I found 
out throughout the years you don’t get too factionalized it you want to get along in a third 
world country.  I mean I stayed friends with the general, I stayed friends with Felix, and I 
stayed friends with General Bastille, and Colonel Bermundas, and all of them that I had 
to work with.  Old Bill Cooper got sidewise with Felix and that was a big mistake.  They

JM:  Yeah.  Yeah.

were the hazards like compared to the…that is the hazards in El Salvador and flying for 
the Contras versus the hazards you faced in Laos flying for the Royal Lao Forces? 
JM:  Well the thing that made it much more hazardous was that our equipment 
wasn’t maintained the way it was in Laos and of course it was a lot older then it was 
when we were in Laos and we didn’t have the FIC, that’s the Flight Information Center, 
to give us you know all the detailed reports to where the enemy were and we didn’t have 
the logistics to get the parts that we needed you know and the equipment we needed so 
that was a problem.  Everything combined you know and not having 115/145 fuel, which 
didn’t even manufacture anymore.  There were just…there was so many things that we 
had to cope with that we didn’t have to cope with in Laos.  In Laos we had everything as 
much in our favor as possible, which was a very comforting feeling and all we had to do 
was go out and fight the bad guys and the weather.   
SM:  And you got all the intelligence that was available.

JM:  I didn’t know anything about it, I didn’t know anything about it until after 
the fact.  We knew they were working on other things.  Bob Dutton I know was busier 
then hell and I kind of got on his case one time when he was down there you know and he 
made the drop with me.  I said, “For crying out loud you know, our logistics is lousy, and 
this is lousy.”  He said, “We’re doing the best we can.  You know we’re not the 
government anymore it’s not like I was in the Air Force and can get us through channels 
and then we’ve got a lot of other things we’re working at.”  It’s the first time I realized 
that we’re doing something other then the Contra deal.   
SM:  Well and how about U.S. Military, did you ever encounter any U.S. Military 
personnel down there? 
JM:  Well yes.  There was a colonel down there that was very pro our operation 
and knew what we were doing and even came and briefed us about terrorism in the city 
that we had to look out for; where they bad areas where and what have you.  
Unfortunately I think he was career was ruined because early in the operation before 
SECORD could get all the Air America people down there the Gad people hadn’t made a 
drop and they were really desperate.  So Gad set up a drop for a couple of Southern Air 
pilots that kind of like went on vacation and were hired by him and a Southern Air 
airplane that was leased by him, went down and made an airdrop in a 130.  This colonel 
who was in charge of the military assistance/El Salvador, went on board checked the load 
and what have you and that was a big mistake on his part because I understand he was on 
the fast track to general and after that I think he got a post in Nome, Alaska.  That kind of 
ruined his career.

JM:  There could have been, but I didn’t see them.  In fact I know Green Berets

SM:  Yeah I didn’t know if maybe you had contact.  Well, speaking of that, did 
you guys ever drop people? 
JM:  Well see, we were briefed pretty well.  SECORD and Dutton hired lawyers 
to checkout what we could do and what we couldn’t do to become in violation of the 
Neutrality Acts and none of us wanted to lose our citizenship.  We knew on this one that 
we were mercenaries and we had to be very careful.  Now we could drop supplies in there 
all day and that wasn’t in violation of the Neutrality Act believe it or not, but drop one 
person in there and you are in violation.  Or set foot in there yourself to aid the people 
that are in there and you’re in violation of the Neutrality Act.  So it would’ve been shot 
down.  My modus operandi I always wore a pair of dark street slacks and a long sleeve 
white shirt and I said I’m just going to get out of the airplane, pick up a bible, and I’m 
suddenly going to become a missionary and walk to the border. 
SM:  Okay.  Were you ever threatened?  You mentioned that this military officer, 
the Army officer colonel advised you of some of the hazardous places.  Did you guys 
ever encounter any dangerous threats or…?  
JM:  No.  I can’t say that we did.  I was downtown with Buzz Sawyer and a 
couple of the other guys one night when we were having dinner and a jealous boyfriend 
took exception to Buzz.  Other then that we never had any trouble with the local people 
down there.   
SM:  Or how about government officials? 
JM:  Well outside of being kept off the airport. 
SM:  Yeah that one time.

ridiculous things.  Like Terry Reed saying we were flying drugs up to Mina Arkansas and 
gun back from Mina.  Well number one a 123 would have to make at least two tech stops 
en route, wouldn’t be able to do it non-stop and the ridiculous thing is that we didn’t use 
M-16s down there and dang we had better things to do then haul drugs to Mina Arkansas.  
So he got a Newsweek guy and I agreed to talk with him.  There was a gentleman and I 
wish I’d had talked to him because he was by far…I’d held the press in d isdain over the

ground school in DC-8 and basic in doc and…another old Contin ental pilot, Steve 
Stevens was with us and towards the end of our program another Air America pilot, 
Dendsley, Ben Dendsley came in and it was kind of like old home week.  They already 
ha… Pete P arker was flying for them and oh let’s see who else…There were Jose 
Konda…there were several Air Am erica pilots there so it was kind of like old home 
week.  Anyway, during the period I was flying with Rosie and old friend that I’d known 
for years was in the FBO business and knew me well…called m e and asked me if I would 
like to go to work for American Airlines.  They were starting a chain of FBOs, some 
fixed base operations around the country and he wanted somebody to work with him.  He 
was the vice president in charge of it and he wanted a director to go out and find the 
FBOs and help get them into the system and train and standardize and so forth.  So about 
that time I, Sandy and I were already planning on getting married and she of course 
would’ve felt a lot better about me not being in the cockpit flying around the world, the 
idea of an executive job and living in one place really appealed to her.  So we…I left 
Rosenbaum in November of 1987 and went with AMR Corp.  They formed a…I was 
working for a division called AMR services, which did ground handling for third party 
airlines and ACSs so for American in some cases.  They were the ones that bought AMR 
Combs.  They bought the Combs Gates operation, which I believe there were six FBOs 
combs and I came in just before they made that acquisition and so we became known as 
AMR Combs.  After about a year, say by ’88 anyway, late in ’88, the chairman, president 
and chairman of the board Bob Crandall expressed his concern that the company was 
acquiring all these independent ma and pa charter operations around the country.  We had 
one in, we bought Northern Air in Grand Rapids Michigan and we bought Memphis Aero 
in Memphis Tennessee.  Jet Co in Birmingham Alabama and AMR Combs had a charter 
operation in Denver.  So he was concerned that if any of the light corporate type aircrafts 
had an accident it would be just like a DC-10 because all the headlines would say 
American airlines had an accident and he wasn’t comfortable with all these little 
operations doing their own thing.  So the flight operations was getting big enough that 
they decided we were going to combine them all and then there were several options on 
which way we were going to go.  Whether we were going to buy a fleet of corporate jets 
and expand the charter business that way or which way we were going to about it.

During that period of time corporate aviation was really at a low point in the United 
States, corporate jet sells were down and corporate chill sells were down.  The flight 
departments were being disbanded right and left and the flight departments that were 
active weren’t flying very much.  So I came up with a plan that I call the alliance 
program where we would take Fortune 500 companies preferably, large corporations that 
had nice corporate aircraft that just weren’t being utilized properly that would like to put 
them on our charter certificate and operate under our rules and regulations and we would 
provide the marketing get charters for them and then we would take a commission for the 
charters that we provided and if they picked up a charter on their own we would take a 
smaller commission.  The pilots would be on our…we would train them  in basic in dock 
and it was quite an expensive program.  We had to come to an arrangement with the FAA 
as to how we would operate this fleet because we had airplanes scattered all over the 
country and I worked out a plan that was kind of a combination of 121 and 135 operation 
that the FAA liked very much and went to Washington and explained it at lengths to the 
head of operation and maintenance and for years they had been trying to put a part 
together called 119 where they would merge the two into one operation.  So they say this 
plan as kind of a four-runner of things to come and being we were going to have a large 
number of aircrafts scattered over a large area they called this the first mega 135 
certificate and they assigned a certificate management office to us like they would an 
airline.  I was somewhat dismayed by the time we got all the certificate approved because 
it wound up that we had a FAA office that had more personnel then I had in the 
management in the actual operation.  But at any rate it worked out very well for us.  We 
had air…we even had airplane space overseas.  We had U.S. registered aircraft…and of 
course I won’t bore you with the problems involved in dealing with the countries and 
dealing with dealing with the FAA and dealing with foreign civil aviation authorities, but 
it was challenging to say the least.  Some of our first…we had aircraft, oh gosh…at one 
time I had an airplane down in Siberia for an engine change; I had the first airplane to 
land in Kuwait at the end of the fighting over there…I had…the operational problem s 
were challenging to say the least, but we managed to cope very well, the program worked 
well and after it was running smooth I was called to…by and old friend of m ine down in 
South America to please come down and help him.  He was having some problems with a

FBO program off separately called AMR combs and it became a part of signature FBOs

president and that was Jim Singer.  Jim wanted me to run a division of it called the 
international division where we would set up third party airline services in third world 
countries and it was a broad spectrum of airline services.  Everything from freight 
handling, ground handling, passenger service, maintenance, flight operations, anything 
they wanted we would provide for them anywhere in the world.  We wanted to get our 
foot in the door with a lot the emmerging third world countries, particularly countries that 
had been spun off by the old Soviet Union and we were also very interested in Vietnam, 
getting in there, we thought there were a lot of economic possibilities there.  So I started

traveling for AMR services and setting up third party airline operations.  We had 
operations scattered over quite a wide area. We operated the entire airport at Warsaw in 
Poland, we took it from a third world Laos run airport to a twentieth century first rate 
airport in ninety days.  We had, we set up a company in Haberask in the Russian far east 
for airline services.  That one was in conjunction with, oh what the heck…a larg e 
construction company, Parsons Constructions and to a smaller extent, Hughes electronics 
division.  We worked, I made a number of trips back to Vietnam, but we could never get 
anything together with the Vietnamese.  They were as difficult to deal with as they had 
been when I was there with Air America.  We travel in all the -stans…Khazakhhtan, 
Uzbekistan, Turkmenistan.  We had operation in Turkey.  It was an interesting job, but it 
kept me in an aluminum tube at high altitude for years!  In December of 1989 AMR had a 
major cut back, down sizing I guess you call it and they spun off a number of more 
divisions including the international division I was in.  At 59 ½ at that time, in 
December…that would h ave been in December ‘94 with my retirement package, it was 
probably April before I wasn’t on the payroll anymore, but for all practical purposes you 
could say I retired about April of ’95.  I settled back in the Rio Grand Valley where’d I 
had my FBO and where I still owned several rent properties, houses that I had rented out 
and I have been down here just managing a few rental properties and taking life easy 
since.   
SM:  Okay.  Well, let me ask you some clarifying questions.  
JM:  Okay. 
SM:  When you first went to work with AMR and you set up that program with I 
guess with the major corporations… 
JM:  On the jet program? 
SM:  On the jet program. 
JM:  That was called the Alliance Program. 
SM:  The Alliance Program, thank you.  When you set that up and you started 
working you mentioned that you also had overseas, aircraft based overseas. 
JM:  Yes. 
SM:  What were some of the major challenges you faced there in terms of 
working with [?]?

JM:  Well there are a number of agreements that foreign countries have for 
allowing foreign aircraft to operate in their country.  For instances, when I had my 
Mexican charter certificate I could take American citizens or a Mexican citizen for that 
matter as long as he was in the United States, I could take them to Mexico and bring them 
back, but I couldn’t go into Mexico and pick people up from Mexico and take them out of 
the country.  Or…what a m inute, I could take them out of the country but I couldn’t take 
them from point to point within the country because that would have been in 
consideration of the…it was the various freedoms they had, 1 through 5, of what you 
could do.  So the most serious one issue, you couldn’t pick up people in the country and 
take them somewhere else within the country because that would be considered to be in 
competition with the local operators in that country.  My first overseas, the airplane we 
first bases overseas, we based a challenger that belonged to a corporation in North 
Carolina that had two of them and the one was just kind of surplus to them.  Of course 
aircraft sales were bad at the time and they were anxious to put it to work and we had 
work for it in the UK, but I had a heck of a time negotiating the necessary freedoms to 
operate the airplane as an N-registered airplane on a foreign certificate in the UK.  But 
we managed to overcome it.  Almost all the flights were international because the UK 
being a relatively small country, the business been going to France, Germany or 
somewhere else, and the fact that they didn’t have that type of aircraft or that class of 
aircraft on charter certificate…we managed to  negotiate an agreement with them.  We 
couldn’t go anywhere within the UK with the airplane, but that wasn’t a problem for us 
because most of our flights were international anyway.  We operated that airplane 
for…oh, it was still under operation when I left , I believe that operated over there for 
about two years.  As a matter of fact that was the airplane that lost an engine on its 
charter into Russia!   
SM:  Whoa!  How about working in Russia at the time.  What were things…?  
JM:  Well I spent a lot of time in Russia trying to set up Dow Transits 
Corporation that we set up in Haberask.  There were a lot of challenges in getting it 
together, I made some very good friends, I have a lot of respect for the Russian people.  It 
would be very easy to transition from capitalism to communism, but the reverse is 
extremely complicated because the government owns everything and to try to get it back 
  156 

--- PDF PAGE 157 ---
 
into private hands is a real…is a real job.  W e had the deal with all these little 
bureaucracies that control segments of the economy.  Like, oh they had the banking 
people; they had the land people that actually owned the land; they had the engineering 
people that approved any changes or new structures; they had a numerous, about fifteen 
different agencies you had to go through to do anything.  But what we did is try to get all 
these people to buy in to the program when they had been spin off as private indemnities, 
we tried them to buy small amounts of stock, we offered them sweat equity in the 
corporation to come into it, and that way we eliminated the resistance to somebody 
coming in and competing with them.  I found on our board, we had eighteen board 
members as I recall, and on the U.S. side we had Parsons Hughes an AMR representative.  
Now the Russian side we had all the various government entities involved that were no 
long government entities, they were privatized, but it was really interesting.  I believe the 
Russians we had…I know we had over ten PhDs , they were very well educated and at 
first we had some concerns that, you know, the quality of their work might not be up to 
western standards and they were dispelled very early.  I know Parsons who has a 
reputation for excellence all over the world gave them some engineering work to do on 
reconstruction of the airport in Haberask and they gave them the work that they thought 
was just the fluff off work because they were afraid to give them any serious work for 
fear it wouldn’t be up to standards and as the drawings and engineering work started 
coming in they were amazed at how professional that the Russian group was.  They 
eliminated any other partners in doing the architectural work and engineering work, they 
turned it all over to the Russians.  They were very, very confident.  Same thing held true 
with the backing and all the other divisions we worked with.  They were very confident 
people.  They were honest people.  We did run into some problems where corruption 
from other sources that created problems in doing business over there…very, very 
difficult, they kept throwing obstacles in out path, but we were sticking with it, when I 
left they still had it.  I assume, well I know it’s still there, I know Parsons is still a partner 
although AMR when they downsized everything they divested themselves with their 
share, but the company was still viable.  I even believe that the people that took over our 
Polish airport operation took over AMR’s part of Powtrans as I recall.  But it was 
challenging, it was interesting, I felt very sorry for the Russian people.  Saw a lot of, you

SM:  Yes sir.  You mentioned corruption, were there expectations by some of 
your bureaucrats to receive compensation?  That is bribes? 
JM:  Yeah, well.  Getting things in, for instance if you wanted to import 
something that generally come in through port at Vladivostok and the Russian Mafia 
control the port and of course they wanted a huge percentage of the value of the 
equipment or the equipment would just disappear, stuff like that.  In fact I believe that 
one of the early success of a Russian company was a Russian security company that 
started business to provide security for Russian and foreign companies that were having 
trouble with Mafia and government and what have you.  This company was started by a 
bunch of ex-KGB guys, they were pretty bad dudes and they became kind of their own 
mafia I believe, only they took on the bad guys and if they were the security for your 
train load of stuff you can be sure the stuff got there because if anybody tried to intercept 
it they were going to disappear.   
SM:  Well was this the equivalent of one protection racket replacing another? 
JM:  Well except they ran it like a business and I understand they didn’t renege on 
their Contract. 
SM:  Right, but I mean basically hire us or your stuff’s not protected. 
JM:  Yeah your stuff was protected and you paid a reasonable price compared to 
what the mafia was charging you. 
SM:  Okay.  Was that your only dealings with the Russian mafia? 
JM:  Yes, yes.  The only ones that we had was that the problem was trying to get 
anything through the port at Vladivostok. 
SM:  The bureaucracy that you had to deal with, do you know if any of those 
representatives were basically FSB, the representatives of the [?], the successor, excuse 
me, KGB? 
JM:  I really don’t know, I don’t know.  The obstacles, the government obstacles 
were fought by our own people, the Dow Trans people who had a best at interest in the

SM:  Okay.  Was anybody ever or were you ever interviewed about the business 
you were conducting there by Russian officials? 
JM:  Well we had numerous meetings and various government offices.  We had to 
meet with all the government officials from both Moscow and from the local government, 
the…I can’t rem ember what they called them they were like, you know like we might call 
a state or county they had a name for the area and we had to meet with the governor of 
that area and then we even had to meet with the mayor of our city or Haberask and 
everybody had to sign off on what we were doing.  But the people that we met with in the 
early days all seems generally interested to get us in there and as helpful as they could be.  
Once we got in there then additional obstacles started cropping up, but initially the 
dealing that I had they were all very, very friendly. 
SM:  Good.  Now did you ever have to travel to Moscow and to the major cities to 
work with officials in higher positions then the region personnel? 
JM:  The Moscow end of it was handled by it by the Parsons director and I 
handled the local, the Haberask end of it.  Jim Chappelle was the representative at 
the…he was based in London, it was fairly easy for him  to get back and forth from 
London to Moscow.  But you know it’s 11 ½ time zones from western Russia to eastern 
Russia, it’s along ways across there.   
SM:  That’s right.  And this is, just to clarify.  This is during the time when AMR 
was expanding its third party air operations. 
JM:  That’s right. 
SM:  Okay.  Going back a little bit before that.  How much about your 
participation in Lima Peru can you talk about? 
JM:  Oh yeah I can talk about anything you know, it’s just a…it was a fairly easy 
operation. 
SM:  How many aircraft were they flying? 
JM:  Oh lets see how many we had.  They were mostly helicopters, which I didn’t 
have a whole lot to do with except brining in supplies to them.  They had a lot of the Bell

2-12s, the twin-engine Bells and on the transport in I think we had three 123s, was the 
maximum.  Right after I left they replaced the 123s with Convair 580s and then since 
then they’ve replaced them with I think it’s called a C-27, it’s a twin engine turbo prop, I 
believe it’s Italian built.  The U.S. Air Force bought twelve of them years ago and finally 
retired those twelve and the state department picked them up and they’re using those 
now.  They look like I guess a 123 but with turbo props, they’re a little smaller I believe 
they only gross 50 or 52 thousand pounds. 
SM:  This was a state run, a state department run operation or DEA 
involvement…how was that?  
JM:  Well you know when Bush, first president Bush, formed this war on drugs 
task force there was a lot of infighting between customs, DEA, Coast Guard, CIA.  All 
the various entities that had interest in drugs were always fighting for turf and money and 
what have you.  They wanted to make this a joint use where everybody got their nose in it 
and so there were…DEA, there’s always DE A agents assigned to every embassy, they 
call them narcotic assistance unit and they work with the local narcotics people.  In Peru 
there were a bunch of them because that’s where, at that time anyway, they figured most 
of the cocaine was grown.  They made a raw paste out of it there and transported that to 
Columbia where it was refined into powder and then next imported it into the U.S. or 
wherever.  The concept of the program was to eradicate the cocoa at the source and the 
upper Wyaga Valley, which is really the Amazon Valley, but it’s you know, oh I’d guess 
the elevation might be two to three thousand, two to thirty five hundred feet in there.  The 
foothills of the Andes on the Amazon side was where most of the cocoa was grown.  We 
would fly what they call a CORA… agricu ltural workers that had been given some 
training in how to recognize a coca plant I guess… into areas that  they found the thing 
growing and the helicopters take them to the fields and they’d chop down the plants and 
burn them, stuff like that.  The helicopters did spraying on the early part and the Peruian 
government raised so much hell about spraying that they actually went to chopping stuff 
down instead.  I believe we were doing spraying in Columbia also or we had a smaller 
operation and I believe they’re still spraying to this day in Columbia.  
SM:  Do you know what they’re spraying? 
JM:  Well it’s some kind of herbicide, something that kills cocoa plants.

SM:  Okay.  But it doesn’t leave the soil destitute? 
JM:  No, not to my knowledge it doesn’t.  You know there’s stuff that heck we 
spray defoliants on cotton in the United States to defoliate the plant before they harvest it. 
So I don’t know what type of herbicide it is, but it’s obviously something that is 
expectable for the Columbian government.   
SM:  Okay.  Now, do you know if they did any kind of…tried to introduce any 
kind of crop substitution? 
JM:  You know I wasn’t involved in that end of it and I really don’t know.  I 
believe that they did.  The people that we worked with in the field were primarily CIA 
and DEA.  There was one old CIA guy that when we came in with a 123 he almost had 
tears in his eye, said he thought he was back in Laos.  (laughing) 
SM:  Okay.  What role were the DEA and the CIA playing there, do you know? 
JM:  Well they were trying to find where the actually laboratories were and where 
the actual crops were being grown and you know, they…I don’t know how effective they 
were.  I always felt that it was kind of like urinating in the ocean to raise tide.  I thought 
we were throwing away our tax dollars, but we’re still doing it so somebody thinks it’s 
important.   
SM:  Well how much of their work do you think involved not just the counter 
narcotics, but also counter terrorism and counter insurgency and of course the…?  
JM:  Well in the early days I’m sure there was quite a bit of it there was roads 
being cut and traffic stopped and extortion.  They were extorting money from truckers 
that were bring produce and things from the upper Wyaga Valley where there’s a lot of 
produce grown over he Andes and into Lima and the would stop the trucks and they 
would claim they were taxing them, but they were just ripping them off…and these were 
communists.  We saw flags with hammers and sickles on them. 
SM:  Oh really! 
JM:  Yeah.   
SM:  Do you know if this was Sundero Luminoso? 
JM:  Yes it was.   
SM:  How much had you heard about some of that activity?
