JM:  Oh we had heard quite a bit about it.  One of the agency guys that worked 
with out of Tango Maria told us where the hot beds were, where the cities were that had 
them, just as matter of you know, if you’re going to fly low don’t fly low over that town 
and kind of gave us a broad overview of what the hell they were doing, you know if they 
were going through. 
SM:  Did you ever get shot at? 
JM:  I can’t personally say that I was, you know with small arms fire you rarely 
know it until you find a hole in your airplane after the fact.  I know a couple of our pilots 
received holes in the airplane while they were flying and you know, when they got back 
to base the maintenance found them.  We lost some people down there in that, we lost 
several Air America people, helicopter pilots, but I believe they were all operational 
accidents.  We lost one of our old legendary pilots, Hithchman was killed down there in 
that war on drugs program, same program I was flying.  In fact in 1989 while I was there, 
May 20th I remember it because it was my birthday again.  We had a caravan that had, I 
can’t remember how many people were on board that thing, I think about a dozen, most 
of them were Air America or not necessarily all Air America, but some Air America 
flight mechanics for the helicopter and some other American Contract personnel and 
some local Contract personnel.  The airplane iced up over the Andes and crashed at about 
sixteen thousand foot elevation, of course killed everybody on board.  That was the big 
disaster while I was down there. 
SM:  But that didn’t threaten the operation. 
JM:  No we kept on truckin’, but they use that caravan going back and forth from 
the sites in the upper Wyaga Valley and it only went back to base maybe once a week or 
once every two weeks.  They really shouldn’t have been carrying a lot of personnel I 
don’t say.  I thought it was a mistake, in fact I had voiced some concerns about the 
caravan carrying people over there.  I said, “You know, when the minimum in route 
altitude is 23 thousand feet and you’ve got some of the worse icing I’ve ever seen 
anywhere in the world, that’s not a place for a single engine airplane to be full of 
passengers.  We went back and forth in the 123 with all four engines, two turning and 
two burning and everybody sucking on oxygen and we carried lots of passengers, we’d 
never had less the twenty or thirty on board.  But my god with four engines and of course

a heck of de-icing system, two huge heaters for de-icing the leading edges of the wings 
and tail and then of course we had electric de-icing on the inlets of the jets and we had 
pneumatic boots on the ailerons because the water would come back from the leading 
edge and freeze the ailerons.   But even with all that we would flame jets out, lots of, I’ve 
had several jets flamed up because a little ice would form on the inlet door.  We had a 
door on those J-85s that when you went into reverse it closed the door so that it didn’t 
suck in a lot of dirt from the dirt runway.  That door had a little lip on it and I could look 
out and see the ice forming on it and it didn’t have to be very thick, I think an eighth of 
an inch would flame a jet out and when it broke loose boom the jet was out.  Man that 
happened on numerous flights…but even then  you know we only had a relatively small 
window where we really had a problem with the altitude.  The peaks were pretty high, in 
fact about thirty miles north of course is a 23 thousand foot peak!  But in our area if we 
got half way over the Altoplano we could lose both jets and just cost down, we’d pull the 
power back on recips and let it down with a 300 a foot rate of decent and descend into the 
Amazon Valley with no trouble at all.  If we were going the other way we could do the 
same thing, half way across we could just head due west and descend over the mountains 
into the Pacific and let down until we broke out so it was probably only a period of about 
eight minutes that was really critical for us where we didn’t have a whole lot of options.   
SM:  Was that the principle hazard or were those the principle hazards flying 
in… ? 
JM:  It was that particular type of airplane.  The hazard was to lose a recip at a 
critical part.  Because we were loaded it maxed gross weight on every trip and at 23 
thousand feet the 123 was kind of a lousy performer.   
SM:  What were you typically carrying besides passengers? 
JM:  Oh we carried fuel for the helicopters, we carried food for all the people that 
were over there, we carried everything you need to sustain forty, fifty or a hundred 
people so the airplanes were always loaded to the gill ever trip. 
SM:  Was it…were you able to utilize the experiences that you had gained as an 
Air America pilot in Laos, in particular in flying in that mountainous terrain? 
JM:  Oh yeah, sure.  We had some strips up in the mountains.  We had Masomore 
down on the southern end of the valley, which was a grass strip in the mountains

and…can’t rem ember the name, we had one strip that was really high that provided some 
challenges landing in that was I’m going to say the elevation was probably about nine 
thousand feet, but there was real high mountains all around it so you’d have to kind of let 
down in a figure eight around some terrain to get in there.  It wouldn’t have been a good 
place to lose an engine on take off, but…and th e strips were dirt strips.  Some of them 
were short.  I opened up Santa Maria when they first built it, which was kind of an island 
like a strip in the river.  It was built like a firebase, it reminded me of an old Special 
Forces type firebase in Vietnam.  The security in that area was pretty bad so they really 
had a lot of fortifications around it and they built a dirt strip.  The first time I went in 
there was some trees on the other side of the river that were really high and made the 
approach a little difficult, but that was the whole purpose of going in there on a test basis.  
Told them what to cut down, they just went out with the chain saws and cut them down.  
After that there was no problem.   
SM:  While you were there were there every any terrorist acts committed in 
Lima? 
JM:  Oh yes, yes!  There were some explosions while we were in Lima.  Security 
was always a concern in Lima, you know we traveled around the time freely, our wives 
did too, but there was always a possibility of a bomb going off.  The electricity was…you 
know they blew a transformer up or a power plant up and I would say that if you had a 
electricity five days in a row you’d consider lucky while we were down there.  We lived 
on the twelfth floor of our apartment building and it was a pain in the neck when the 
power went out you had to walk up twelve flights of stairs.    
SM:  Any other hazards? 
JM:  No not particularly.  I would say it was very enjoyable flying, I loved it, I 
took every trip I could get.  I really enjoyed flying!   
SM:  I guess when you finished that you were called back to AMR working on the 
third party air operations. 
JM:  That’s right. 
SM:  You mentioned that the Vietnamese when you tried to start operations there, 
the Vietnamese were just as difficult as they were back during the war.

JM:  You know they had all the bureaucracy from the French when we were there 
an then after I went back years later they had all the bureaucracy of the French plus the 
bureaucracy of the Communists and they were almost childlike in there… I think they 
were so devious they thought everybody else was devious.  So they would pick a 
Contract apart, every word they thought was a poison pen in the dad gung Contract and 
as a result there was just no way that we could ever get anything together that a 
responsible corporation would want to get involved in in sending a lot of expensive 
equipment there and pouring in some money when there were so many potential pit falls.  
I could never…you know nor mally you’d take those out with the Contract.  You’d say, 
“Look now we…you guys say that we can charge th ese rates and this is all the tax there’s 
going to be.  Now if you turn around and raise the taxes we have to raise the rate for the 
airline,” and we’d put that in the Contract.  “No, no, no, no, you have to guarantee this 
rate.”  “Well then you guarantee you won’t raise taxes.”  “Oh we can’t do that!”  I said, 
“Well then you know…” It was always som ething like that we found it impossible to deal 
with them. 
SM:  Okay.  Now while you were trying to do this business with them, were there 
still the stipulation that any foreign investor or any foreign company trying do business in 
Vietnam had to have a Vietnamese counter part businessperson? 
JM:  I believe there was and we did have a…we had a com pany that was being 
spun off of Vietnam Airlines and it was called VASCO, Vietnam Aviation Services 
Company and they were going to have the domestic airline, they were going to have the 
FBOs and the crop dusting and the off shore helicopter work and all the general aviation 
type of flying plus the domestic airline flying and Air Vietnam was going to have the 
international routes.  This VASCO, the chief pilot was a black gentlemen who spoke 
fluent French from Surinam, not Surinam, it’s on the west coast of Africa…I think it 
starts with a S…Senegal, Sene gal.  I made very good friends with him, he turned out to 
be a really upstanding man.  He had gotten there because he had ferried an airplane to 
them.  They had bought, I can’t remember what it was, I think it was a Shorts 330 or 336, 
and he ferried it in and of course their pilots, he was supposed to train a couple of their 
pilots.  While he was there instructing the pilots the Vietnamese authorities or the people 
that were running the company realized that he was a real good man and they offered to

hire him as their chief pilot.  And they were smart to do that because he was an excellent 
man.  He had gotten his pilot’s license in the United States.  He was from an upper 
middle class family in Senegal, wanted to be a pilot, they sent him to the U.S., and he got 
all of his ratings.  In fact he even did a little flying for a U.S. company for a while, but 
then he went back to Senegal and wound up ferrying that airplane to Vietnam, which 
changed his career.  But he was really a fine young man and I would assume he’s 
probably still well.   
SM:  Alright sir why don’t you go ahead and discuss those business cards and the 
stories that you had. 
JM:  Okay.  I was looking through some of my business cards from my trips to 
Russia and Vietnam and various other places while we had the break and I came across 
some of the people that I had mentioned, I have their names now.  I might mention that 
who we use for interpreters in a lot of these countries were mostly American Airline 
employees.  With well over 100,000 employees American had people who could speak 
dang near any language you could think of throughout the system.  On my trips to 
Vietnam we had a Vietnamese American who worked for American Airlines as a 
maintenance manager in Paris.  His name was Jean Claude Vo Van Nguyen and he of 
course spoke fluent Vietnamese and French and he accompanied me on most of my trips 
to Vietnam as an interpreter.  The chief pilot of VASCO was a young man named 
Christian Huchard, H-u-c-h-a-r-d, and I believe the H was silent…A real fine young m an.  
Jean Claude Vo Van Nguyen, his father was a doctor and they left Vietnam before the 
fall thank goodness to Paris and that’s where Jean Claude was raised.  He was…oh he 
was just a teenager when they left, maybe even younger then that.  He became a 
mechanic and eventually wound up with American Airlines in Paris, but his father had a 
lot of good contacts in Vietnam and had even gone back and was accepted because… of 
course they confiscated all of his land and everything when he left, but seeing he had 
never really been with the government he’d always been a doctor and done private 
practice they kind of left him alone and the communist didn’t have any ax to grind with 
him.  So he introduced…Jean Claude’s father introduced [?] to a couple of people that he 
thought would be very helpful as consultants in my business in Vietnam and they turned 
out that they both were extremely helpful.  One of them was Thiet Vu, V-u, who was a

SM:  Now when you were traveling through Russia, did you witness any 
environmental problems? 
JM:  Oh god yes, everywhere!  You can’t drink that water anywhere in Russia, 
it’s polluted everywhere… it’s really bad.  The ecology is such in Russia that I don’t think 
it will ever clear up and it will take centuries to clear up, it’s a mess.   
SM:  Anything else besides the water that struck you? 
JM:  Well, the way they went about doing just about everything added to the 
problem I thought, but according to Jim Chappelle that I traveled with who was an 
engineer he said, “Well you know, really if you look at it strictly from an engineering 
standpoint they’re doing it the right way.  However when something breaks down 
everything breaks down because of that.”  For one thing the city would supply all the heat 
to all the house, like we have electricity piped in to the house.  Well in Russia they pipe 
the heat into the house and the hot water into the house and it all comes from a central 
plant, the pipes are wrapped with insulation and what have you, but the system is old and 
decrepit and as a result you rarely had water of any kind and then on top you rarely, 
rarely had hot water and heat was very, very sporadic.  So it was a mess, it was a 
mess…felt sorry for the people. 
SM:  Yeah.  How about oil spills things like that, did you witness anything 
firsthand? 
JM:  I wasn’t in the oil areas, but my friends that were told me that yes, leaky 
pipeline leaking, they didn’t take precautions in the drilling.  So where we had isolated 
holding pits for holding contaminated drilling mud and drilling water and oil, they didn’t 
it just went out on the ground.  As a result the whole area is polluted.  It’s a shame, it 
really is a shame.  You wouldn’t believe that that large a country could be so totally 
polluted by, what 250 million people, but it is. 
SM:  Well, how would you compare that experience in Russia to what you 
experience in Lima and Peru?  How was Peru by comparison? 
JM:  Peru was much better by comparison.  They’d dump their garbage in the 
ocean and what have you, but the city itself seemed to have a fairly decent…well at least

JM:  I flew some of the ruggedest…y ou know the Andes are awesome mountains 
so you really don’t see a whole lot there, glaciers and those huge lakes that they had up in 
the Altoplano.  They had these gigantic fresh water lakes that…gosh I guess the elevation 
13 to 15 thousand feet in there.  In fact I believe Kasue actually did some research on 
some of those fresh water lakes at high altitude in Peru.  You could see slash and burn

there that was very much like the smoky season we had in Laos; real restricted visibility.   
SM:  Would that diminish you’re operations when that season came through? 
JM:  Well no it didn’t.  The fellow that started that operation for the state 
department was Ed Adams.  Ed is an old Air America pilot and he believed that if you 
gave him a job he was going to do it and he always did, but the DEA was scared to death 
and made him look bad because they refused to fly day after day and Ed would do his job 
day after day.  So they jumped on saying he wasn’t safe and that’s one of the reasons they 
wanted me to come down.  Well we’ll get a fellow from American Airlines down here to 
straighten it out, but we still continued to fly minimums they wouldn’t fly.  They had to 
have…they flew their operation like a 135 operation.  A lot of those airports didn’t have 
approaches into them and you could only let down to the minimum en route altitude, 
which at the foothill of the Andes was really high you know, but it was perfectly safe to 
let down if you knew where the heck you were.  You could use…you could triangulate or 
you could use more then one standard broadcast station along there, position yourself 
well over the Amazon and you could let down safely.  Good grief you could let down

finally called us into the ambassador’s office with their chief of air ops, a fellow that had 
been a retired customs pilot, Peter…oh gos h I can’t think of Peter’s last name, but 
anyway.  He was the arbitrator anyway between the chief pilot of the DEA that came

doing all these dangerous things I mentioned to Peter and to the ambassador that you 
know, “they’re trying to run their operation like an airline, which does ensure a very high 
level of safety, but if you’re going to catch bad guys who fly in bad weather and go 
below minimums you’re never going to catch them because that’s when they’re going to 
be flying.  If we’re going to get in their and do our job to catch these people we’re going 
to have to find a way that will give us a high degree of safety, but far exceed the 
standards of 135 or 121.  And here’s how we do it…”  I just laid out  all the approaches 
that we’d drawn up and the safety factors we had where we’d use more then one beckon 
to ensure that we were past the mountains before we started the let down.  It all seemed 
very reasonable to Peter and it sound reasonable to the ambassador and the DEA chief 
pilot went out with egg on his face.  In their defense though I’ll say this for them, the 
DEA insisted that all their pilots be DEA people first and pilots second.  As a result they 
had a lot of very minimum, minimum time aviators that I would barely say were safe in a 
Cessna 172 on a VFR day and to put them in that situation wasn’t fair.

route if anything happens over the Andes and alternate landing strips, etcetera.  All it as 
was lip service though.  What they were doing… they were doing all that anyway, they

But one of the things about the U.S. way of working with foreign countries is they don’t

give the money and the supplies directly to the government and the government gives the

happened that way where we had government officials who had large Swiss bank

ten cents on the dollar for the money it spends.  If they would go in directly like a lot of 
other foreign countries do and they would run the aid program or the military program

JM:  No, no.  I don’t believe so.  We ran everything through our legal department.  
Of course American Airlines has got a…they ’ve got a legal department that puts some 
major law firms to shame for the number of lawyers they’ve got and of course they use 
lawyers out in the field close to wherever they’re needed as well as their company 
lawyers.  I had a fulltime lawyer assigned to all the projects that I went on and I ran 
everything by him, all the Contracts and everything of course.  I came up with a…for all 
these third world countries we ran into a lot of things that we wanted to make sure that 
we got into every single Contract and I developed a term sheet for if it isn’t in your 
Contract you better find out why type of thing.  It made it easy for the legal department.  
We all carried the term sheets and we would make sure that all those squares were filled, 
then we’d turn that over the legal department they’d draw up a Contract.   
SM:  How about on site?  When you’d go into these different countries was it 
typical for you to make a stop in real quick to the embassy just to check in, let them know 
that you were in-country conducting business? 
JM:  No I didn’t.  You mean with the U.S. Embassy? 
SM:  Yes sir. 
JM:  No.  I’d become so disgusted with the U.S. Embassy in my dealings around 
the country that I avoided them like the plague.  My last experience with dealing with the 
U.S. Embassy was…well actually it was on that  project in South America, come to think 
  177

of it.  I’d ferried an airplane to the U.S., my co-captain was an ex-Air America pilot.  In 
fact he flew my last trip, the last trip that I flew ferrying airplanes to Clark Air Force 
Base this man was my co-pilot, now years later I’m flying with him in South America, 
which was going to me my last trip I was flying back to Miami and then I was going back 
to AMR.  We had a real sick airplane, we were taking it back.  One of the engines was 
due for change and temps and pressure were running…pressure’s running low, tem ps’s 
running high type of thing and real borderline.  On the first leg of the trip we were 
supposed to make a tech stop in…oh heck, th e capital, not the capital but the seaport 
city…I can’t believe I can’t think of that in a ll the time I spent there.  Just north of Peru, 
how’s your geography?  Goyaquil Ecuador!  Goyaquil Ecuador was my tech stop.  
Before I got to Goyaquil I lost a transfer pump that transfers fuel from the drop tanks to 
the  main tank and I caught it when there was only about a hundred gallon imbalance, but 
if it had gone to four hundred gallons we’d have had a real problem landing with 425 
gallons of fuel in one tank and the other one empty.  So I could no longer use the fuel and 
drop tank and limited my range significantly.  So where I was scheduled to go from 
Goyaquil to…oh heck…it’s over on the northea st coast of Columbia…that was going to 
be my tech stop for fuel before I jumped off.  I was going to Jamaica and offload some 
company cargo in Jamaica and then to Miami, but I couldn’t make it without a tech stop 
in route so half way was Kalie Columbia.  Now I had to go to the embassy and work out 
a clearance with the Columbian government to make a tech stop in Kalie and that was my 
last time that I’d gone to the embassy and it was kind of interesting because the character 
I met there was right out of a Graham Green novel, in the seersucker suit 
and…(laughing)…he actually was very help ful, he was a friendly enough guy, but we 
had to get the clearance and he had to got to several departments and talk to several 
people in the civil aviation of Ecuador and Columbia on the phone, but he did negotiate it 
and I managed to make a tech stop in Kali.  Then between Kali and…Baronkia, Baronkia 
was the place I was originally going to.  Then in between Kali and Baronkia we lost the 
engine that we thought was good.  We’re already three engine ferrying because on of the 
jets was out, which happened to be the same side as the recip that we lost and now the 
other engine we’re running with the cowl flaps full open and the oil cooler full open and 
the temps are still going in the red.  We got a real bad situation on our hands and it’s

him and went ahead and made our approach and landed uneventfully, but when we

Avianca pilot, I said, “Ah, we’ve got a lot of guys out here that don’t look friendly.  
They’ve got guns…”  He said, “Oh senor you’re in big trouble!”  I was like, “Can you do 
me a favor?  When you get…”  He was goi ng to Bogotá.  “When you get to Bogotá 
would  you call the U.S. Embassy and tell them that the state department airplane is on 
the ground at this airport?”  and he said he would and he did thank god because we really 
got the third degree there.  The night commandant that we met was right out of a B Grade 
movie.  I’ll tell you this guy hated Norte Americanos number one, number two he 
thought we were druggies, which he didn’t really mind because I believe he was a 
druggie himself, but he wanted to rip us off.  Then when he found out that we really were 
international narcotics matters he got really difficult to deal with.  Oh man, he split the 
crew, took my co-pilot and my air freight specialist to the terminal building to interrogate 
and he kept myself and our flight engineer…we carried fligh t mechanics on those 
airplanes…out at the airplane while he wa s going through all of the cargo.  They broke 
open all of our suitcases, emptied the clothes on the floor, walked on them to show their 
complete disdain for us, and every time I would show my displeasure I’d get a gun stuck

nothing happened to it until we could repair it and get it out.  The day after we left it blew 
up with a thermite grenade in the cockpit and burned to the ground.  (laughing)   
SM:  Oh for crying out loud.  And what happened after that? 
JM:  Well we went to Bogotá and awaited word from the company what they 
wanted us to do.  They finally told us to go back to Lima rather then go to the states from 
Bogotá and so we went back to Lima and the crews that were still staying there went 
back to work and I got on the airline about a day or two later and came back to the states.   
SM:  Okay.  You knew that you’d be leaving? 
JM: Oh yeah, I was supposed to leave…I ha d all my baggage and all the stuff that 
Sandy had bought down there with me.  It was kind of a funny thing.  I had several boxes 
of Alpaca rugs and sweaters and all kinds of junk that she had bought from the several 
months that we were down there and I took them on the airplane because I was going to 
Miami and I was staying back there.  I was going back to AMR and it was just a way to 
get all that stuff home without shipping it on the airline.  Then when they told us to go 
back to Lima I’ve got, oh about three or four suitcases and boxes full of Peruvian rugs 
and Peruvian sweaters and all the art…you know the stuff you buy in Peru.  W hen I came 
back to Peru they looked through all this stuff and all the customs people were just 
absolutely amazed.  They pulled over to their supervisor and they looked at this stuff and 
they look at me like what’s this idiot bringing all this Peruvian stuff into Peru for! 
(laughing)  They said, “Peru?”  and I said, “Yeah!”  They just scratched their heads, “Go 
on.”  (laughing) 
SM:  (laughing) Too funny.  Okay, well is there anything else concerning your 
time with ARM that you’d like to discuss? 
JM:  No I can’t think of anything.  There’s all kinds of stuff, but I mean I think 
we’ve covered the highest high part. 
SM:  Okay.  Well, I guess we could close with some grander questions if you will. 
JM:  Sure.

SM:  I was just wondering.  In what ways was your service in Laos…in what 
ways did it affect your life the most and would you do anything over again? 
JM:  Well number one I think all of us were doing the right thing in Laos.  We all 
believed in the domino theory…that was what our government certainly believed in at the 
time and…we believed that the work we were  doing in Laos was probably the way that 
they war should’ve been fought in Vietnam because with a fraction of the U.S. resources 
they were keeping several North Vietnamese division at bay for years while we were 
getting chewed up little by little in Vietnam.  In fact not little by little, big!  We thought it 
was an excellent holding action.  I think we thought we probably could hold on forever, 
but the real sad thing was that the most important resource that we had, which was the 
Hmong men were being chewed up like cannon fodder and after we’d gone through the 
majority of the Hmong Army there wasn’t anything left to fight with.  That was probably 
the real disaster of the whole thing is we whipped out a whole generation of young men 
up there.  But…no we all believed in what we  were doing I think to a man.  Rather proud 
of what we were doing, we thought we were doing something that not every Tom, Dick, 
and Harry could do.  I would say that my years in Laos when I’d get up in the morning 
and shave, I’d look in the mirror, I wouldn’t have traded places with anybody in the 
world.  I thoroughly enjoyed what I was doing.  I felt quite a bit of pride in what we were 
doing.  If you’re an aviator it was an absolute [?] flying in the world, you couldn’t get, it 
didn’t get any better then that.   
SM:  Are there any lessons that you think we should take away from that 
experience, things that maybe we haven’t yet? 
JM:  You know, I spent the years that I lived in Laos and Vietnam with very 
little…you know, no television, and newspapers  and what have you…I did a lot of 
reading and I went through Will and Aerial Durant’s World History from one end to the 
other and at the end they published a very small book called A Lesson from History and 
that seems to be the way it is.  We learn very little over the years from what we’ve done 
and the U.S. seems to make the same mistakes over and over again.  I guess I’m getting 
cynical in my old age, but I cringe some of the things that we do, some of the statements 
that our leaders make that I just can’t believe what I’m hearing sometimes because we

SM:  Is there anything else, in addition…you m entioned earlier the way we 
handled aid basically just like Vietnam that hasn’t changed… 
JM:  Oh, now aid…No it hasn’t change d and it should change.  Our state 
department is…it needs to be over hauled from  top to bottom.  We put in a new secretary 
of state, but all those old winnies that do nothing…the  average state department 
employee, particularly the ones bases abroad, feel that the fact that they’re living there in 
that third world country or wherever it is that they’re living is their job and try to get 
them to do anything else.  Try to get them to do what their job really is, like maybe help 
you with a visa or help you with a problem that you have with the local economy or the 
local police or a landlord or what have you…no way!  They ’re not effective.  They’re not 
effective at their job and their polices, other then suck, I don’t know what word to use.  I 
guess one of the stories…I’ve never m et the gentlemen but I heard if from other pilots… 
In Vietnam there was an old well driller that was drilling water wells throughout the 
central highlands in Vietnam and every village he went to that didn’t have water he’d get 
them water.  He would teach them how to drill a well or help them drill the well and he’d 
get the dang well drilled.  Later on US-Aid became very bureaucratic and they decided 
that this well driller that didn’t even have a high school education just wasn’t, he wasn’t 
educated and sophisticated enough to be working in the foreign service and they replaced 
him with what we call the Tennis Shoes and Tommy Gun Crowd.  They hired a bunch of 
young college graduates, engineers and political scientist and what have you and brought 
them in and…they were living a big advent ure, but they accomplished absolutely 
nothing.  I understand after that old well driller left there was never another water well in 
any of those Hamlets ever drilled again.  I think that’d be just ethical of the way our state 
department operates.  In Laos we had that old pig farmer, as you know, you probably 
have heard.  We called him Pop Pule, Mr. Pop and he would have been in the same boat 
except that by the time US-Aid realized who he was up country he was too powerful and 
had too many friends to get him thrown out.  But the people that were really effective, 
they didn’t fit the profile of what the state department felt that their Foreign Service 
officer should fit, they were out.  No matter how effective they were.  So you get the

mind set and the same mentality and when they finally retire after doing nothing for 
twenty or thirty years the people that come in are clones of them and they don’t do

completely overhauled from top to bottom and the mission from the very top they could 
change that overnight by just saying ‘we are not doing things through the local 
governments, we’re doing it from the…if they wa nt our help, we’ll help them if we deem 
it that we should, but we will administer the program, we will spend the money, we’ll 
decide who’s going to do the building and who’s going to do the farming, etcetera.  The 
local government won’t make those decisions.’  And if they don’t agree to that I wouldn’t 
give them any aid.  That way we wouldn’t have so many foreign desplets living high in 
Paris and various other places around the world with millions or hundreds of millions of 
dollars in Swiss bank accounts.   
SM:  Well, do you think the United States should employ troops like we did in 
Southeast Asia? 
JM:  I think that we had to.  It all led to finally the end of the Cold War.  If we 
would have sat back and let them just run over Vietnam I’m sure Laos and Thailand, the 
whole works would’ve gone.  We needed to make a stand, it might not have been for the 
right reasons, but we needed to make a stand.  Our planning in that stand was poor, but I 
believe it needed to be made. 
SM:  What about contemporary deployment or more recent deployments, things 
like Haiti, Somalia, the Balkans? 
JM:  Well again, I’m not sure that they didn’t need to be made, but again the 
planning was poor.  You know there’s something that I don’t understand from the very 
top of our government down, it’s a…we never address a problem directly because we 
have preconceived ideas that go back for generations as to how these things will be 
settled diplomatically, how they’ll be talked out, and then if it runs into a military 
operation how that’s going to run.  For instance, I’ll give you a perfect example.  We’ve 
got a despot, we know that he’s a drug smuggler; we know he’s a criminal, in Noriega in 
Panama.  What do we do?  We send in a war!  We send in troops, we kill the local troops, 
we kill the local civilians, we create all kinds of havoc to capture this idiot.  But we know 
he’s the problem there’s not question about that, we’re willing to kill hundreds of people,

he’d got to go, then address your forces directly at him, not at the local soldiers and the 
local populace.  I guess that would have happened in the Balkans is Milosevic, if it’d 
taken him out directly the war would’ve probably been over in short order, but 
instead…oh look at all the havoc  we brought over the whole dad gung country, but that’s 
find because it’s just not nice to shoot a public leader, you can’t do that but you can kill 
all the women and children and bomb the Chinese Embassy that’s okay. 
SM:  Is there anything else that you’d like to discuss? 
JM:  No not really.  I guess I’m not all that right wing although I guess I sound 
like it, but really I think that when we knew we’ve got a problem we knew exactly where 
it is we ought to address the problem head on at the top rather then fight these stupid 
wars.  Why send all these troops out there and line up all these tanks when it isn’t 
necessary?  You take out fifteen, twenty or a hundred people and the dang war is over.  
We’ve got the capability to do that, but we don’t.   
SM:  Do you think that would have been a better alternative let’s say to the Gulf 
War? 
JM:  Yes I think it would’ve!  I think it would have concentrated right on 
Baghdad and right on the president and his immediate staff I think it would have been 
better then killing all those dang soldiers.  You know, we like body counts like 
Westmoreland killing all those stupid soldiers and they weren’t the problem.  They 
problem was a handful of… It seemed to m e that the PhDs that were on our IR program 
they did a lot of little games of how to end the Vietnam War and they figured, they even 
knew the names of about 1200 people, if you eliminate them the war is over and not only 
is it over for tomorrow, but it’s probably over forever.  But instead of those 1200 people 
we’ll kill millions or hundreds of thousands of people because we’ve got it engrained in 
us you just can’t kill heads of states, I mean that’s really a nasty way to play the game. 
You can slaughter children and old ladies, blow up nurseries and what have you, but you 
can’t kill a head of state.

SM:  Yeah, but none of those things are really condoned either.  I mean there have 
been mishaps for those things happen… 
JM:  But why aren’t they condoned?  If they are the problem, if they are the ones 
that are waging war and they ultimately going to me responsible for killing hundreds of 
thousands of people why don’t you go after them? 
SM:  No I mean the killing of innocents, woman, children, old people. 
JM:  Oh but that’s…it’s inevitab le in war.  It’s going to happen.  Every war I 
think that’s ever been fought they get killed and it’s a shame that it happens that way. 
SM:  But that was minimized in the war in Laos? 
JM:  Yes it was although I’m sure that the…we did a lot of bom bing in Laos.  B-
52s dropped bombs in Laos and some of those bombs hit villages and killed innocent 
children and women you can be sure of that. 
SM:  While you were in-country did you hear specific stories about that? 
JM:  Oh I’ve heard a lot of stories about it, yeah.  I can’t give you any real 
specifics, but I know they were.  It’s inevitable you know.  Particularly in a B-52 in one 
of those strikes when they’re dropping tons of bombs you know and they stretch for a 
mile or so, anybody that’s in that range that happens to be there…if it’s a far mer and his 
rice paddy or a mother fixing dinner for her kids, she’s going to get hit.  
SM:  Well, what was… I guess what was the most positive thing you took away 
from your experience?   
JM:  Well, I…W ell I really have never given it any thought as what positive thing 
I’ve personally took away from it.  A real sense of accomplishment I think in that we did 
a very difficult job and I think we did it very well.  It’s hard to put something positive 
when the outcome was not what you wanted at all.  You know we left a lot of good 
people there just like we left in Vietnam.  Did you happen to read, I believe it was 
Schleisenger that was the…he held a cabinet  post under Nixon and then Ford inherited 
him and when Ford told him to get all the third country nationals, get every airplane 
available and get them out of Vietnam when he thought it was going to fall and he just 
turned his back on that and never did it, nothing happened to him.  Why not?  He 
should’ve under a prison somewhere as far as I’m concerned, he disobeyed a direct order 
from his Commander in Chief.  I’ve read that in a historical book so I assume it’s true.

He also disobeyed a direct order on the Mayaguez incident where the Cambodians 
attacked our ship and held [?] prisoner and of course we send in the troops, the Marines 
and they freed them and then old Ford ordered him to make retaliatory strikes to really 
teach them a lesson and he didn’t do it because he just didn’t think it was proper.  I can’t 
understand that.  I know that if an officer had given me, if a superior officer would’ve 
given me an order and I didn’t do it there’s no doubt of what would’ve happened to me, 
why isn’t that turkey where he belongs. 
SM:  Well, I guess when I asked you if there was anything positive in addition to 
the pride you felt I know the outcome wasn’t what you wanted… 
JM:  No I think that that they only thing that you weight positives then you can 
probably put on it is that we probably freed up some additional forces in Vietnam and 
maybe saved a few lives of the people over there and of course we did save the life of 
some military airmen that they picked up in Laos.  We probably all in all I would 
probably say from the poor people that we directly affected it was more negative then 
positive unfortunately.  Vang Pao’s living in the United States and there’s several 
thousand other Hmong living in the United States and for them I guess life is better, but 
for all the ones that were left behind it sure wasn’t or the ones that were left for 
generations in the refugee camps in Thailand. 
SM:  Speaking of people left behind, to your knowledge were any Americans left 
in Laos. 
JM:  I don’t…you know I thought that Ri tter and Townlee and Wisenbock may 
have been prisoners but as time wore on I’m convinced that they probably died with their 
airplane.  I think that there were prisoners in Laos a lot longer then we thought, but I 
can’t imagine them being alive after reading books like Ernie Brace’s A Code to Keep 
and the other Air American helicopter pilots and the guy that was with him on their 
24

Report to be Alive book.  I don’t know if you’ve read that or not.  They were on a H-34 
that was shot down and they were captured.  They conditions they lived under you just 
couldn’t…I don’t think an Am erican could live that long.  On my trips to Vietnam a 
friend of mine, Brian Johnson, you probably know Brian…I knew he’s not one of your 
friends I’m sure…but Brian went to Hanoi, rent ed a car and driver and drove all the way 
to Saigon a few years back and in Saigon he looked up his old…the  man and woman that

worked for him as their housekeeper and gardener and he found them.  They had spent a

sure as well weren’t happy with the way things went, but they said something that was 
very, very profound.  They said that if we had won the war and the North Vietnamese 
would’ve been defeated, there might have always been this thought that maybe 
communism really was something that was great and an opportunity that they missed, but 
now that the Americans had left and they had lived under communism there was 
absolutely no doubt in their mind about what a bad thing it was and it doesn’t work.  So 
from that standpoint I guess, although they’re still communist, nothing positive’s come 
out of it yet.   
SM:  But they are trying to open the economy, they are making… 
JM:  They’re very slowly…they’ve le t up and a lot and they’re economy is 
opening up and maybe someday they’ll be a democracy again, but boy it’s going to be a 
long time I’m afraid.  The old hard liners hang on real tight, they’re hanging on real tight 
in Laos too.   
SM:  Have you been back to Laos? 
JM:  I haven’t been back to Laos but I have several friends who have.  Of course I 
talked to the people in the MIA/POW thing about their problems with operating in Laos 
and they’re still hard liners that are hanging on to their old beliefs.  
SM:  Is there anything else you’d like to talk about? 
JM:  Oh, not really.  Not that I can think of. 
SM:  Okay.  Well let me put an official ending on this.  Thank you, this will end 
the interview with Mr. John McRainey.
